Note to Accredited Entities on the use of the funding proposal template  
• Accredited Entities should provide summary information in the proposal with cross -
reference to annexes such as feasibility studies, gender action plan, term sheet, etc.  
• Accredited Entities should ensure that annexes provided are consistent with the details 
provided in the funding proposal. Updates to the funding proposal and/or annex es must be 
reflected in all relevant documents.  
• The total number of pages for the funding proposal (excluding annexes) should not 
exceed 60.  Proposals exceeding the prescribed length will not be assessed within the  usual 
service standard time.  
• The rec ommended font is Arial, size 11.  
• Under  the GCF Information Disclosure Policy , project and programme funding proposals 
will be disclosed on the GCF website, simultaneous with the submission to the Board, 
subject to the redaction of any information that may not be disclosed pursuant to the IDP. 
Accredited Entities are asked to fill out  information on disclosure in section G.4.

A.20. Executing Entity 
information  The programme  will be executed by three institutions with clear roles according to their 
capacities and role in the region  
CABEI will be the accredited entity for the programme, but will also be directly executing the 
major part of component 2 – output 4  - of the programme (activity 4.1 and activity 4.2) as 
they will be directly responsible for the set up and management of the loan and guarantee 
facilities of the programme. FAO will be the Executing entity for the Technical Assistance 
facility under this component , (activity 4.3 ). CABEI will also be the executing entity for the 
grant facility under output 2 (activity 2.3)  
The Food and Agriculture Organization ( FAO) will play a major role in the execution of the 
capacity building, technical assistance and implementation of the Ecosystem based 
Adaptation demonstration sites  throughout the region  as the Executing Entity of outputs 1 
and 2 of the programme.   
The Central -American Commission for Environment and Development ( CCAD) will play a 
key role in political coordination among participant countries through the direct execution of 
output 3 focused of gathering and disseminating key results and analysis derived f rom the 
execution of the programme aiming at generating evidence to inform and mainstream 
adaptation into key national policies and regional strategies.   
 
 
A.21. Executive summary   
 
1. The Central American Dry Corridor and the Arid Zones of the Dominican Republic are among the world's most 
vulnerable tropical regions to the impacts of climate change , with Honduras, Nicaragua, Guatemala, the 
Dominican Republic and El Salvador recognised as being among the sixteen most vulnerable countries in the 
world to extreme climate events  for the period 1998- 2017 (Eckstein, Hutfils and Winges 2018) . The term "Dry 
Corridor " emerged in the last three decades to describe the increasing frequency and intensity of droughts in the 
region. These droughts are linked to El Niño events, which are occurring more frequently and intensely as a result 
of climate change and have had sever e impacts on agriculture and food security in the region. The impacts of 
droughts during El Niño are contrasted by an increase in the frequency and severity of extreme rainfall events 
linked to concurrent changes in La Niña. The resulting extreme rainfall leads to reductions in aquifer recharge, as 
well as increased surface runoff and soil erosion. These impacts are exacerbated by poor land management 
practices — which result in extensive environmental degradation — and widespread poverty.  
 
2. With more than one million families in the Dry Corridor and Arid Zones reliant  on agriculture for their livelihoods, 
the impacts of increasingly severe droughts over the past five years have resulted in the need for considerable 
humanitarian assistance. In 2014 alone, intense drought resulted in an estimated US$ 465 million worth of 
agricultural losses across the region, with more than 2.5 million people experiencing food insecurity. From 2015 
to 2018, food insecurity has increased annually in the region, wi th indigenous populations, women and rural 
communities the most likely to suffer from chronic hunger and malnutrition. By the last quarter of 2018, 2.2 million 
people had experienced crop losses that year, with 1.4 million urgently needing food assistance.  These impacts 
are expected to continue to worsen under future climate scenarios.  
 
3. Climate projections for the region indicate that, by the end of the century, temperatures will increase by 3– 3.5°C 
under RCP4.5 and by 6– 7°C under RCP8.5 (Lyra et al. 2017) . At the same time,  mean annual rainfall is projected 
to decrease by 11% on average. These changes  will be compounded by further changes in the El Niño- La Niña 
cycle, which will result in prolonged droughts  (Rauscher et al. 2008)  as well as more frequent and intense extreme 
rainfall events across the region. If average global temperatures reach 1.5°C above pre- industrial levels, extreme 
El Niño events are likely to happen twice as often — approximately every ten years as opposed t o every 20 years 
— with severe consequences for agriculture and food security. The impacts of future climate change will include 
further decreases in important crop harvests such as maise, beans and coffee, leading to increased poverty, food 
insecurity and migration from rural areas  (Hannah et al. 2017) . The land area suitable for growing these crops is 
also expected to decrease across the region, which will be exacerbated by soil degradation linked to increased  
rainfall intensity. Furthermore, as a result of climate change- related increases in temperature and drought 
frequency, the extent of the Dry Corridor and Arid Zones is predicted to expand. Currently, the Dry Corridor 
encompasses ~64% of the municipalities in Central America and is expected to extend to ~85% by 2050. These  
changes in temperat ure all also have an impact on the ranges of certain crop yields. For example, as  cooler 
highland areas warm up, they will become more suitable for specific crops  which may be pushed beyond thermal 
limits in l owland areas . However, the  desire to shift crops into these highland areas will likely catalyse forest


---
*Page 7*
---

 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.2.1 | PAGE 5 OF 96 
 A 
clearing in what are critical water recharge zones of river catchments , thereby reducing aquifer recharge.  This 
impact, coupled with an increase in extreme rainfall events, will increase the likelihood and severity of flooding. 
Climate change will also increase the frequency of wildfires, causing further forest degradation and threatening 
farming, forest -based  livelihoods and fuelwood supply.  
 
4. Without comprehensive adaptation interventions, agricultural losses will continue to increase, along with 
ecosystem loss, water shortages, poverty and food insecurity. These impacts  will ultimately increase the exposure 
and sensitivity of communities in the Dry Corridor and Arid Zones who are already extremely vulnerable to climate 
change. However, several barriers currently constrain the adoption of climate- adaptive practices by vulnerable 
communities. These barriers incl ude limited: i) access to credit for adaptation interventions among vulnerable 
populations;  ii) access to financial resources for the implementation of ecosystem -based adaptation (EbA) , water - 
and resource- efficient  technologies among the private sector; i ii) knowledge and understanding of climate change 
impacts as well as appropriate adaptation measures among decision- makers, communities and commercial 
farmers; iv) limited integration of EbA and other adaptation measures into sectoral policies; v) technica l capacity 
within governments and local communities to implement EbA and other adaptation measures; and vi) development 
of economic incentives for investment in sustainable natural resource management and EbA.  
 
5. In response to the above- described climate threats, GCF loan,  grant  and guarantee finance are requested to 
initiate a paradigm shift in the regional approach to catchment and water demand management in the Dry Corridor 
and Arid Zones. This shift will see governments of the s even participating countries —  Guatemala, Honduras, El 
Salvador, Nicaragua, Costa Rica, Panama and the Dominican Republic —  promoting large- scale EbA . At the 
same time,  smallholder and commercial farmers, as well  as entrepreneurs in rural communities, will have 
increased access to the financial resources and technical skills required to implement impactful adaptation 
interventions. A combination of GCF loan and grant finance, a guarantee,  as well as  a senior loan provided as co-
finance,  will be used to establish three financial facilities at the regional level, namely: i) a grant facility  to support 
bottom- up selection and promotion of local EbA activities through non- reimbursable financing (US$28 million); ii) 
an EbA lending facility for small - and large -scale EbA investments at farm- , enterprise-  and household- level 
(US$102.8 million) ; and iii) a guarantee facility that provides an additional layer of security to financial institutions 
in order to promote EbA finance (US$30 million) .  
The facilities  will be managed by CABEI  with a blended financing structure, where the relevant funds in the form 
of senior loans for the EbA lending facility are channelled via two separate streams towards end beneficiaries  and 
are blended at the level of IFIs: (1) one stream for CABEI’s co -financing funds , passing from CABEI’s balance 
sheet as senior loans to the IFIs’ balance sheet within an existing Global Credit Line (GCL),  and (2) a second 
stream of GCF funds   provided by CABEI  (as trustee of the GCF funds) via limited recourse loans to the IFIs. The 
co-financng ratio for the EbA lending facility among the CABEI Co- financing and the GCF funding provided to 
each selected IFI shall be of 40:60,  respectively. . Both streams are henc e being blended at IFI level, and from 
there channeled by the IFIs to the PFIs that directly on- lend to end beneficiaries .   
A guar antee facility will be implemented by CABEI  with the aim to increase  IFIs appetite to take on further financing 
to channel to PFIs by guaranteeing the PFI EbA finance performance.  The grant facility will be chann elling grant 
funds to on- the-ground stakeholder s (rural communities, provider s of EbA solutions, among others),  to be 
distributed via two competitive bottom- up identification rounds for eligible projects per year, for four years (each 
round providing USD 3.5 million to an expected number of 70 project s per round) . The grant facilit y aims at 
strengthening the identif ication, incubation and even acceleration of promising EbA solution in the localities, to 
prepare EbA solution markets for commercial and mainstreamed EbA investment and finance favouring the 
development of a business ecosystem around EbA . These mechanisms  will make US$ 130.8 million available for 
investment in EbA and water - and resource- efficient technologies in the region  (US$102.8 million from loans and 
US$28 million from grants) . The total grant request of US$84 .3 million will be used in the following ways ; i) 
US$10. 1 million will be used to strengthen the technical capacity of local government , farmers and rural 
communities to implement EbA and other adaptation measures ;  ii) US$ 28. 5 million will be used to implement 
large- scale adaptation interventions — including EbA, water - and resource- efficient technologies — in rural 
communities across the region; i ii) US$28 million will form the grant facility through which vulnerable communities 
can access on- grants channelled through financial institutions ; iv) US$6.3 million will be used to generate and 
share information on climate change adaptation and finance across the region, and mainstream it into local and 
national policies ; v) US$ 6.5 million will be used to set up the financial mec hanisms and strengthen the technical 
capacity of CABEI accredited and non- regulated financial institutions to access and channel funds through them;  
vi) US$4.2 million will be used for overall management and monitoring of programme activities  (Independent 
evaluations and PMC costs ) ; and vii) US$ 0.7M will be requested as contingency . Additionally,  national

governments have committed US$51.3 million in co -financ e which will further scale and mainstream EbA, water 
and resource- efficiency in their  respective countries.  
 
6. Together, these interventions will catalyse the autonomous upscaling of EbA and water - and resource- efficient  
technologies by combining technical capacity -building, policy instruments and accessible financial mechanisms. 
This approa ch will transform the financial landscape in the region to enable vulnerable groups to access climate 
finance and implement climate- resilient measures to strengthen their adaptive capacity. The proposed programme 
will directly benefit ~ 1,084,441 people across the target catchments  through on -the-ground interventions, with  
~134,694 of these people  also able to access loans for the implementation of adaptation measures  through 
financial mechanisms  established under the programme (lending and guarant ee facilities) . Furthermore, the 
combined impact of programme  interventions will facilitate the uptake of adaptation measures beyond the target 
municipalities, ultimately indirectly benefitting ~1,364,044 people across the region during the pro gramme lifetime, 
and thousands more into the future.  
 
7. The programme will be executed by three institutions with clear roles according to their capacities and role in the 
region. CABEI will be the accredited entity for the programme, but will also be directly executing the major part of 
output  2 and  4 of the programme as they will be directly responsible for the set up and management of the three 
financial facilities of the pro gramme CABEI will therefore be the executing entity for activities 2.3 (grant facility), 
4.1 and 4.2 (lending and guarantee facilities) . FAO will be the Executing entity for the Technical Assistance facility 
under output 4 (activity 4.3), supported by UNEP as technical advisor.  The responsibility of output 4 is thus  shared 
between CABEI and FAO.  FAO will also play a major role in the execution of the c apacity building, technical 
assistance and implementation of the Ecosystem based Adaptation demonstration sites throughout the region as the Executing Entity of outputs 1  and 2 of the programme. CCAD will play a key role in political coordination among 
par
ticipant countries through the direct execution of output 3 focused of gathering and disseminating key results 
and analysis derived from the execution of the pro gramme aiming at generating evidence to inform and 
mainstream adaptation into key national poli cies and regional strategies. UNEP will provide technical advice to 
CCAD for the development of technical knowledge products on financial mechanisms for EbA, methodologies for ecosystem services valuation and technical guidelines for EbA implementation. UN EP will play a key role also as 
technical advisor mainly on the financial component and the regional hub component contributing with all the 
technical experience already developed in the region on the design and deployment of financial mechanisms for 
EbA a nd on EbA mainstreaming into national policies.

  PROJECT/PROGRAMME INFORMATION  
B.1. Climate con text 
Context  
8. The Dry Corridor of Central America consists  of semi -arid and sub- humid ecosystems that cover most of the 
Pacific slope of Guatemala, Honduras, El Salvador and Nicaragua, as well as smaller areas of Costa Rica and 
Panama (Figure 1). Similar climatic conditions are found in the arid and semi -arid areas (hereafter ' Arid Zones' ) 
in the west of the Dominican Republic (Figure 1). The Dry Corridor is the most densely populated region of 
Central America1, and the Arid Zones are home to a significant proportion of the Dominican Republic's 
population — 12% of the total2, amounting to ~1.2 million people. In a ddition, Guatemala, Honduras, El 
Salvador and Nicaragua have comparatively small Gross Domestic Product (GDP) per capita, while Costa Rica, Panama and the Dominican Republic are comparatively wealthy and have relatively low poverty levels
3. 
However, all these countries have high income inequality, with instances of  extreme poverty — particularly in 
rural areas.  
 
 
Figure 1. Current (red) and projected extent in 2050 (red and dark grey) of the Central American Dry Corridor and the Dominican 
Republic's Arid Zones4. The m ap is based on calculations by the International Centre for Tropical Agriculture (CIAT), using the 
current cl imate distribution5, future scenarios6 and evapotranspiration estimations7,8. 
 
9. Across the region, poverty in rural areas is significantly higher than in urban areas. Nearly 60% of people in the 
Dry Corridor live in poverty9, while the Arid Zones of the Dominican Republic have the highest poverty rate in 
the country10. A further example  is that  in Costa Rica double the number of people in rural areas are living in 
extreme poverty compared with in urban areas11. Similarly, in Panama, prevalence of extreme po verty is 27%

 in rural areas compared with only 4% in urban areas12. Most rural populations in the Dry Corridor and Arid 
Zones depend on agriculture for their livelihoods, practising both subsistence and commercial farming,  and 
farming  both crops and livestock. In addition to agriculture, plantation forestry supports the livelihoods of some 
local communities.  
 
Climate rationale  
10. The Dry Corridor and Arid Zones are among the world's most vulnerable tropical regions to the impacts of 
climate change13, with Honduras, Nicaragua, Guatemala, the Dominican Republic and El Salvador recognised 
as being among the fifteen most vulnerable countries in the world to extreme climate events14. The term " Dry 
Corridor " emerged in the last three decades to describe the increa sing frequency and intensity of droughts in 
the region. These droughts are linked to El Niño15 events, which are occurring more frequently and intensely 
as a result of climate change16, and have had severe impacts on agriculture and food security in the regi on. 
Since agriculture and forestry are greatly affected by climatic conditions, the livelihoods of rural people in the 
Dry Corridor and Arid Zones are extremely sensitive to the impacts of climate change. For example, during the 
2015 drought, over three mi llion people experienced acute food insecurity17. These impacts are exacerbated 
by poor land management practices — which result in extensive environmental degradation — and widespread 
poverty. Furthermore, these communities have limited access to financial  resources for the implementation of 
adaptation interventions. Without urgent adaptation measures, the vulnerability of the region's population to 
climate change impacts will increase. Further details of climate change risks and impacts for the Dry Corridor and Arid Zones are described below.  
 
Climate change risks  
11. Past climate trends and future projections show that the region's  climate is changing in several ways, including: 
i) temperatures increasing across the region; and ii) shifting rainfall patterns.  In most parts of the Dry Corridor 
and Arid Zones, rain seasons are shortening and the intensity of midsummer droughts18 is increasing19. 
Droughts that extend over a year or more are also becoming increasingly frequent and severe, mainly because 
of the increasing frequency and intensity of El Niño events. Concurrently, extreme rainfall events are increasing 
in frequency and severity because of changes in La Niña. Country -specific observed and predicted changes in 
temperature and rainfall are presented in Sect ion 3 of Annex 2: Feasibility Study.  
 
12. Overall, temperatures across Central America are anticipated to continue to increase in future. By the end of 
the century, temperatures are projected to increase by 3– 3.5°C under a medium emissions scenario (RCP4.5) 
and by as much as 6 –7°C under a high emissions scenario (RCP8.5)20 relative to the baseline of 1961– 1990. 
While the trend of increasing temperatures is consistent across Central America and the Caribbean, the 
magnitude of change is expected to vary  among countries and between geographical areas within countries 
(Figure 2). Mean annual rainfall is projected to decrease across much of Central America by 2070 for both 
medium (RCP4.5) and high (RCP8.5) emissions scenarios (Figure 3 ). In addition to the predicted future trends 
in mean annual temperature and rainfall, extreme temperatures are predicted to increase in the coming 
decades (Figure 4).  From 2021 –2050, daytime high temperatures (TXx)21 and night -time high temperatures 
(TNx)22 are projected to incr ease everywhere in the region. Rates of warming are expected to be strongest over 
the northern portion of the Dry Corridor, particularly over the eastern mountain slopes in Guatemala, Honduras,

32 Dry spells  affecting vegetation growth can be assessed through the relationship between actual (ET) and potential (ETP) evapotranspirati on, which 
combines the effects of precipitation, temperature, soil moisture, an d vegetation cover, among other atmospheric conditions. Dry spells are defined as 
the annual largest number of consecutive days when ET <= 0.5*ETP.

34 Annual mean water excess, defined as P -ETP, is a proxy of water availability from runoff. When P -ETP is positive, there is a surplus of water 
availability, while when P -ET is negative, there is a deficit of water availability.

 and Nicaragua. Similarly, temperatures of cold days23 are predi cted to increase across the region. The rates of 
these increases are projected to be strongest over the Pacific coasts of Nicaragua, El Salvador, Honduras and 
Guatemala24. In addition — and although rainfall  in the Dry Corridor and Arid Zones is expected to decrease 
by 11% on average by the end of the century — extreme rainfall is predicted to increase significantly across 
parts of the region by mid-century . The number of intense rainfall events25 and extremel y intense rainfall 
events26 occurring each year is predicted to increase (Figure 5 ). The R50mm index is used to represent 
precipitation exceeding 50 mm/day which represents heavy rains, and projections show an increase in R50mm 
in the Atlantic coast of Cost a Rica and northern parts of Panama27. A similar trend is observed in projections 
for the 90th percentile of precipitation index (R90p) , which represents extreme precipitation, with rains heavier 
than the R50mm, that are less frequent. The R90p indicator ha s been projected to have a strong increase in 
the Atlantic coast of Panama, Costa Rica, and Nicaragua. The Pacific coast of Panama and Costa Rica also 
shows some increase, but at a smaller rate. In the Pacific coast of El Salvador, Honduras, and Guatemala the 
R90p projections show a decreasing trend, which is attributed to the decrease of winds or hurricane activity28.    
 
13. Recent evidence29 and model simulations predict that the risk of drought will increase with climate change, as 
more intense and prolonged El Niño events occur across the region30. Indeed, if average global temperatures  
reach 1.5°C above pre- industrial levels, extreme El Niño events are likely to happen twice as often — 
approximately every 10 years as opposed to every 20 years.  Both droughts and dry spells will increase in the 
Dry Corridor and Arid Zones as a result of c limate change. Droughts are years or seasons when rainfall is below 
average, while dry spells are defined as large number of consecutive days when minimal rain falls. From 2021 –
2050, it is projected that parts of the Dry Corridor and Arid Zone will experie nce an increase in dry spells31,32. 
While the number of dry spells is projected to decrease in parts of Panama, Costa Rica, Nicaragua and 
Honduras, it is expected to increase over the Pacific basin in Nicaragua and El Salvador, as well as in 
Guatemala (Figur e 6)33. In addition, annual mean water excess34 is projected to decrease across most parts of 
the Dry Corridor (Figure 6). Projections for drought events in the region show an increase in drought length, 
intensity and frequency under both a medium emissions scenario (RCP4.5) high emissions scenario (RCP8.5) , 
for both short -term and long- term droughts35. These increases are projected to be moderate in early decades 
of the century (2011– 2040), then become more pronounced at the mid- century (2041– 2070) before bec oming 
extreme at the end of the century (2071– 2100) . During dry spells and droughts, there is a pronounced risk of 
the spread of aridity in parts of the Dry Corridor and Arid Zone as the availability of water is reduced, which 
could have lasting impacts on the local environment36. On an annual scale, the region currently experiences a 
rainy season with two peaks separated by a dry period of weeks to over a month in duration, termed the mid-
summer drought (MSD).  Projections for MSD trends under a high emiss ions scenario (RCP8.5)  show an 
increase in duration for the MSD period, which is projected to increase by an average of over a week . 
Additionally, t he MSD minimum precipitation is projected to decrease by an average of over 26%, with

Climate change impacts  
14. Climate change is already having severe impacts on communities in the Dry Corridor and Arid Zones. Indeed, 
its impact on agriculture is commonly cited as a major driver of migration out of the region to North America, 
especially from Honduras, Guatemala and El Salvador47. In 2014, for example, severe drought across the 
region resulted in an estimated US$ 465 million worth of agricultural losses, including basic grains, coffee and 
livestock. In Honduras, Guatemal a and El Salvador alone, a total ~2.5 million people experienced food 
insecurity as a result of approximately 75% loss of maise and bean crops48. In 2016, 50– 90% of crop loss 
resulted in 3.5 million people requiring humanitarian assistance across the region49. This trend is continuing, 
with subsistence farmers in the Dry Corridor of Guatemala, El Salvador, Honduras and Nicaragua having lost 
more than half of their maise and bean crops in 2018, with 2.2 million people affected50. These recent droughts 
have been unprecedented in their severity and the magnitude of their impacts .  Such intense droughts are 
expected to increase in frequency under future climate conditions  and, in the absence of adaptation 
interventions, people living in the Dry Corridor and A rid Zones will continue to experience adverse impacts of 
climate change51.  
 
15. Future predicted impacts of climate change include decreased harvests of maise , beans, coffee and other 
important crops, leading to increased poverty, food insecurity and migration from rural areas52. Redu ctions in 
coffee yields have been predicted to be between 7% in 2020 and 39 % in 2100 across all Central American 
countries  under climate change conditions53, while a similar study predicted reductions in maise yields of 
between 4% in 2020 and 1 8% in 210054. These reductions will result from  direct climate impacts (e.g. droughts 
and floods) as well as indirect impacts on crops (e.g. declines in crop pollinators)55. Clim ate change will also 
impact the land area suitable for growing specific crops, with declining areas expected for coffee and beans 
across the region56. For example, El Salvador and Costa Rica are expected to experience decreases in land 
suitability for growing  coffee  of 40% or more57. Similarly, modelling of maise  indicates that land suitability is

58 Hannah et al., 2017. Regional modeling of climate change impacts on smallholder agricu lture and ecosystems in Central America. Climatic Change

61 Details are provided in Section 5  of Annex 2 : Feasibility Study.  likely to decrease by up to 34% in El Salvador, Guatemala, Honduras and Nicaragua58. Although some cooler 
highland areas that are currently unsuitable for thes e crops may become more suitable as temperature 
increases , desire to shift crops into these areas will likely catalyse forest clearing in critical water recharge 
zones of river catchments thereby reducing aquifer recharge59. This impact , coupled with an inc rease in 
extreme rainfall events , will increase the likelihood and severity of flooding. Climate change will likely also 
increase the frequency of wildfires, causing further forest degradation and threatening farming, forest -based 
livelihoods and fuelwood supply60.  
 
16. The climate change impacts described above are exacerbated by the baseline problems of forest loss and land 
degradation, which result in decreased water supply and food securit y61. Without comprehensive adaptation 
interventions, agricultural losses will continue to increase, along with ecosystem loss, water shortages, poverty 
and food insecurity. This will ultimately increase the exposure and sensitivity of communities in the Dry Corridor 
and Arid Zones who are already extremely v ulnerable to climate change.  
 
17. The programme will target seven catchments for interventions — one in each of the participating countries. In 
these  catchment s, the application of the Soil and Water Assessment Tool ( SWAT ) allows for the analysis of 
potential impacts of climate change in the programme’s target areas .The availability of historical observed 
meteorological  data and site-specific climate change projections are very limite d. As observed in the following 
figure, t here are only 24 meteorological stations within the limits of the Dry Corridor . Only for three of the 
countries , there were stations within the prioritized pilot sites and just one of them (Honduras)  had complete 
observed rainfall and temperature data for a period of over 30 years  with less than 15% of discontinuity .

18. Input data: Data from Choluteca- baja are measurement stations that were verified locally and statistically. The 
regional data set for the rainfall and temperature that was used in the Dominican Republic and Costa Rica, was 
complemented and used as input was the CFSR obtained by the SWAT mode lling online data system. The 
Global Weather Data (SWAT, https://globalweather.tamu.edu ), is a product from the National Center for 
Environmental Prediction (NCEP, https://climatedataguide.ucar.edu/climate- data/climate- forecast -system -
reanalysis -cfsr ) Cl imate Forecast System Reanalysis (CFSR). Th ese have been continuously run over the 36 -


---
*Page 16*
---

G 
 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.2.1 | PAGE 14 OF 96 
   
 year period of 1979 through 2014, and continuously updated in real time. The CFSR was designed and 
executed as a global, high resolution, coupled atmosphere- ocean- land surface- sea ice system to provide the 
best estimate of the state of these coupled domains. These results have been used in several publications and 
evaluated with good validation results in different climates . 
19. The results from the analysis undertaken in three basins in the Dry Corridor indicate that t he foreseen impact 
will be severe in the programme areas , with a total predicted decrease in average rainfall of 11%, which is 
equivalent to a decrease of 655 mm/year  under RCP 4.5 for 2050 and reaching up to 15% under RCP 8.5 for 
2050 . This decrease will also likely result in  a significant delay in  the timing of  peak annual rainfall , with a shift 
from the month of May to the month of June, with important implications for agricultural activities. Additionally, 
climate change projections also predict  substantial  changes in the main hydrological variables  in the 
programme areas,  with a significant de crease between 4.1% and 5.7% in soil humidity  and 18% to 25%  in 
percolation for the Choluteca basin.  These variables are critical for ecosystem maintenance and also  for the 
sustainability of the agricultural activity and soil stability.  Results from the hydrological model are included in 
the Feasibility Study in Annex 2 and Annex 31 of this proposal.  Figure 7b below summarizes the changes in 
main hydrological variabl es under RCP 4.5 and RCP 8.5 for 2050 for the main hydrological variables  in two 
selected basins in the Dry Corridor.  These analysis is also further specified and discussed in the draft EbA site-
specific plans developed for the pro gramme sites in Honduras and Costa Rica (Annex 31).

20. In addition to the 2 detailed hydrological modelings produced, Figure 7c provides a summary of the climate 
projections for the other 5  countries  based on the Coupled Model Intercomparison project Phase 5 (CMIP5 
from the Global Climate Models -  GCM) and the Regional Climate Models  from the Coordinated Regional 
Downscaling experiment ( CORDEX )62. The projected  changes in soil humidity, aridity and water runoff  are 
consistent with the trends identified by the hydrological modelling: the annual precipitation is projected  to 
decrease significantly , from a range of -14% to - 21% for RCP 8.5 in 2071- 2100 in four of the five countries. A 
similar trend is observed for soil moisture and water runoff’s variation, that are generally projected to decrease 
throughout all four  scenarios, while the annual mean erosion is generally  increas ing. Panamá’s modelling do es 
not seem to follow these general  trends, but this is believed to be  linked to the limited consistence among 
models (no projections are provided on water runoff and soil moisture) which  could explain this inconsistency 
with the proj ections in the other countries.  
21. Further  results of the above hydrological modelling show a general positive impact of the interventions proposed 
for the programme . The modelling has allowed for the comparison between different alternatives of 
interventions , and demonstrates how  increasing benefits are realised w hen the adaptation solutions are 
introduced in combination rather than in isolat ion.  
22. The study shows that adaptation measures have the potential to generate hydrological benefits at the level of 
intervened use and at the basin scale; Increased  water available for plants  through improved  soil moisture, 
contribute in water recharge and reduce d water erosion, are some main benefits; positive changes depend on 
the type and magnitude of intervention. Implementation of adaptation measures holisticall y, meaning 
simultaneous implementation of a group of these, shows the best results in hydrological processes and avoided 
erosion. Accorindg to the results of this  study, the best results occurred under the implementation of combined 
adaptation measures at the medium and high levels .   
23. Figure 8 summmari ses the changes in the main hydrological variables after the implementation of EbA 
interventions  in comparison to the change expected under climate change (in red). The introduced EbA options 
include:  forest c onservation  (at the top) ,  reforestation, agroforestry systems, irrigation systems , instalment of 
vegetative barriers  and the combination of all these EbA options (at the bottom) . As stated above, t he 
aggregated analysis of all hydrological variables concludes that the combination of EbA options show the best 
resuls.  This combination of interventions results in substantial  improvements for all key hydrological variables 
in the catchment areas. Soil humidity increases from large losses under the climate change base scenario to 
positive values under  EbA combinations; this results in benefits for water availability both for agriculture and 
forests that draw water from the top soil layers. The changes in water production (surf ace water) and percolation 
(water recharge) show even greater benefits from the implementation of this combination of EbA measures. 
These values are key for ensuring water availability throughout  the year from a scenario of significant water

24. The above results demonstrate the benefits and positive impacts that the proposed programme interventions 
will have in the target areas, substantially reducing the expected negative impacts of climate change. . In 
addition to the results from hydrological modelling undertaken for the proposed programme, other studies have 
demonstrated links betw een the proposed interventions — EbA, water - and resource -efficient  technologies — 
and improved climate resilience among vulnerable communities in the region.  
25. Economic analysis of the benefits of land restoration in Latin American provides evidence to sup port that the 
adoption of policy measures and removal of barriers to land restoration efforts by national and local 
governments result in substantial benefits, including gains in agricultural production, alleviation of food 
insecurity and carbon sequestrat ion63. The economic benefits will contribute to the climate resilience of 
communities in combination with the direct benefits of EbA and water - and resource -efficient  technologies on 
land and ecosystems. The use of soil conservation methods and EbA interventions, such as vegetative barriers, 
is recognised in the region as increasing climate adaptation by increasing soil water infiltration, retaining soil 
moisture and reducing soil erosion64. Land restoration also decreases the demand for agricultural expansio n 
by enabling improvements in production on degraded lands through agroforestry, silvopastoral systems65, and 
other types of EbA and water - and resource- efficient  technologies. The proposed la ndscape- scale adaptation 
interventions implemented in target catc hments will restore degraded lands and prevent subsequent forest 
clearing in critical recharge zones and other ecosystems. This is expected to protect communities from risks of 
flooding and will decrease the need for migration from rural areas.  
26. The propose d EbA interventions and water - and resource- efficient technologies will aim to reduce the 
vulnerability of targeted beneficiaries by increasing resilience to drought and extreme rainfall impacts on 
agricultural production through improved hydrological flow  and restoration of catchments.  During the initial 
stage of implementation, sound stakeholder consultations will be undertaken in each of the pilot  sites to build 
on the information developed during the proposal design and the EbA interventions will be val idated from a 
wide range of identified EbA interventions that are adjusted to the specific climate risks and socio -environmental Level SW PERC WYLD EROSION

66 A comprehensive  list of past and ongoing projects, programmes and initiatives, as well as details of their focus and alignment with the  proposed

68 Ecosystem -based adaptation refers to “the use of biodiversity and ecosystem services to help people adapt to the adverse effects of cli mate change” 
(Convention on Biological Diversity, 2009)  context of the catchment. Further specific criteria that will be applied for the selection and validation of these 
EbA interven tions are described in paragraph 50.  Improved ecosystem health through EbA interventions — 
such as reforestation, vegetative barriers and agroforestry systems — will create enhanced groundwater 
resources for use during droughts and decrease the risk of flo oding and erosion during extreme rainfall events. 
Reducing demand for scarce water resources through water -efficient technologies in farms and households will 
further reduce the stress on ecosystems’ hydrological cycles and will improve ecosystems’ resilience to climate 
change impacts. Further to this, the economic benefits and increased agricultural productivity gained from land 
restoration in the region will help improve livelihood resilience and food security in communities.  
 
Complementarity with related projects, programmes and initiatives  
27. Considerable investments have been and are being made through public expenditure and donor -funded 
initiatives to increase the resilience of vulnerable communities to climate change throughou t the Dry Corridor 
and Arid Zones. Lessons learned and best practices from these investments have been incorporated into the 
design of the proposed programme  to replicate successful techniques and ensure that there is complementarity 
between the programme and existing initiatives. The most relevant investments that will be complemented by 
the proposed programme are presented below66. 
 
28. CABEI is implementing the GCF -funded ‘Productive Investment Initiative for Adaptation to Climate Change’ 
(CAMBio II) pro gramme  in the same seven countries the proposed programme will work in but in areas outside 
of the Dry Corridor and Arid Zones. Specifically, CAMBio II is working to: i) provide loans to producers through 
CABEI’s accredited intermediary financial institutions ( IFIs) for the implementation of adaptation measures; ii) 
build capacity of micro, small and medium- sized enterprises (MSMEs) and IFIs through grants to improve 
technical and business skills; and iii) establish an incentive scheme to promote adaptation meas ures 
implemented by MSMEs.  
29. The proposed programme will be complementary to CAMBIo II in several regards. First, the two operate in 
complementary geographic regions. While CAMBio II works in an expanded range across the region — focusing 
on areas where there is demand from MSMEs — the proposed programme will only work within the Dry 
Corridor.  A detailed vulnerability assessment of the region at the municipal level has been undertaken, resulting 
in the select ion of the 36 most vulnerable municipalities67 in which this programme will be focusing.  Second, 
the two will target different groups. CAMBio II is explicitly focused on MSMEs, while the proposed programme  
will work with individual producers and their associated groups, focusing specifi cally on the most vulnerable 
smallholder farmers . Third, the two will operate through different financial mechanisms. CAMBio II is working 
through financial intermediation with IFIs, channelling funds through credit .  
In contrast, the proposed programme will operate through a trust structure for GCF funds , andCABEI , as direct 
trustee for GCF , will then provide senior limited recourse loans to the IFIs , combined with an on- lending 
structure via senior loans for CABEI co- financing, both to existing Intermediary Financial Institutions (IFIs) that 
will blend the funds on their balance sheet to channel to Partner Financial Institutions (PFIs) in the programme 
areas . PFIs in that context are smaller institutions such as small banks, cooper atives, NGOs, or rural community 
banks, that serve the last mile for the end beneficiaries . Fourth, the proposed programme will expand on the 
range of activities offered — focusing on water security as a whole, rather than only agriculture. Fifth, by 
providing a guarantee mechanism to be funded by GCF  to exclusively cover non- GCF /non- CABEI co-financing 
and leveraged funds , the programme will enable the scale- up and mainstreaming of successful EbA finance by 
further leveraging additional private and possibly public funds  for the on- lending structure, giving the GCF fund‘s 
trust structure the catalytic role it is intended for . This expanded focus will include on- the-ground ecosystem -
based adaptation (EbA)68 and restoration.  
 
30. In addition to the above, the technical assistance provided by the two programmes will be compl ementary. 
CAMBio II is providing a variety of technical assistance to IFIs and MSMEs, where the beneficiaries receive 
initial training on the programme  operations and thereafter  can request assistance on specific matters. The 
proposed pro gramme otherwise offers technical assistance  — both practical and theoretical  trainings  — for 
local government, farmers and rural communities,  as well as  IFI's and smaller  financial institutions , that focus 
on: i) the technical areas in climate change ; ii) accessing and channeling funds through established financial


---
*Page 20*
---

G 
 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.2.1 | PAGE 18 OF 96 
   
  
69 In the Dry Corridor, Costa Rica, El Salvador, Guatemala, Honduras, Nicaragua and Panama have committed to restoring 6.4 milli on hectares in total, 
while the Dominican Republic has committed to restoring 0. 09 million hectares . El Salvador, Honduras, Nicaragua and Panama: 1 million hectares per 
country; Guatemala: 1.2 million hectares ; Costa Rica: 2.7 million hectares . Available at: http://www.wri.org/our -work/project/initiative- 20x20.  
70 www.solucionesabe.org   mechanisms ; and iii) implementing EbA and other adaptation measures . Furthermore, the proposed 
programme will expand on the outputs of CAMBio II, drawing on experiences during implementation to inform 
higher -level outcomes. For example, CAMBio II offers incentives (Adapt -Award) for IFIs and MSMEs which use 
loans to implement adaptation measures. However, these incentives will not scale well beyond programme 
implementation. The proposed programme will improve on this approach by promoting the mainstreaming of 
adaptation incentives into local and, ultimately, national policy through capacity building activities for decisio n-
makers and mainstreaming policy initiatives.  Overall complementarity and sharing of best practices will be 
ensured as both will be executed by CABEI , with  lessons learned being  directly  exchanged between 
management units within CABEI.  However , it is impo rtant to highlight that, although complementary, thes e 
initiatives  have a fundamental difference in their overall goals . In the case of the Dry Corridor , the approach is 
to ensure water security for communities at the landscape/basin level, w hile for CAMB Io II, the goal is to remove 
funding barrie rs for MSMEs.   
 
31. In addition to CAMBio II, the proposed programme  will align with the Microfinance for EbA (MEbA) project that 
is being implemented in eight countries across Latin America and Africa, including three of the targeted Central 
American countries, namely Costa Rica, Dominican Republic and El Salvador. MEbA i s promoting private 
sector engagement in adaptation finance by facilitating microfinance products aimed at small -scale farmers to 
invest in EbA options to improve their resilience to climate change. The proposed programme will draw on 
lessons learned and b est practices established through MEbA to guide the establishment of financial 
mechanisms for the Dry Corridor and Arid Zones.  
 
32. Several national projects are being implemented in Central America with a focus on water -efficient 
technologies, EbA and land restoration to improve ecosystem services  and livelihoods , increase food security, 
and build climate resilience of vulnerable communities. Examples of these include' Upscaling climate resilience 
measures in the Dry Corridor agro- ecosystems of El Salvador (RE CLIMA) ' and 'Building livelihood resilience to 
climate change in the upper basins of Guatemala' s highlands ', both funded by GCF. These projects are 
implementing several adaptation measures that will be replicated and scaled up regionally by the proposed 
programme. These include: i) riparian forest restoration; ii) establishing agroforestry and silvopastoral systems; 
iii) rainwater collection; and iv) water -efficient agricultural technologies such as drip irrigation.  The proposed 
programme will use best prac tices from these projects to scale up interventions across the region. In addition, 
increased access to financial products and services under Component 2  of the proposed programme will ensure 
that a larger number of individuals and enterprises are able to implement these kinds of interventions. The 
proposed programme will also contribute directly to restoration commitments made by several countries under 
Initiative 20x20. This init iative is a country -led effort69 whose primary objective is to restore 20 million hectares  
of degraded land across Central America and the Caribbean by 2020. The proposed programme will contribute 
directly to these goals by restoring ~19, 200 hectares  of for ested area across the seven catchments targeted by 
the programme.  
 
33. Finally, a project funded by the German Federal Ministry for the Environment, Nature Conservation and Nuclear 
Safety (BMU) has been  implemented from 2012 –2019 by the International Union for  Conservation of Nature 
(IUCN) in several countries in Central America, including Costa Rica, El Salvador, Guatemala, Honduras, 
Mexico and Panama. This project — 'Transforming evidence into change: a holistic approach to governance 
for EbA' (Go4EbA) — focused on improving existing governance frameworks for implementing EbA through 
developing an evidence base for its implementation. One catchment per country was selected to demonstrate 
EbA measures and ~400 people received capacity -building. Go4EbA also esta blished a database including 
information about over 100 EbA -related cases in the region, and a virtual platform known as ' EbA Solutions'70 
to share knowledge and provide technical assistance.  The platform includes information on: i) adaptation 
projects by type of activity; ii ) availability and management of water resources; and iii) ecosystem protection 
and recovery . The proposed programme will draw lessons learned from Go4EbA with regards to b est practices 
when implementing a bottom -up approach focused on promoting water and food security  in the region . It will 
also upscale measures implemented under Go4EbA  in several additional catchments within the Dry Corridor 
and Arid Zones.  
B.2. Theory of change (max.  1000 words, approximately  2 pages  plus diagram )


---
*Page 21*
---

G 
 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.2.1 | PAGE 19 OF 96 
   
  
71 Details  are provided in Section B.1 and Section 6  of Annex 2: Feasibility Study.  Description of the problem  
34. Climate change is causing  increasingly frequent and intense droughts, as well as a higher occurrence of 
extreme rainfall events, in the Dry Corridor region of Central America and the Arid Zones of the Dominican 
Republic. These areas are densely populated and have high levels of poverty — concentrated in rural areas — 
when compared with the rest of the region. In addition, most rural communities in the Dry Corridor and Arid 
Zones are dependent on agriculture and plantation forestry for their livelihoods, both of which are extremely 
sensitive to climate impacts — specifically those impacts that result in changes in water availability. These 
communities al so have limited knowledge of climate change adaptation measures, coupled with an inability to 
access funds to finance adaptation. As a result, rural communities in this region are extremely vulnerable to the 
impacts of climate change. This vulnerability is  exacerbated by widespread environmental degradation and an 
associated loss of ecosystem services such as water production. Future climate projections indicate that the 
Dry Corridor and Arid Zones will continue to become warmer and drier, with increasingly  variable rainfall and 
an associated increased risk of severe drought71. Past and ongoing adaptation efforts in the region have not 
been implemented at a  sufficient scale to prevent rural communities experiencing severe negative impacts of 
climate change, w hich will likely only increase in the future.  
 
Preferred adaptation solution  
35. The preferred adaptation solution to the above- described problem is to improve the climate resilience of rural 
communities in the Dry Corridor and Arid Zones by: i) implementing integrated catchment management and 
restoring catchments; ii) improving hydrol ogical flow and infiltration of rainwater into groundwater reserves 
through forest and ecosystem restoration; and iii) reducing demand for scarce water resources by implementing water -efficient technologies at farm-  and household- level.  The selection/valid
ation of the specific EbA 
interventions will be based on scientific climate risk assessments and sound stakeholder consultations as 
described in paragraphs 51  and 99 . Specifically, under the preferred adaptation solution, commercial farmers 
and entrepreneu rs in rural communities would have access to the financial resources and technical skills 
required for the widespread implementation of adaptation measures — including EbA, and water - and resource-
efficient  techniques. This access to financial and technica l resources would strengthen the adaptive capacity 
of rural communities to withstand increasingly frequent and intense droughts , which result in water shortages, 
and subsequent reductions in economic activity , productivity  and food security. In addition, t he mainstreaming 
of adaptation considerations into policy and planning by government, and the generation of knowledge and 
awareness of adaptation options should take place across the region. Mainstreaming in this way would enable the uptake of large- scale 
EbA and other adaptation measures. The overall approach under the preferred 
adaptation solution would  ensure access to adaptation measures for all impacted communities in the Dry 
Corridor and Arid Zones, building their climate resilience in the long- term.  
 Barriers to the preferred adaptation solution  
36. The proposed programme will address the following eight barriers to the preferred adaptation solution in the 
Dry Corridor and Arid Zones. These barriers are described in further detail in Section 11 of Annex 2: Feasibility 
Study.  
 
• Barrier 1: Limited knowledge and understanding of climate change impacts among decision- makers.  Decision-
makers in Central America and the Dominican Republic often have knowledge gaps with regards to climate change 
impacts,  and how these impacts affect farmers.  These gaps include knowledg e on: i) water availability ; ii) areas 
susceptible to extreme weather events; iii) locations of smallholder farmers; iv) projected changes in temperature 
and rainfall; v) expected impacts on crop yields; and vi) adaptation strategies.  These knowledge gaps impede the 
mainstreaming of climate change adaptation into the policies and plans of the different sectors.  In this regard and 
in particular for the selected pilot sites, the availability of historical observed meteorological data is very li mited  as 
well as the availability of site- specific climate change projections.  There are only 24  meteorological stations within 
the limits of the Dry Corridor . Only for three of the countries , there were stations within the prioritized pilot sites and 
just one of them (Honduras) had complete observed rainfall and temperature data for a period of over 30 years  
with less than 15% of discontinuity.  
 
• Barrier 2:  Limited knowledge and understanding of EbA and other adapt ation measures among decision- makers 
and communities.  Decision -makers and communities in the Dry Corridor and Arid Zones frequently have limited 
knowledge and understanding of EbA, and of adaptation measures such as water - and resource- efficient  
technologies. They have limited access to information and limited understanding of the value of ecosystem services


---
*Page 22*
---

G 
 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.2.1 | PAGE 20 OF 96 
   
 and the costs and benefits of ecosystem restoration, in particular because of the large scales at which many 
ecosystem ser vices are generated.  As a result, they have limited capacity to implement these interventions and 
reap their benefits.  
 
• Barrier 3: Insufficient implementation of existing policies on climate change adaptation and limited integration of 
EbA and other adaptation measures into sectoral policies.  Climate change adaptation is a priority in the NDCs of 
all seven participating countries. National policies also indirectly support EbA; for example, conservation policies, 
and the need to restore, conserve and sustainably manage the Dry Corridor and Arid Zone forests, wetlands and 
agroforestry systems is widely recognised. However, institutions at both national and local level have insufficient 
knowledge and technical capacity to implement existing policies, preventing  policies from achieving the necessary 
impacts.  
 
• Barrier 4:  Limited technical capacity within governments and local communities to implement EbA and other 
adaptation measures.  Governments have gradually reduced public budgets for the provision of extension services 
that could promote EbA. As a result, communities receive limited support, training and technical assistance to 
implement climate- resilient practices at landscape- , community - and household- level.  
 
• Barrier 5: Limited knowledge and technic al capacity to adopt technologies and approaches for efficient water use.  
Technologies that support efficient water use have been implemented successfully in the region, but these cases 
have not been well documented. As a result, there is limited  information on water management and improved 
technologies , both for policy -makers at the local -level,  and for water users . In the agricultural sector, specifically, 
successful water -efficient technologies are known for large commercial farming (e.g. drip irrigation of sugarcane 
and oil palms), but knowledge of water -efficient technologies is less widespread among smallholder farmers.  
 
• Barrier 6: Limited access for financial institutions to  specific refi nanci ng options  for the implementation of EbA , 
water - and resource- efficient  technologies in the private sector.  Private sector investors, credit agencies and 
financial institutions have limited evidence of the benefits of EbA and other adaptation measures  and limited ac cess 
to specific EbA refinancing  options . In addition, valuations by these institutions do not take into account important 
long-term benefits of EbA. As a result,  these institutions have limited opportunity to assess the commercial viability 
of EbA approaches and natural resource- based businesses, and to develop related financial services and products.   
 
• Barrier 7: Limited access to credit for adaptation interventions among vulnerable populations.  Smallholder farmers 
and rural households require credit to cover the initial costs of implementing EbA and water - and resource- efficient  
technologies. However, the high risk of such credit to financing institutions limits the availability of credit to 
vulnerable communities. This is because banks  often lack guarantees from central  banks/multi -national 
development banks to reduce their risk in providing credit to  individuals and small businesses with little collateral 
and small farm sizes .  
 
• Barrier 8: Absence or limited development of economic incentives for investment in sustainable natural resource 
management and EbA.  Although some water funds and schemes for payment for environmental services (PES) 
exist in the participating countries, the majority of these funds and schemes operate at limited and local scales.  
Barriers to the implementation of such economic incentives include lack of enabling strategic, legal and institutional 
frameworks and lack of transparent monitoring arrangements.  
 
Programme  objective  
37. The proposed programme's in terventions have been designed to overcome the above- mentioned barriers and 
contribute to the programme objective of strengthening the adaptive capacity of  vulnerable people, including  
smallholder and commercial farmers, as well as entrepreneurs in rural communities in the Dry Corridor and Arid Zones.  They will also contribute to the conservation and re storation of degraded, climate- vulnerable landscapes 
through improved agricultural and agroforestry practices, as well as reforestation 
and improved forest 
management practices.  This will be achieved  through the facilitation of  a shift in the approach to catchment and 
water demand management , seeing governments promoting large- scale implementation of  EbA and other  
adaptation measures, while simultaneously  unlocking finance for the adoption of such sustainable practices 
that will build resilience against extreme climate events such as severe drought and extreme rainfall. This shift will take place through the implementation of  four interconnected Outputs, described in detail in Section B.3.  A 
description of how the programme ’s activities will overcome the identified barriers is provided below.   
 
38. Barriers 1 and 2 will be overcome predomi nantly through activities under Output 3, including through the 
dissemination of knowledge products on climate change impacts, EbA and other adaptation measures through


---
*Page 23*
---

G 
 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.2.1 | PAGE 21 OF 96 
   
 a regional knowled ge hub linked to the existing Environmental Observatory  based at the Central American 
Commission for Environment and Development (CCAD) . In addition, policy - and decision- makers wi ll be trained 
on relevant aspects of climate change — including the disproportionate impact of climate change on women 
and other vulnerable group s — and the role of EbA and other adaptation measures in mitigating these impacts. 
To ensure the sustainability of programme interventions in overcoming these barriers, local community 
organisations — including  environmental and women's groups — will be trained to continue the development 
and dissemination of climate information and knowledge products. Policy - and decision- makers will also be 
trained on how to integrate climate change adaptation into local , national and regional  policies  and strategies ; 
and their experiences in doing so will be disseminated throughout the Dry Corridor and Arid Zones to promote 
this process across different sectors throughout the region.  Compone nt 3 will also contribute to overcoming 
barrier 1 through the collaboration with  regional research institutions as one of the main areas of research will 
be focused on strengthening the availability of downscaled climate projections for the Dry Corridor and the 
analysis of impacts over hydrological variables. This training will overcom e Barrier 3 . The above activities will 
combine to result in the dissemination of information on climate change adaptation and its financing across the 
Dry Corridor and Arid Zones, as well as the mainstreaming of this information into local and national pol icies. 
This result will contribute to the outcome of strengthened awareness of climate threats and risk -reduction 
processes in the seven target countries.   
 
39. The technical capacity of local government, farmers and rural communities will be strengthened through 
activities under Output 1, enabling them to implement EbA and other adaptation measures, and overcoming 
Barriers 4 and 5. To achieve this, country -specific protocols will be developed for the: i) implementation of 
sustainable EbA practices; ii) adoption of water -efficient technologies by households; and iii) adoption of 
resource- efficient  technologies that reduce fuelwood demand. These protocols will be developed by the local 
experts  in the Project Execution Units   with support from the regional experts and the monitoring and evaluation 
committees  under the supervision of the Executing Entity  (FAO) prior to the implementation o f on-the-ground 
interventions  in each country ,and will be validated through stakeholder engagements in each municipality . 
Training in the use of these protocols will be provided to local government representatives using a training- of-
trainers approach. In addition, local communities will be trained on EbA and other adaptation interventions, 
including visits to demonstration sites to facilitate farmer -to-farmer knowledge transfer. Through activities under 
Output 2, on-the-ground EbA interventions and water - and resource- efficient technologies will be introduced 
across seven catchments, the implementation of which will result in lessons learned and best practices to 
contribute to the knowledge base regarding the adoption of these interventions. The evidence base generated 
through the implement ation and monitoring of these interventions will be shared with local decision -makers, the 
private sector (including commercial farmers) and local communities through the regional knowledge hub linked to the CCAD’s Environmental Observatory . This evidence base will facilitate the uptake  by communities  of 
financial products and services to finance EbA and other adaptation measures as described further below. In addition, t he activities that have been designed t o overcome Barriers 4 and 5  will result in
: i) strengthened 
technical capacity of beneficiaries to implement EbA and other adaptation measures ; and ii) the implementation 
of large- scale adaptation interventions in rural communities across seven target catc hments. These results will 
both contribute to the outcome of strengthened adaptative capacity and reduced exposure to climate risks 
among vulnerable communities in the Dry Corridor and Arid Zones.  
 
40. Barriers 6 and 7 will be overcome mainly through activities under Output 4 . These include the establishment 
of financial products and services to finance EbA investments, including  via a blended EbA finance structure 
with two main  facilities , namely  a; i) a blended EbA  lending facility for small - and large- scale EbA investments 
at farm- , enterprise-  and household- level via two different funding streams  provided by CABEI  to the IFIs ; and 
ii) a guarantee facility  to provide additional security for participating intermediary financial institutions for on -
lending to partner financial institutions to engage in the riskier target beneficiary segment . The technical 
capacity of financial institutions will be increased to enable the operationalisation and delivery of these new 
financial products and services, whilst awareness will be raised among the private sector and vulnerable 
communities on how to access these mechanisms for the implementation of adaptation measures. These 
activities will result in financial products and servi ces to finance EbA investments being offered by last mile 
partner financial institutions  to the communities that need them the most. To increase economic incentives for 
investing in EbA and other sustainable land management practices, an evidence base will  be constructed based 
on the introduction of adequate and where possible digital monitoring, reporting and verification systems for all 
activities financed. This will help to demonstrate the value and effectiveness of proposed adaptation activities 
to policy -makers and financial institutions so that these considerations will be integrated into development 
plans. A methodology will also be developed to value ecosystem service s. It will be used to guide local 
governments in making policy changes and implementing economic incentives — such as water funds — for


---
*Page 24*
---

G 
 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.2.1 | PAGE 22 OF 96 
   
 sustainable land management practices. This will overcome Barrier 8 . The activities described above will 
combine to contribute tow ards the outcome of strengthened institutional and regulatory systems for climate -
responsive planning and development in the seven target countries.  
 
41. Together, the above- described interventions will overcome the barriers to improving climate resilience of rural 
communities in the Dry Corridor and Arid Zones. They will contribute to the achievement of three outcomes  (as 
detailed in the Theory of Change d iagram below) and, ultimately, to the goal of increased climate resilience of 
vulnerable communities  across the region.  The sustainability of these interventions will be ensured th rough a 
detailed exit strategy  centred around the principles of : i) long-term community ownership; ii) mainstreaming 
initiatives into catchment management and planning frameworks; iii) incentivising  private sector investments 
into EbA ; and iv) sustainable financial instruments .

B.3. Projec t/programme description  
42. Programme  interventions will be implemented in all six countries of the D ry Corridor — Costa Rica, El Salvador, 
Guatemala, Honduras, Nicaragua and Panama — as well as the Arid Zones of the Dominican Republic. A 
single catchment has been selected for each country based on a detailed climate change vulnerability 
assessment, with five to six municipalities targeted within each catchment (see Section 12 of Annex 2: 
Feasibility Study). The target municipalities  for each country are:  
• Guatemala — Canillá, Chicamán, San Andrés Sajcabaja, Uspantán and Zacualpa in the  Río Salinas 
watershed;  
• Hond uras —  Apacilagua, Choluteca, Duyure, Marcovia, Morolica, and Orocuina  in the  Choluteca watershed ;


---
*Page 25*
---

G 
 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.2.1 | PAGE 23 OF 96 
   
 • El Salvado r — Concepción Batres, El Carmen, El Tránsito, Jucuarán and San Miguel in the  San Miguel 
watershed;  
• Nicaragua — El Jícaro, Palacagüina, Somoto, Telpaneca and Yalagüina in the Rio Coco watershed;  
• Costa Rica — Bagaces, Carrillo , Liberia , Nicoya and Santa Cruz in the Tempisque watershed;  
• Panama — Guarar, Macaracas, Pocrí, Pedasí and Tonosí  in the  Los Santos watershed; and  
• Dominican Republic  — El Pino, Los Almácigos, Monción, Partido and San Ignacio de Sabaneta in the  
Guayubín and Mao watersheds.  
The programme' s regional approach will create an enabling environment for scalable and sustainable catchment 
and water demand management, facilitating large- scale investment in, and implementation of, EbA and water -
efficient technologies. The total grant request of US$ 84.3 million will be used in the following ways:  i) US$10. 1 
million will be used to strengthen the technical capacity of local government, farmers and rural communities to 
implement EbA and other adaptation measures through the activities comprised in output  1;  ii) US$ 2 8.5 million 
will be used to implement large- scale adaptation interventions — including EbA, water - and resource- efficient 
technologies — in rural communities across the region throughout output 2; i ii) US$28 million will form the grant 
facility through which vulnerable communities can access on- grants to foster the creation of a busi ness ecosystem 
around EbA available technologies  as per activity 1.2. ; iv) US$ 6.3 million will be used to generate and share 
information on climate change adaptation and finance across the region, and mainstream it into local and national 
policies  within the output 3; v) US$ 6.5 million will be used to set up the financial mechanisms an d strengthen the 
technical capacity of CABEI accredited and non- regulated financial institutions to access and channel funds through 
them throughout output 4; vi) US$ 4.2 million will be used for overall management and monitoring of programme 
activities  (Independent evaluations and PMC costs) ; and vii) US$0.7  million is requested for contingency . 
 
 
Component 1. Mainstreaming of EbA, water -efficient technologies and natural resource -based businesses  into 
selected catchments  
 
43. Under Component 1 , three interrelated outputs will facilitate the widespread implementation of adaptation 
measures across the region. This will include: i) providing technical assistance to local government, farmers 
and rural communities to implement adaptation measures; i i) mainstreaming adaptation measures and 
incentives into policy and planning as well as disseminating information on climate change and adaptation 
across the Dry Corridor and Arid Zones; and iii) implementing on- the-ground EbA and water/ resource- efficient  
technologies.  These outputs will complement Output 4 by providing the technical assistance needed by local 
government, farmers and rural communities to implement EbA activities and water/ resource- efficient  
technologies financed by the mechanisms establishe d under Output 4 . In addition, increased knowledge 
disseminated under Component 1  regarding EbA and water -efficient technologies and newly -available financial 
mechanisms will facilitate access for those previously unable to fund these adaptation measures, as well as 
creating an enabling environment for the establishment of economic incentives for investment in EbA.  
 
Output 1.  Strengthened technical capacity of local government, farmers and rural communities to implement EbA and 
other adaptation measures.  
 
44. Output 1 will be fully executed by FAO who will be responsible for the establishment of the National  Project 
Execution Units in each country as well as host ing the Regional Project Management Unit in their Regional 
Office in Panama.  CABEI will s ign a Subsidiary Agreement with FAO  for the execution of this output. This output 
will be executed directly through the national structure of FAO as well as through agreements with national 
partners when necessary  for the undertaking of capacity building ac tivities, trainings and community 
engagement  as well as for the promotion of opportunities through the financial mechanism of the programme  
and the monitoring of the impact of the investments.  
45. Activities under Output  1 will contribute to GCF Outcome A7.0 — Strengthened adaptive capacity and reduced 
exposure to climate risks — by facilitating the adoption of improved land use practices, as well as natural 
resource- based businesses (NRBs)  and alternative climate- resilient  livelihoods. A community -based approach 
will be adopted to identify site- specific interventions in each of the 36 municipalities targeted by the programme 
during the first year of the programme as described in paragraphs 51 and 99, which will be implement ed under 
Output 2. FAO as the Executing Entity for these activities will ensure that the site-specific intervention plans 
meet all eligibility criteria  and ultimately retain the final decision to approve implementation of all interventions 
under this output.  Technical assistance will be provided to local governments to adopt and implement these 
intervention plans  during the first year of the programme and with follow up activities along the programme.
