72 Technical assistance will not be limited to farmers accessing the financial mechanisms from Output 1, but  to any farmers in the target t 
municipalities that are open to adopting improved practices but lack the technical knowledge to do so.  
 46. Technical assistance will also be provided to farmers and rural communities in 36 municipalities, on the 
implementation of on- the-ground EbA practices, including silvopasture, agroforestry and sustainable land 
management72. The technical assistance under this component will also support the integration of EbA in policy 
and regulatory frameworks at the local level focusing mainly on water management regulations and  water 
management community structures as well as local land use planning instruments. Target municipalities  were 
selected following  a rigorous vulnerability assessment and extensive consultations with key stakeholders in 
each country . Using  IPCC guidelines, v ulnerability  was determined as  a function of: i) the profi le, magnitude 
and rate of climate changes to which a system is exposed; ii) the sensitivity of the system to those changes; 
and iii) the adaptive capacity of that system.  Based on the result of the  vulnerability assessment , a single 
watershed was identifie d in the most vulnerable area for each country  (except for Dominican Republic, where 
two adjacent watersheds were selected) , and then 5- 6 municipalities  were selected within the target 
watersheds.  Details of the vulnerability assessment and site selection process are provided in Section 12 of 
Annex 2: Feasibility Study .  
 
47. Country -specific implementation protocols will be developed for: i ) the implementation of sustainable EbA 
practices; ii) the adoption of water -efficient technologies; and iii) the adoption of resource- efficient technologies 
that reduce fuelwood demand.   These protocols will be developed by the local experts in the Proje ct Execution 
Units  with support from the regional experts and the monitoring and evaluation committees under the 
supervision of the Executing Entity (FAO) prior to the implementation of on- the-ground interventions in each 
country,and will be validated thr ough stakeholder engagements in each municipality.  To operationalise these 
protocols, a training- of-trainers programme will be introduced for representatives of local departments of 
environment/water, community -based organisations, water committees, local cooperatives , environme ntal and 
women's organisations within each country. Particular attention will be paid to the inclusion of women in this 
training, especially considering their active role in water and fuelwood use. Site visits will be arranged to 
demonstration sites that will be established in each of the target municipalities (Output 2 ), as well as showcasing 
mature plots where EbA interventions are already providing demonstrable benefits regarding water security. 
Commercial farmers, smallholder subs istence farmers, local communit ies and individual households will also 
be provided with technical assistance to adopt water -efficient technologies, including drip irrigation and 
rainwater harvesting, as well as for resource- efficient  practices, including f uelwood drying and efficient charcoal 
production which will reduce the pressure of fuelwood collection on remnant forests.   
 
48. In addition to the technical assistance provided for on- the-ground implementation of EbA practices and 
water/ resource- efficient  technologies, training will be provided to communities on the development of small, 
locally appropriate livelihoods. This will include training for natural resource- based businesses (NRBs) that: i) 
will support the implementation and mai ntenance of EbA interventions — such as tree nurseries and the 
manufacturing, distribution or repair of water -efficient technologies; and ii) rely on non- destructive use of 
ecosystem goods and services provided through EbA — such as businesses developed ar ound the ecotourism 
industry, or non- timber forest products (NTFPs). The establishment of NRBs not only contributes to the 
upscaling of EbA interventions, but also enhances the resilience of vulnerable communities by providing 
climate -resilient income- generating opportunities. The promotion of NRBs — in conjunction with increased 
access to financing under Output 4 and increased knowledge and understanding of adaptation options under 
Output 3 — will strengthen the adaptive capacity of smallholder farming communities under future climate 
conditions. Programme activities that will be implemented under Output 1 are lis ted below.  
 
• Activity 1.1. Develop site- specific intervention plans for the seven target catchments to integrate EbA measures 
through a participatory process with municipal authorities, local communities and other stakeholders.  
• Activity 1.2. Provide technic al assistance to municipal authorities, farmers and rural communities for the 
implementation of EbA practices as well as water - and resource- efficient  technologies.  
• Activity 1.3. Provide technical assistance to farmers and rural communities for the develop ment of natural 
resource- based businesses and alternative climate- resilient livelihoods.  
 
Output 2. Demonstration adaptation interventions implemented in rural communities across seven target catchments 
in the Dry Corridor and Arid Zones.

73 A description  of the target municipalities and catchments is provided in Section 12 of  Annex 2: Feasibility Study . 49. Activities under Output 2 will contribute to GCF Outcome A7.0 — Strengthened adaptive capacity and reduced 
exposure to climate ri sks — by providing the necessary resources for the on- the-ground implementation of EbA 
interventions as well as water - and resource- efficient  technologies in rural communities across the seven target 
catchments. Support will be targeted at the most vulnerable communities in 36 municipalities73, that lack the 
capacity to access financing and equipment to implement adaptation interventions without external assistance.  
The hydrological modeling undertaken for the programme areas (described in Section B.1 of the proposal and 
in further detail in Section 13 of Annex 2: Feasibility Study) has demonstrated that a combination of adaptation 
interventions, such as forest conservation, reforestation, transition to agroforestry systems, introduction of 
irrigation systems and vegetative barriers results in substantial improvements for all key hydrological variables 
in the catchment areas. As a result, a combination of adaptation measures will be implemented in each of the 
target catchments to maximise the benefits conveye d and the reduction of climate change impacts, specifically 
on hydrological parameters . FAO will be the EE for the implementation of these measures under activit ies 2.1 
and 2.2 . The grant -supported interventions all also serve as demonstration sites that w ill provide practical 
examples to surrounding communities on how to implement sustainable practices. CABEI will be the EE for the 
implementation of the grant -facility under activity 2.3.  
 
50. The programme  will work with local organisations in each municipality to implement on- the-ground EbA 
interventions that provide multiple ecosystem goods and services to vulnerable communities. The relevant 
organisations will be identified during the development of site- specific intervention plans (Activity 1 .1). Relevant 
interventions have been identified for each municipality based on community consultations , sectoral expert 
opinion obtained for the Feasibility Study  and the results of the hydrological models for three  of the pilot 
catchments . The selected interventions remain as indicative and are subject to validation during the inception 
phase and in consultation with the specific communities  These indicative interventions , pre-identified with the 
available and devel oped information  during the formulation process include : i) establishing one tree nursery per 
municipality focusing on native species and fuelwood trees (36 total); ii) establishing 4,160 ha of forest 
protection zones across the seven catchment areas; iii)  protecting 1,820 ha of natural forest in major recharge 
areas and riparian zones, including assisted regeneration;  iv) restoring 5,170 ha of natural forest areas across 
seven catchments; v) restoring 820 ha of native pine forests in Guatemala, Honduras an d Nicaragua; vi) 
establishing 1,293 km of agroforestry systems using diversified living fence arrangements in basic grains crops; 
vii) establishing 612 ha of agroforestry systems for natural shade in coffee plantations; viii)  establishing 1,280 
km of silv opasture systems using diversified living fence arrangements; ix)  establishing 1,280 ha of silvopasture 
systems across the 36 target municipalities; x) establishing 5,305 ha of sustainable fuelwood and timber 
plantations across the 36 target municipalities; xi) establishing 129 km of firebreaks for forests and plantations; xii) constructing 118 km of living barriers for soil conservation around agricultural plots;  
and xiii) constructing 
118 km of superficial drainage for soil conservation. In addition to t hese EbA practices, activities under Output 
2 will also address the projected increase in water scarcity under future climate conditions by introducing water -
efficient technologies to reduce demand for scarce water resources in rural communities. This will  include 
installing: i) 109 rainwater harvesting systems at the community level targeting clinics and schools; ii)  156 
community -level rainwater reservoirs (500 m3 each); and iii) 38  community -level Water pumping systems by 
solar panels. Finally, resource- efficient  technologies will be introduced in each of the target municipalities, 
focussing on efficient charcoal production methods using half -orange kilns (43 systems). This will maximise the 
energy output per unit of biomass, thereby reducing the pressure of fuelwood collection on natural ecosystems.   
51. The validation of these indicative pre- selected interventions will be based on the development of the site-
specific plans (activity 1.1) that will include both a scientific analysis of s pecific climate projec tcions through the 
hydrological models , as well as sound stakeholder consultations that will allow to identify the most appropriate 
interventions . The validation of these indicative interventions will therefore be based on the following criteria:  
• - Relevance to site- specific climate risks  
• - Positive i mpact potential over climate risks 
• - Familiarity and acceptance by stakeholders  
• - Positive impact potential over most vulnerable sectors of the community  
• - Positive impact potential on gender balance  
• - Technical feasibility of the intervention  
• - Compliance with social en environmental safeguards  
• - Potential co- benefits of the interv ention  
• - Minimal level of risks and constraints  for implementation  
• - Potential for self -sustainability

52. To foster grant support for identification, incubation or even acceleration of site- specific solutions an d 
distribution models , a US$28 million grant facility will be established to support site- specific EbA and water -
/resource- efficient  technologies for the most vulnerable communities, who are unable to access loan finance. 
A bottom- up approach will be used to identify eligible projects, following stringent eligibility checks for all grant 
applications. The Regional Project Steering Committee (RPSC) — comprising representatives from FAO, 
CCAD, CABEI, UNEP, national -level CCAD liaisons and the Regional Project  Manager — will define the 
eligibility criteria for each country at programme  inception, with final sign off provided by CABEI as the the 
Executing Entit y. This process will include identifying specific companies and organisations that have a strong 
commitment to delivering agreed results of EbA activities and water technologies. The grant eligibility criteria 
and selection process is foreseen to focus on individuals or legal entities:  
• with a proven track record in implementing or providing EbA solutions or water technologies;  
• situated or attending the programme areas of operations in the defined catchment areas;  
• with a clear vision or plan on how to scale EbA solution or water technology provision;  
• able to provide clear and measurable result metrics that can be verified and audited; and  
• that adhere to the established grant management principles.  
 
53. Legal entities can refer to business comp anies, unions or cooperatives, associations, cooperatives, sector 
organisations, public research and training centres and NGOs that directly cater to targeted end beneficiaries.  
 
54. The qualifying stakeholders will be invited to apply for funding following the application process of the grant 
manual. Gr ant finance will be available for the following purposes – in four innovation areas of business linkage 
innovation, service and input innovation and sector organizations and institutions strengthening:  
• New or additional working capital of existing or new p rojects ; 
• New or additional investment capital of existing or new projects ; 
• Seed capital for new projects ; and  
• New or additional technical assistance for new or existing projects .  
 
55. National Project Execution Units (NPEU) will guide communities on how to de velop proposals to access the 
funds . The NPEUs will be directly hired by FAO as the Executing Entity for output  2 (for activities 2.1 and 2.2)  
and will be based in the selected municipalities for the execution of the EbA demonstration sites. NPEUs will 
identify project s that can be supported with the above- mentioned grant purposes to promote innovation in three 
categories: 1) Business linkage in novation, focusing on strengthening commercial cooperation agreements 
between different service providers, such as between PFIs (financing EbA solutions packages, rather than 
stand- alone solutions), a set of EbA solution providers (e.g. drip irrigation sys tem, water reservoir installation, 
organic fertilizer) and agricultural extension service providers to drive capacity building on how to access the 
EbA solutions and their finance, bundled in a comprehensive offer to end beneficiaries; 2) Service and input  
innovation, focusing on identifying locally relevant EbA solutions that are not yet available in the market and 
can be “imported” to be made available; and 3) the strengthening of sector organization and institutions, such 
as multi -stakeholder water funds , producer associations or municipal and community coordination entities.  
 
56. On-the-ground EbA -oriented grant support will benefit community -based institutions including 
mancomunidades74, municipal associations, farmers cooperatives, solution providers or civ il society 
organisations (CSOs). Eligible projects will include community -level investments, the establishment of water 
funds, seed funding for payment for ecosystem services (PES) and support for the adoption of selected EbA 
practices and/or water - and resource- efficient  technologies . The grant facility will channel investment to 
selected projects in each target municipality. The selection of eligible projects will be managed within the 
proposed governance structure in two competitive selection rounds per year during year 2, 3, 4 and 5 of the 
programme by i) identifying suitable EbA project s at Project Execution Unit level, ii) apply first screening a t 
National Advisory Committees  level, iii)  pre-selecting proposed EbA projects from the countries at Nationa l 
Advisory Committees  level and iii) deciding upon EbA projects in the Regional Steering Committee  and iv) final 
approval by CABEI as the Executing Entity . Further details on the selection process are described in the 
Operational Guidelines (Annex 23). In some less developed areas in terms of solutions available, successful

Output 3.  Information on climate change adaptation and its financing disseminated across the region and mainstreamed 
into local and national policies.  
 
58. This component will be executed by CCAD as the regional entity that leads the formulation of the region al 
strategy for Environmental Management and the regional strategy for Climate Change. The Regional 
Environmental Observatory gathers information from all countries in the region and provides the opportunity to 
build scientific information into the regiona l decision -making process. It is expected that this programme will 
feed a significant amount of information into this platform that will also be strengthened through communities 
of practice on EbA to  foster science- based decision making at the regional lev el. CCAD has collaborated in the 
past with regional technical organizations such as IUCN, FAO, UNDP and UNEP in the production of technical studies for the region and it provides a key political platform for the dissemination of this information to influence decision making at the national level. As such, their leadership in this output of the programme  
is essential to 
ensure that results from the programme are being disseminated among countries and used to inform both key 
regional strategies and national p olicies. UN Environment will also provide technical advice through this 
component developing knowledge products  including sustainable landscape management standards and

 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.2.1 | PAGE 28 OF 96 
   
 procedures, opportunities for access to financing and women economic empowerment as we ll as technical 
guidelines for the assessment of ecosystem services . 
59. Activities under Output 3 will contribute to GCF Outcome A8.0 —  Strengthened awareness of climate threats 
and risk -reduction processes — by building an evidence base for the effectiveness  of EbA and other adaptation 
measures in reducing the impacts of climate change and by disseminating this information to local decision-
makers, the private sector (including commercial farmers) and local communities. The dissemination of 
information will b e managed through the establishment of a regional knowledge hub linked to the CCAD 's 
Environmental Observatory. The knowledge hub will serve two primary functions. First, it will act as a repository 
for EbA information, including, but not limited to,  all protocols developed by the programme as well as the best 
practices and lessons learned collected through the programme's monitoring and evaluation processes , 
previous and ongoing complementary projects . This will include guidance on how to access opportunities for 
accessing finance provided through the programme, with specific focus on wom en's economic empowerment. 
Second, the hub will provide users with specific knowledge products that will guide policy - and decision- makers 
to effectively implement sustainable landscape management practices.  The knowledge products will also 
include technic al assistance for creating business opportunities as well as technical guidelines for the 
assessment of ecosystem services. This will support decision- making for climate change adaptation, as well 
as drought and flood risk management. Training will also be provided to policy - and decision- makers at the 
municipal level on how to use the knowledge hub and associated tools. Furthermore, community organisations – including women's  groups – and traditional leaders will be trained to act as champions in developin g, revising 
and disseminating information products to support the adoption of EbA and other resilient practices. This 
approach will facilitate the sharing of ideas and sustainable practices between communities from the grass 
roots level, using existing str uctures and traditional methods which will not only extend the reach of the 
dissemination campaign, but also the uptake by local communities. Awareness will also be raised regarding 
the financial mechanisms established under Output 4, thereby facilitating access to climate financing for local 
governments, farmers and local communities. The information products made available through the knowledge 
hub will be demand- driven and will draw on best practices and lessons learned from governments, 
municipalities, community -based organisations, water managers and private sector stakeholders. By 
centralising the knowledge hub, information will be effectively disseminated across the region and decision -
makers will be able to learn from the experiences of other countri es within the region.  
 
60. The hub will be led by a Regional Knowledge Coordinator , who will be hired by CCAD for this output . The 
coordinator will be responsible for the strategic positioning of the hub as the region' s foremost online platform 
for evidence- based products to facilitate the mainstreaming of an ecosystem- based adaptation approach for 
long term catchment and municipal level planning. Additionally, an administrative officer will support the 
coordinator in all aspects related to the daily management  of hub activities and the implementation of its annual 
work plans. Part -time senior and junior researchers will be hired in every country to assist with the synthesis of 
findings from catchments, programme advances, impact and experiences learned for diss emination across the 
region. The researchers will develop regional knowledge products informed by the compilation and analysis of 
data and information from the seven catchments where the programme is implemented. Additionally, the 
researchers will work closely with the Project Execution Units to help with the preparation of evidence- based 
communication products at country and regional levels.   
 
61. Output 3 will also contribute to GCF Outcome A5.0 — Strengthened institutional and regulatory systems for 
climate -responsive planning and development — by building the technical capacity of national - and local -level 
decision -makers to promote climate change adaptation in the Dry Corridor and Arid Zones. This  will be 
supported by the development or adjustment of a methodology for the valuation of ecosystem services to 
develop accounts profiles of natural capital for integration into development plans in the Dry Corridor and Arid 
Zones. The methodology will draw on best practices and lessons learned from ongoing initiatives, including the: 
i) "incentivo forestal" in Guatemala; ii) payment for environmental services in Panama and Costa Rica; iii) Water Fund of the city of Quito in Ecuador ; and iv) water funds es
tablished in cities in Guatemala, Costa Rica and 
the Dominican Republic . Ultimately, the valuation of ecosystem services will be used to promote the 
establishment of economic incentives for EbA investments — for example, water funds and payments for 
envir onmental services. The capacity of local governments to make policy changes that include these kinds of 
incentives will also be strengthened, including the use of protocols and criteria for the adoption of EbA and 
implementation of economic incentives for sustainable land management. This output will also support the 
integration of EbA into national sectoral policies by providing inputs into the currently under development 
National Adaptation Plans (NAPs). At least 5 out of the 7 countries are currently dev eloping their NAPs with a 
sectoral or local focus and have identified water security, ecosystem restoration and resilient agriculture as 
main priorities. The technical assistance provided through this output to policy makers will also focus on the

Component 2. Financing and implementation of EbA as well as water - and resource- efficient technologies  
across selected catchments  
63. The execution arrangements for this component will be distributed among CABEI and FAO . CABEI will be 
responsible for those activities related to the set up and management  of the EbA financing mechanism including 
the lending, guarantee and grant facilities  and  the set up of the Regional Project Steering Committee guidelines 
in relation to the financial mechanism,  (Activit ies 4.1 and 4.2). FAO will be responsible for the ex ecution of the 
awareness raising activities for the financial component of the programme as well as for the monitoring of the 
investments undertaken at local level by the local financial institutions through the Project Execution Units  (Activit y 
4.3). UN Environment will deliver technical support in this component through the development of training materials 
for financial institutions, support in the development of MRV systems and annual support in the monitoring of 
results from the investments undertaken by financial institutions  (under activity 4. 3). UNEP will be engaged by 
FAO through a UN to UN agreement.  
64. The second component under the proposed programme will establish and operationalise financial mechanisms to 
facilitate investment in EbA practices, as well as the adoption of water - and resource- efficient technologies  (Activity 
4.2). This component will build on the experience of CABEI's regional CAMBio II project as well as the regional 
MEbA project to increase access to climate financing for local or ganisations and communities — including non-
regulated financial institutions, commercial and subsistence farmers, as well as vulnerable households —  through 
an existing network of intermediary financial institutions  (IFIs). Using the increasing body of kno wledge on EbA 
financing generated through CAMBio II and other similar initiatives, this component will focus on providing grants 
and blended finance for EbA investments as well as water - and resource- efficient technologies. This will promote 
an approach of  bottom -up identification of adaptation interventions and de- risked top- down financing for adaptation.  
The types of EbA and water - and resource- efficient technologies eligible for financing through the established 
financial mechanisms include (among others ):  
• agroforestry systems;  
• silvopasture systems;  
• agrosilvopastoral systems;  
• windbreaks;  
• firebreaks;  
• mixed plant nurseries;  
• agricultural terraces;  
• soil conditioning;  
• efficient biomass stoves;  
• firewood drying and charcoal production;  
• rainwater collection in small reservoirs;  
• rooftop rainwater harvest systems;  
• solar water pumping systems;  
• drip irrigation;

Output 4.  Financial products and services to finance EbA investments are offered by Partner Financial Institutions 
(PFI), including PFI acces s to EbA on- lending funds and support mechanisms.  
 
65. Activities under Output 4  will contribute to GCF Outcome A7.0 — Strengthened adaptive capacity and reduced 
exposure to climate risks — by establishing financial mechanisms to support the adoption of EbA pr actices as 
well as water - and resource- efficient technologies. To that end t wo financing facilities will be established, 
concessional lending for EbA solutions relevant to reduce climate risks in the Dry Corridor  as well as credit 
guarantee to further streamline and disseminate EbA lending across the entire Dry Corridor.  
 
66. The EbA lending facility (EbA credit line) will be established and operationalised with the GCF senior loan 
provision of US$ 60 million and CABEI US$4 2.8 million co -finance together being offered to PFIs as blended 
finance mechanism at below -market conditions for small - and large- scale EbA investments at the farm -, 
enterprise-  and household- level. The objective of the EbA lending facility is to establish and operationalize 
financial mechanisms to facilitate investment in EbA practices, as well as the adoption of water - and resource-
efficient technologies. The EbA Lending facility deployed under sub- activity 4.2.1 will have a revolving character  
at the PFI level: while it will be established for a period of 10 years, loans to be disbursed to PFIs are expected to have a tenor of 2 -  5 years maximum (as a function of the expected EbA f inance portfolio to be created with 
end beneficiaries, i
.e. focusing more on working capital or investment capital, and in accordance with market 
standards). Additionally, loan agreeme nts with PFIs will foresee capital (and interest) payments over the tenor 
of the PFI loans. Returning funds will be reinvested to available PFIs, ensuring a closing of laid out loans before 
the facility’s closure.  
67. The guarantee mechanism can be used to coveco -financing,  to support the initial introduction of the lending 
facility and provide further de- risking . This is needed to support the introduction of EbA finance to the most 
vulnerable, an end beneficiary segment th at is usually without access to any finance due to its elevated risk 
profile. The r emaining guarantee mechanisms  will be used to leverage additional finance.

69. By providing a blended financing mechanism composed of concessional finance from GCF in combination with 
a market rate senior debt as co -financing from CABEI ( and an associated guarantee facility  – see above ), 
incentives are created for financial institutions on different levels to engage in EbA finance, for which there exist 
no or only limited offerings as of today. Intermediary F inancial Institutions in CABEI's established network will 
manage  the EbA lending facility  to  local partner  financial institutions such as cooperatives , small banks, NGOs, 
rural community banks, among others -  Partner Financial Institutions . In paralel these IFI will recei ve GCF funds 
and CABEI Co -financing funds via senior loans  to on -lend to the same PFIs. PFIs in turn will on- lend to end 
beneficiaries. Investment will be targeted towards community -level investment, rural MSMEs or other 
institutions that can foster  EbA, as well as water - and resource- efficient technologies, for end beneficiaries 
across different sectors.  
 
70. Partner Financial i nstitutions  will be able to access the EbA Lending facility at concessional below -market rates 
for direct on- lending to final customers for EbA investments. Investment will be targeted to finance  small-  and 
large- scale EbA investments at farm- , enterprise-  and household- level to initiate and support a paradigm shift 
in the regional approach to catchment and water demand management in the Dry Corridor and Arid Zones. As 
such, the facility will aim to invest in initiatives that improve the climate resilience of rural communities in the 
Dry Corridor and Arid Zones by: i) implementing integrated catchment management and restoring catchments; 
ii) improving hydrological flow and infiltration of rainwater  into groundwater reserves through forest and 
ecosystem restoration; and iii) reducing demand for scarce water resources by implementing water -efficient 
technologies at the farm-  and household- level.

requirements set out in the eligibility criteria, the TA facility will be available to strengthe n respective institutional 
capacities. Technical Assistance in EbA finance methodologies will be a condition precedent to gain access to 
the EbA Lending facility and Guarantee facility.

Committee at programme inception and finally approved by CABEI as the Executing Entity. Sub- credits75 will 
be made available in the local currency  to end- beneficiaries in the eligible geographic regions for both working 
capital and investment purposes . The s ub-credits will be provided under a sub- credit agreement (SCA) between 
the local PFIs  and the beneficiary using the PFI’s loan agreement forms an d including the standard provision 
to be developed in the credit manual. Further details on the eligibility criteria are provided in Annex 23. Elegibility 
criteria will also be guided by vulnerability assessments and learnings from the lending mechanism it self as the 
execution advances.  
 
73. Additionally , a guarantee facility76 will be linked to the EbA lending facility to — in the case of documented 
success EbA, i.e. increasing productivity and lowering production cost vis -à-vis non- programme scenarios — 
secure PFI’s on -lending and mobilise additional lending from IFIs’  and PFIs  own and additional public and 
private investors’ resources by mitigating against credit risks associated with commercial EbA Finance to end 
beneficiaries. CABEI will manage the guarantee facility as a window in its recently established Guarantee Fund 
(managed in- house), guaranteeing PFIs ’ sub-credit portfolio to the end borrowers (which in turn will be pledged 
by the PFIs to the IFIs) Such loans are usually –  especially for subsistence farmers – not fully covered by real 
guarantees such as real estate or other moveable assets pledges, hence making such constructs prohibitively 
expensive.  As digital EbA lending facility MRV systems will be integrated, CABEI will be in position to automate 
its extension of guarantee certificates over end beneficiary loans in line with current guarantee fund stipulations 
– IFIs will indirectly have partial c overage by CABEI’s guarantee fund, which will lower collateral requirements 
by PFIs and enhance access to the lending facility and leveraged finance for end beneficiaries. . The guarantee 
facility will issue a capped partial credit risk guarantees to IFIs f or their on lending to PFI portfolio.  If successful, 
the guarantee facility will hence be able to provide guarantee coverage to additional funds. The EbA Guarantee will be established as a liquid facility managed directly by CABEI with a US$30.0 million g
uarantee funded by 
GCF. A recent guarantee facility established by CABEI to attend Covid- 19 impacts for eligible end borrowers 
will provide the mechanism for the Guarantee facility in the proposed programme. Once operational and with 
increased EbA Finance experience at hand in the programme areas, its coverage will be expanded, following 
established risk management principles and depending on the guarantee’s performance (in terms of claims and outreach). In the same way, in case of claims exceeding expected  levels, the emission of additional guarantee 
obligations will be limited . The guarantee is foreseen to cover 25– 75% of end beneficiary sub-loan portfolios to 
eligible PFIs and hence targeted to leverage additional EbA finance with a ratio of 1:4.33 in the 
range of 
US$150.8 million  (see 6- 1, section 5 in Annex 3).  To that end, the Guarantee facility will de- risk operations in 
these new end beneficiary segments  and therefor increase the acceptance of the Lending facility, as well as to 
gather data that will be reviewed annually to establish the baseline of actual expected default of resulting sub -
loan portfolios. As function of this baseline analysis, the coverage of the Guarantee facility will be expanded up to the above expected leverage.  
 
74. IFIs will have the flexibility to use the guarantee facility and the EbA lending facility as stand- alone solutions or 
in combination. Potential options for IFIs include:  
1. EbA lending facility only for EbA investment to be financed where the IFI is comfortable taking the full credit risk of PFIs and for which it needs liquidity from the Lending facility;  
2. EbA guarantee facility only for EbA investment to be financed for which the IFI would be willing to pay for credit enhancement but for which it does not need external liquidity . This mechanism is being targeted to leverage 
additional finance from IFIs for on- lending to eligible PFIs directly;  and  
3. Blended guarantee/ lending facility mechanisms for EbA investment to be financed for which the IFIs  seek both 
risk mitigation and funding support from the two facilities. In that way, IFI can be incentivi sed to finance parts of 
eligible portfolios through the EbA l ending facility and finance remaining balances from resources making use 
of the de- risking  EbA guarantee facility offer.  
 
75. The financial facilities will be accompanied by a technical assistance (TA) facility (see below) that will develop 
capacity within municipalities and PFIs to monitor and assess the implementation of EbA and water/ resource -
efficient investment projects as well as to monitor and document the respective economic and ecosystem 
benefits. Where possible, Information and Communication Technologies will be introduced to provide data


---
*Page 35*
---

G 
 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.2.1 | PAGE 33 OF 96 
   
 analytics and satellite data insights into the EbA lending portfolio and climate impacts on the portfolio. FAO will 
be the Executing Entity for thi s TA Facility (under Activity 4.3) and will receive technical support from UNEP.  
 
76. To facilitate access to and operation of the facilities, Activity 4.3  will provide TA to selected PFIs. Technical 
assistance will be a mandatory condition for PFIs to access the EbA lending facility. This will ensure that 
capacities, especially for adequat e monitoring, reporting and verification (MRV) systems, are in place to 
manage the EbA finance. Such technical assistance will focus on developing capacity within municipalities and 
PFIs to monitor and assess the implementation of EbA and water/ resource- efficient investment projects as well 
as to monitor and document the respective economic and ecosystem benefits. Where possible, Information and 
Communication Technologies will be introduced to provide data analytics and satellite data insights into the 
EbA lending portfolio and climate impacts on the portfolio.  
 
77. The TA facility is geared to focus primarily on direct technical assistance and a comprehensive Training- of-
Trainers or online course mechanism to strengthen: i) the awareness of climate change, ecos ystem degradation 
and water scarcity; ii) the identification and promotion of solutions to overcome these threats; iii) the application 
of methodologies, technologies and tools to foster EbA finance and manage climate and ecosystem risks via 
modern information management solutions, iv) the provision of specific EbA oriented tailor -made financial 
products and services to end beneficiaries, as well as of non- financial services such as information services or 
training; and v) the monitoring, reporting and veri fication systems in place via technology transfer to support 
learning on successful EbA and water/ resource- efficient technologies. The TA will comprise capacity -building 
for financial intermediaries on the concepts, financial methodology, and tools to prov ide products and services 
oriented to EbA and water -efficient technologies. Expert pools will be established in each country to provide 
country -specific technical assistance to PFIs based on a standardised methodology.  
78. The deployment of the loan facility w ill follow a phased approach along the programme to ensure cohesive and 
substantive impact of the financed activities as well as proper vulnerability assessments and identification of 
priorities for other vulnerable regions within the Dry Corridor where th e intervention could be scaled up. This 
phased approach will also allow for more time to strengthen capacities of the financial institutions and to 
promote the financial mechanism with communities and producers.  
79. The three implementation phases of the loan facility are described below:  
Launching the loan facility  
The loan facility will be initially deployed through local PFIs to attend priorities and requests from producers and 
communities within the 5 prioritized municipalities in the Tempisque basin. The specific eligibility criteria for the type 
of interventions to be included in the loan facility will be detailed during th e first year of the programme through 
consultations with communities, experts and financial institutions  – subject to final approval by CABEI . An MRV 
system will also be structured to gather information about the deployment of the loan and guarantee facili ties and 
the impacts of the EbA interventions financed. Additionally, the technical team (RPMU  in collaboration with PEUs 
and technical experts engaged through output 3)  will initiate a climate risk assessment and prioritization of other 
municipalities wit hin the Dry Corridor.  
Replicating the loan facility  
 As a result of the initial phase, the technical team (as above) and PFIs will count on information about the 
deployment rate of the loan facility and initial results from the impacts of the financed interventions. The larger 
climate risk assessments will have also provided information about climate risks and adaptation potent ial for 
additional municipalities  within the Dry Corridor. In this second phase, loans will be disbursed within the 5 initially 
prioritized municipalities and other surrounding municipalities  that have been prioritized and characterized through 
complete cl imate risk assessments.  
Scale up of the loan facility  
Finally, and after gathering more detailed information about the results of the deployment of the loan facility, the 
beneficial impacts of EbA interventions and undertaking substantive capacity building  both with community 
members and PFIs in the 5 prioritized municipalities and other vulnerable ones within the Dry Corridor, the loan 
facility will be expended geographically to cover for additional municipalities and always within the limits of the Dry Corridor to ensure substantive action to build resilience.

 The potential of the lending facility was modelled at the regional and national levels  to understand the absorption 
capacity on the ground, and is further described in annex 3  (financial model)  and 30 (demand analysis) of this 
proposal.  
An initial review of existing and publicly available literature, from academic research to practitioner report and 
project s’ references, shows not only a grow ing interest into the topic of adaptation and EbA Finance of the global 
and Central American financial sector, but also a diversifying set of methodologies available for the capacity building 
of financial institutions willing and interested to engage in the field.  
Furthermore, data from publicly available databases is accessible to define solid assumptions on potential demand for the implementation of EbA solutions and in consequence the demand for EbA Finance .  
81. REDCAMIF (Microfinance Network of Central Am erica and the Carribbean) is the most important network of 
microfinance institutions in the region. The organization provides a centralized database with data on its 
members’ performance.  
The network is organized as a network of networks, organizing 7 countries’ national networks into a regional one, 
representing 130 microfinance institutions that reach nearly 1.5 million clients via 1,211 branches. Of the 
aggregated outstanding loan portfolios of over US$ 2.1 billion , almost 49% is deployed to rural clients.  
While REDCAMIF is not representing the total of institutions active in all 7 countries, the network clearly provides 
the most aggregated reference data sets regarding especially inclusive rural finance which is one of the focus of 
this programme  
In order to understand financial institutions’ installed capacity, two dimensions were reviewed as preparation for the 
analysis:  
1. The traction of inclusive agricultural finance in the seven countries (see also section 4.2.4 in annex 30):  
2. The presence of financial institutions in the programme pilot areas in order to understand the level of 
potential offerings to disseminate EbA finance in pilot areas, to generate the required scaling of EbA finance as 
part of the programme . 
82. According to latest statistics from the REDCAMIF’s MIFIndex for June 2020,  the networks PFIs manage a total 
inclusive agricultural portfolio of US$324 million across all 7 countries for 221,477 clients. This represents more 
than 3 times the total size of the targeted lending facility’s volume of US$102.8 million. The microfinance 
sector’s involvement in inclusive agricultural finance is a good proxy for its ability to serve EbA finance, which 
will primarily  be targeted at Small -scale farmers.  
Usually, the microfinance sector refrains of serving these populations, due to key challenges, such as:  
1. Rural and agricultural finance are more costly due to greater distances to cover for loan origination as 
well as recovery.  
2. Especially agricultural producer s are considered more risky, as the low levels of technification of Small -
scale producers makes them more prone to adverse climate (change) impacts.  
3. Little incentives are being offered from investors to incentivize greater activities and outreach in 
agricultural microfinance, especially due to the lack of concessional finance.  
Further, especially for EbA and other green finance, markets are not developed and documentation requirements 
are regularly higher than for traditional finance, which increases cos t due to  
1. The need for more client awareness raising especially to not well known alternatives to traditional 
agricultural practices,  
2. More time to be spend with client assessment, due diligence and loan evaluation,  
3. Increased personnel training and re- training activities as consequence of higher complexity of EBA and 
climate finance interdynamics.  
83. It can hence be expected that the proposed programme will address all of these barriers by raising awareness 
and ultimatel y demand of target populations for EbA alternatives, providing digital process and data 
management solutions for better loan management, build capcities to better identify and mitigate (physical) 
climate -related financial risks and provide concessional finance to confront perceived risks.  
84. As the distribution of financial flows from the Lending facility are expected to be distributed among countries 
based on prioritization of the Regional Project Steering Committee, under the chair of CABEI, the following 
considerations are based on the assumption of an equal distribution – and put the total amount to be disbursed

 over the initial 4 years in relation to the existing agricultural microfinance portfolio. Hereby it is assumed that

as well as  
2. Increase the appetite of partner financial institutions to engage with Small -scale farmers that are 
deemed more risky.  
85. The results of the analysis of microfinance portfolios at the national level are summarized below:  
86. Costa Rica  
In Costa Rica, at June 2020, a total agricultural loan portfolio of US$ 16 million to 10,181 clients has been reported 
– representing the highest share of a total microfinance portfolio of US$38 million with 42%. All loans reported are 
being managed following individual lending methodologies (as opposed to group lending or community bank 
lending).  
The existing US$ 16 million loan portfolio to 10,181 clients is almost equal to the total amount disbursed via the 
proposed Lending facility of US$15 million  (assuming equal distribution of loan across countries  for discussion 
purposes) . According to the assumption in the financial modell ing of the Lending facility, this portfolio shall be 
reached after 4 years – or after year 5 of the programme.  
In the case of Costa Rica it is though expected, that only a minor part of 19% of that facility (or US$ 2.8 million) will 
actually be destined to Small- scale famer EbA finance, as commercial farming in the more developed agricultural 
sector plays a more important role. This represents roughly 17% of the present portfolio.  
87. Dominican Republic  
In Dominican Republic, at June 2020, a total agricultural loan portfolio of US$ 38 million to 22,151 clients has been reported – representing the second lowest share of a total microfinance portfolio of US$498 million with 8%. Almost 
all loans reported are  
being managed following individual lending methodologies, with only 1% following group 
lending methjodologies. Community bank lending is not being provided.  
The existing loan portfolio of US$ 38 million to 22,151 clients represents 2.5 times the targeted EbA portfolio, which 
in case of the Dominican Republic is expected to be destined to Small -scale EbA finance by 39% -  or with US$ 5.8 
million roughly a 15% of the current outstanding portfolio.  
88. El Salvador  
In El Salvador, at June 2020, a total agricultura l loan portfolio of US$ 46 million to 60,507 clients has been reported 
– representing the third lowest share of a total microfinance portfolio of US$325 million with 12%. Individual lending 
methodologies are used for 96% of the portfolio, with 2% following  group lending and community bank lending 
methodologies each for smaller loan amounts – probably in rural areas.  
The existing loan portfolio of US$ 46 million to 60,507 clients represents 3 times the targeted  EbA portfolio, which in case of El Salvador is  expected to be destined to Small -scale EbA finance by 54% - 
or with US$ 8.1 million 
roughly a 18% of the current outstanding portfolio.  
89. Guatemala  
In Guatemala, at June 2020, a total agricultural loan portfolio of US$ 40 million to 21,214 clients has been reported – representing the third highest share of a total microfinance portfolio of US$312 million with 15%. Individual lending 
methodologies are used for 96% of the portfolio, with 7% following group lending and community bank lending 
methodologies each for smaller loan amounts – probably in rural areas.  
The existing loan portfolio of US$ 40 million to 21,214 clients represents 2.7 times the targeted EbA portfolio, which 
in case of Guatemala is expected to be destined to Small -scale EbA finance by 52% -  or with US$ 7.9 million 
roughly a 20% of the current outstanding portfolio.  
90. Honduras  
In Honduras, at June 2020, a total agricultural loan portfolio of US$ 108 million to 75,176 clients has been reported 
– representing the third highest share of a total micr ofinance portfolio of US$467 million with 23%. Individual lending 
methodologies are used for 96% of the portfolio, with 3% and 1% following group lending and community bank 
lending methodologies respectively for smaller loan amounts –  probably in rural areas.

93. Comparing these numbers to the results of the financial model, the deployment of the lending facility at the 
national level could be summarized and compared to the numbers above:   
• In average each country could absorb (assuming equal distribution for purposes of discussion) around US$ 
14.7M in funding form the lending facility (US$ 60M from GCF and US$ 42.8M from CABEI)  
• By rotating this capital at the PFI  level, a total disbursement per country over the lifetime of the lending facility  
of US$ 51.5M via close to 9,800 loans could be reached.  
• This translates to an average disbursement of 980 loans per country, per year, or in average 82 loans per 
month (due to dynamics of building up the total facility, as well as winding her down, the pe ak is expected with 
roughly 1,780 loans in year 3, resulting in 148 loans per month).  
• Per country an expected 4 Partner Financial Institutions will gain access to the lending facility, disbursing around 
20 loans in average (in peak: 37) per month.  
 
94. Assuming this equal distribution, w ith exception of Panama, the programme countries will take up concessional 
EbA microfinance in the range of 7 –  22% of their current agricultural microfinance portfolios. Disbursed over a 
period of 4 years, and accompanied with increased awareness raising and capacity building acti vities, it can be 
expected that the markets in the region present a solid base for its absorption.  Further analysis of demand at 
the country and regional level is available in Annex 30.

97. Intervention strategy.  
The programme will be implemented following a sequence that allows for continuous feedback of results and observed 
impacts of the interventions into the enabling activities to strengthen capacity building, mainstreaming of EbA into


---
*Page 40*
---

G 
 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.2.1 | PAGE 38 OF 96 
   
 relevant policies and into the regional communication platform. The described sequence below is divided into 5 main 
phases, with a summarized  description of main activities implemented in each of them.  
 
Figure. Graphic representation of implementation str ategy  
98. 1/Set up and preparedness  
During this first stage of the programme, the site -specific implementation plans based on vulnerability and 
hydrological models will be strengthened and finalized in consultation with communities and key actors including 
detailed climate risk assessments.  These plans have been drafted for Costa Rica and Honduras (annex 31)  and 
will be strengthened and validated with communities during this initial phase of the programme and will be 
developed for all other 5 coutr ies during the first year following the process described in Annex 31.  
99. The specific EbA interventions funded by the grant component  will be based on the development of these site-
specific plans that will include both a scientific analysis of specific clim ate projections through the hydrological 
models, as well as sound stakeholder consultations that will allow to identify the most appropriate interventions. 
The validation of these indicative interventions will therefore be based on the following criteria:  
• - Relevance to site- specific climate risks  
• - Positive impact potential over climate risks  
• - Familiarity and acceptance by stakeholders  
• - Positive impact potential over most vulnerable sectors of the community  
• - Positive impact potential on gender balance  
• - Technical feasibility of the intervention  
• - Compliance with social en environmental safeguards  
• - Potential co- benefits of the interv ention  
• - Minimal level of risks and constraints for implementation  
• - Potential for self -sustainability  
• - Potential for sustained monitoring capacity  
 
This stage is critical for the engagement of actors and the Monitoring and Evaluation committees will be established 
within the participating communities. Technical protocols will be designed for the implementation of the large- scale 
EbA interventions that will serve as capacity building, demonstration and promotion sites.


---
*Page 41*
---

G 
 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.2.1 | PAGE 39 OF 96 
   
 In the process for the finalization of the site -specific EbA implementation plans, a selection process will be 
undertaken to select and engage the adequate financial institutions at the local level. Legal agreements will be 
established including the definition of specific eligibility criteri a and investment criteria of financial mechanisms and 
trainings on adaptation finance will be undertaken. The lending and guarantee facilities will be set -up.  A schematic 
representation of this intervention phase is provided below :

interventions defined by communities and included in component 1 will be implemented.

alternative livelihoods and opportunities for initial funding through the financial mechanism bas ed on vulnerability 
assessments, hydrological modelling and to reinforce site specific plans.  
A grant facility will be set up to strengthen the EbA business ecosystem in the region including training of commercial 
and subsistence farmers on EbA financial products available. The eligibility criteria for the grant facility will be based on specific site priorities.  
At this initial stage, the focus  will be on  promoting the blended EbA lending facility with potential network institutions 
and non- network instit utions. MRV systems will be established and formalized with IFIs and PFIs.

102. 3/ Implementation  
This stage will focus on full implementation of the grant financed activities making emphasis on awareness raising 
and capacity building of vulnerable communities through demonstration of EbA interventions and benefits through 
pilot interventions.  
The grant facility will also be in full implementation at this stage and results from investments will serve as input for 
research project s and shared through the knowledge Hub. These results will also be shared with national 
governments at the regional level to inform National Adaptation Plans and Regional strategies . 
The deploy ment of the loan facility will follow a phased approach along the programme to ensure cohesive and 
substantive impact of the financed activities as well as proper vulnerability assessments and identification of 
priorities for other vulnerable regions withi n the Dry Corridor where the intervention could be scaled up. This phased 
approach will also allow for more time to strengthen capacities of the financial institutions and to promote the 
financial mechanism with communities and producers.

services and their contribution to human well -being. The produced results, tools and methodologies will be shared 
through the Regional knowledge Hub with government institutions across the region.

105. 5/ Replication upscaling and sustainability  
The last stage of the programme  will be key to ensure proper consolidation of technical capacities and sustainability 
of the implemented activities. The results gathered from research and through the Regional knowledge hub will be 
shared with government institutions and used to inform national and local policies and decision- making processes. 
Main actors from local and national governments will receive training on how to use that information and integrate 
EbA into policy frameworks.  
106. Results, tools and methodologies developed by the Regional knowledge Hub will also be shared with financial 
institutions across the region, thus consolidating their capacity on EbA financial products. The private sector will be 
further engaged and sensitiz ed to potential benefits of EbA interventions through technical assistance to 
demonstrate benefit to implement EbA for businesses with economic inputs from valuation of ecosystem services. 
This will contribute to leverage funding from financial institution s for the expansion of EbA financial products after 
the programme ’s completion . A schematic representation of this intervention phase is provided below.

107. Site-specific EbA plan development process  
The elaboration of this site -specific intervention plan will follow a four -step approach in the seven participating 
countries, as described in the schematic representation below. All participating countries have completed the initial 
steps, and the remaining steps will be concluded during the first year of programme implementation (activity 1.1).


---
*Page 45*
---

G 
 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.2.1 | PAGE 43 OF 96 
   
   
Figure. Steps for the elaboration of site- specific intervention plan  
 
The following figure shows the linkages of the programme components to the site specific EbA  plan that will be 
developed during the first phase of the proposal : 
 
 
108. Draft sample EbA site- specific plans have been developed for Honduras and Costa Rica and will be 
validated with key actors in the initial satges of implementation. The other 5 countries will follow the described 
process below for the completion of their specific plans. This process and the sample plans for Costa Rica and 
Honduras are further described in Annex 31.  
109. Step 1. Selection of the most vulnerable catchments within the Dry Corridor (already done for the 7 
countries)  
Step 1.1 National identification of up to four prioritized catchment areas within the Dry Corridor : The selection of up 
to four catchments areas of interest per country at the Dry Corridor and the arid zones of the Dominican Republic 
was done through workshops held with government representatives.  
Step 1.2 Regional clim ate change vulnerability assessment : For each of the prioritized catchment areas, 
vulnerability assessments were developed. Variables used for exposure, sensitivity and adaptive capacity included 
access to credit and irrigation, agricultural employment, am ong others.


---
*Page 46*
---

G 
 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.2.1 | PAGE 44 OF 96 
   
 Step 1.3 Workshops and consultations for final catchment selection : Governments selected 5 -6 bordering 
municipalities within one of the prioritized catchment areas, based on their vulnerability to climate change and other 
criteria.  
 
110. Step 2 Loc al consultations to characterize climate risks and EbA opportunities (already done for the 7 
countries. To be reinforced during the first year of programme implementation)  
Step 2.1 Assessment on territory and identification of key actors : A characterization of the territory and key actors 
was done. Variables assessed included: demographics, indigenous communities, main regulatory and legal 
framework, livelihoods, and financial mechanisms available.  
Step 2.2 Analysis of main ecosystems, c limate risks and perceived climate impacts : The consultation methodology 
included a land use classification by participants, in which they analyzed main ecosystem’s benefits/use, climate 
risks, perceived impacts, among other, in a sex -disaggregated manner.   
Step 2.3 Participatory identification of adaptation options : Consultation participants proposed adaptation measures 
based on the potential impact on the identified climate risks, ecosystems, livelihoods, and specific needs.  
Step 2.4 Perceived barriers and mitigation strategies during consultations : Participants identified main barriers they 
perceived could jeopardize the implementation of the proposed adaptation measures and the programme in 
general.  
Step 2.5 Alignment with demand analysis from microfinance institutions : Financial institutions in the area were 
interviewed about their existing portfolios and demand. Climate risks identified during the consultation were 
compared and aligned with those identified by these institutions.  
 
111. Step 3 Climate impact modelling (done for Costa Rica, Honduras and Dominican Republic. To be 
developed for the remaining countries during the first year of programme implementation and validated for the 
three initial countries)  
Step 3.1 Selection of a sub-basin for the modelling purpose: The Soil Water Assessment Tool (SWAT) is used for 
the hydrological modelling of climate change potential impacts and impacts of EbA interventions. Water catchment 
areas are characterized through land use cover, digital elevation model and soil c haracterization. Based on 
topography, the basin is divided into sub- basins, and further divided into Hydrologic Response Units (HRU) based 
on unique combinations of slope, soil, and land cover. SWAT input data are precipitation, temperature, digital model 
elevation (DEM), soil cover, land use, and drainage network.  
Step 3.2 Identification of the main climate risks : The scenarios considered for modelling climate change impacts 
include a baseline, RCP 4.5 2050, RCP 4.5 2100, RCP 8.5 2050, and RCP 8.5 2100. The hydrological variables to 
estimate climate change impacts include soil water, percolation, water yield and erosion.  
Step 3.3 Impact modelling for main EbA interventions : A modelling scenario is  generated to analyse the potential 
impact of EbA int erventions individually or in combination under the RCP 4.5 climate change scenario. Selected 
interventions included those prioritized through the local consultations and a matrix was generated for the evaluation of impacts for different levels of intervention intensity.  
Step 3.4 Conclusions of the climate impact modelling: Results from the hydrological model were discussed through 
the analysis of changes in the hydrological variables for the Climate change RCP 4.5 scenarios with and without 
EbA interventi ons. 
 
112. Step 4 Site intervention strategy (Pre- drafted for Honduras and Costa Rica. To be developed in 
consultation with key actors for all remaining countries during the first year of programme implementation)  
The site intervention strategy will include the following components:  
Definition of the problem statement : Based on the results from the community consultations and the climate impact 
modelling, the problem statement can be defined identifying the main climate risks and barriers for adaptation to 
clima te change.  
Intervention objective: The specific objective of the programme is defined with a clear identification of the expected 
results in terms of EbA interventions, enabling conditions and objective population.


---
*Page 47*
---

G 
 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.2.1 | PAGE 45 OF 96 
   
 Intervention structure : The intervention logic describes the components that will shape the programme , their 
contribution and their interconnection towards the main objective.  
Grant -financed EbA interventions  to address prioritised climate risks in the Tempisque basin (Output 1.2): A number 
of Eb A interventions will be financed through a grant mechanism as demonstration pilots for the target territory. 
This section describes what EbA interventions have been prioritized for the specific climate impacts.  
Grant facility established to promote an EbA  business ecosystem  (Output 1.3): The establishment of a Grant facility 
will then finance a series of EbA intervention, supporting business and distribution models through a competitive 
bottom- up approach. A number of criteria is raised to identify potenti al projects and beneficiaries at the community 
level, and this facility is also expected to leverage demand for EbA loans.  
Loan and guarantee facility to accelerate access to finance for other relevant EbA interventions for the Tempisque 
basin (Output 2.2) : An EbA Lending Facility will be set at below -market conditions to enable financing for small - 
and large- scale EbA investments at farm- , enterprise-  and household- level. A number of criteria for the selection of 
PFIs and of possible beneficiaries and EbA interventions to be financed is also predefined.  
Building capacities of key actors  (Output 1 and 3): To ensure sustainability and as a complementary activity of the 
Grant and Lending facility, trainings will be organized at the local and national level, based on identified needs with 
targeted stakeholders.  
Mainstreaming EbA into key policy and planning frameworks  (output 1 and 3): Technical assistance will be provided 
to strengthen the capacity of policy makers and decision makers and identify entry points of the integration of EbA 
in policy and regulatory frameworks at the local, national and regional level.  
Monitoring and evaluation framework : The implementation strategy of an M&E framework and a proposed set of 
indicators are included in the intervention plan to support the communities in the established of an M&E framework during the first year of the programme.  
 
113. * Validation of Site- specific EbA plans  
A final step for validation of the site plans has been added for the pre- defined site plans in Costa Ri ca and Honduras 
where the initial year of implementation will be focused on strengthening the hydrological model and validating and 
confirming the specific sites for the grant interventions and the refinement of the elegibilty criteria for the grant and 
loan component in consultations with communities, experts and the financial institutions.  
 
 
B.4. Implementation arrangements  
114. The proposed programme  will be implemented over a period of seven years, from 202 1–2028. The 
Central American Bank for Economic Integration (CABEI) will be the Accredited Entity (AE) for the programme, and will be responsible for overseeing the implementation, financial managem
ent, evaluation, reporting and 
closure of the programme. GCF and CABEI will enter into a single FAA in the form of a trust agreement . 
 Accredited Entity  
115. As the AE, CABEI will oversee programme implementation, using its experience having successfully 
implemented similar programme  activities involving financial intermediation across the region. Over the past 56 
years, CABEI's  support to the region has resulted in project approvals amounting to more than US$ 30.9 billion 
and disbursements of more than US$ 26.2 billion. CABEI will be responsible for financial management and will 
be accountable for the use of GCF resources under the programme . It will maintain programme accounts, 
facilitate staff recruitment and procurement processes and will monitor resource mobilisation of baseline as well 
as co -finance. Financial transactions will be subject to annual audits undertaken by internationally -certified 
auditors. In addition, as the AE, CABEI will: i) ensure that the programme is executed in accordance with GCF 
standards; ii) supervise, oversee and manage the implementation of programme interventions; iii) report on 
programme progress; and iv) ensure that programme activities are well coordinated and aligned with countries' 
national priorities. To ensure adequate oversight of the programme, CABEI will use its robust fiduciary, technical 
oversight and quality assurance systems such as its Evaluation Office, its Project Formulation Department 
(FOPROY) and its Financing for the Majorities Unit (FINAM) to closely support the Executing Entity (EE) and 
the Regional Project Management Unit (RPMU). Programme  implementation by CABEI will also take place in 
close coordination with a Regional Project Steering Committee (RPSC).


---
*Page 48*
---

G 
 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.2.1 | PAGE 46 OF 96 
   
 Executing Entity  
116. The Food and agriculture organization of the U nited Nations in collaboration with the Central American 
Commission for Environment and Development (CCAD) will serve as one of the Executing Entit ies for the 
programme  through a Subsidiary agreement signed with CABEI . FAO will oversee the execution of the 
programme at the regional level, hosting the RPMU. At the national level, FAO will guide the day -to-day 
operations of the programme through National Project Execution Units (PEUs) based in each target catchment, 
as well as through national executing partners. FAO, through the PEUs, will also provide the technical execution 
and manage relations with stakeholders and institutions in the respective participating countries. CCAD will 
also sign a Subsidiary agreement with CABEI for the execution of  output 3 ensuring the  regional aggregation 
and dissemination of information to all countries in the region to strengthen t he scientific information of the 
decision making processes at the regional and national levels.  
117. The CCAD is the environmental division of the Central Ameri can Integration Secretariat (SICA) and 
has independent legal personality exercised by the Pro Tempore Presidency ; the highest authority in the CCAD 
is the Council of Ministers, which is composed of representatives of the environmental authorities of each 
Member State.  For administrative purposes, all contracts and operational agreements signed by CCAD are 
run through the Financial Administrative Directorate (DAF) of the General Secretariat of SICA (SG -SICA). SG -
SICA provides accounting, purchasing and cont racting services, and CCAD follows SG -SICA’s regulations on 
procurement and HR management.  The CCAD’s  mandate in the region includes: i) strengthening of national 
authorities and regulations; ii) harmonisation of policies and legislation; iii) distribution of information; iv) 
determining priority action areas; and v) promoting a participatory, democratic and decentralised environmental 
management. The CCAD is well -placed to execute this output of the  programme as a result of its strong 
institutional presenc e and its direct link s to each participating country . A detailed capacity assessment 
undertaken for the CCAD has confirmed their capacity to act  as the EE for this output of the programme and 
can be found in annex 29. The CCAD is the coordinating body for Central America in terms of environmental 
management and climate change strategy  and they have extensive experience executing projects  with a wide 
range of donors , including  the GEF, the European Union, the World Bank  and the Interamerican Development . 
They also have  the capacity to engage with governments  at the highest level as they are the convening 
organisation for the environmental ministries in the region,  including all countries participating in the 
programme.  
118. The EEs will be accountable to CABEI as the AE for the effective execution of programme interventions. 
FAO, through the RPMU, will be responsible for ensuring technical and operational cohesiveness at the regional 
level and will report to the RPSC on the progress of the programme at both national and regional levels. All 
operating policies and procedures will follow CABEI's standards of operation. The EE s will work in close 
coordination with the RPSC and RPMU (described below).  
Track record of Executing Ent ities 
119. The FAO is an international organisation with the goals of eradicating food insecurity and malnutrition; 
eliminating poverty; and promoting sustainable management and utilisation of natural resources. The FAO supports 
countries in both mitigating and adapting to climate change through a range of programmes and projects  based on 
research and best practices, as part of the 2030 Agenda and the Sustainable Development Goals. With national 
offices in seven countries of the regional programme and SICA region, the FAO maintains a close relationship with 
the Ministries of Agriculture and the Ministries of the Environment for implementation related to the Programming 
Frameworks set with countries. Technical assistance is provided to Executive Secretaries of bot h councils of 
ministries for regional implementation arrangements. Since October 2016, the FAO has been accredited by the 
GCF as an International Organisation for the category of medium projects (up to US$250 million). It currently has a 
portfolio of US$79 3.4 million at a global level, of which approximately US$110.1 million is being executed by the 
FAO´s Office for Mesoamerica. In addition, the FAO has made important efforts with countries to facilitate access 
to environmental financing with the Global Env ironment Facility (GEF). Projects are implemented both nationally 
and locally, with experience with local governments, farmers’ and women’s organisations, and using a multicultural 
approach.  
120. The FAO promotes climate -resilient agriculture as a way to increa se productivity, build the resilience 
of food systems and, wherever possible, reduce GHG emissions. Regionally -relevant examples of this work 
include:  
• GCF FP145 REsilient LIVElihoods  (RELIVE) of vulnerable smallholder farmers in the Mayan landscapes and 
the Dry Corridor of Guatemala, aimed to bolster the resilience of the most vulnerable farmers and indigenous 
groups (US$66.7 million);  
• GCF FP089 Upscaling climate resilience measures in the dry corridor agroecosystems of El Salvador 
(RECLIMA). This programme  seeks to restore and reforest degraded ecosystems to protect water sources and


---
*Page 49*
---

G 
 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.2.1 | PAGE 47 OF 96 
   
 stimulate aquifer recharge through adaptive agroecosystem management, simultaneously improving the 
resilience of small -scale farmers to climate change impacts (US$127.7 million); and  
• the FAO project ‘Climate Smart Agroforestry Systems for the Dry Corridor of Central America’ which supports 
farmers in Guatemala and Honduras to increase the uptake of clima te resilient agroforestry systems through 
the farmer field school approach.  
121. Other experiences the FAO has working in the region include:  
• the ‘Resilient landscape management project’ (US$4.3 million) and ‘Strengthening the resilience of multiple-
use protected areas to deliver multiple global environmental benefits’ (US$5.8 million) in Nicaragua;  
• the ‘Promoting climate- smart livestock management’ project in the Dominican Republic (US$1.5 million);   
• the ‘Protecting biodiversity and recovering degraded ecos ystems’ project  (US$1.8 million) in Honduras;  
• the ‘Rural Development with local governments’ project (US$1.5 million) and the ‘Adaptation of vulnerability 
communities to climate change’ project (US$5 million) in Guatemala; and  
• the ‘Enable the concerted management of FUENTE AL MAR in the Paz river basin’ project (US$1.6 million) and 
the ‘Systematization of agricultural good practices for adaptation and climate change’ project (US$400 million) 
in Guatemala and El Salvador.  
The FAO also supports the implementation of the Forest and Farm Facility, which strengthens and empowers Forest 
and Farm Producers Organizations (FFPOs), including women, youth and indigenous peoples as primary change 
agents for climate- resilient landscapes and improved livelihoods (Guat emala, Nicaragua and Panama in Phase I).  
122. The CCAD was established with the mission of developing a regional regime for environmental cooperation 
and integration that contributes to improving the quality of life of the populations of its Member States. The CCAD 
was appointed by the Tulum Declarati on and ratified in Tulum +8 as the leader of regional efforts for the 
conservation and management of the Mesoamerican Region (MAR) as a shared transboundary ecoregion. The 
CCAD hosts the Regional Environmental Observatory (REO), a regional digital repository of environmentally -
relevant information. Its actions have responded to the Environmental Plans of the Central American Region 
(PARCA).  
123. The CCAD served as the executing agency for the ‘Integrated Ridge- to-Reef Management of the 
Mesoamerican Reef’ projec t in Belize, Guatemala, Honduras and Mexico (US$9 million). It also served as an 
executing partner for:  
• the ‘Increasing Capacity for the Reduction of Flood and Drought Disaster Risks and Promotion of Resilience in Central America’ project carried out in B
elize, Guatemala, El Salvador, Honduras, Nicaragua, Costa Rica and 
Panama (US$2 million); and  
• the ‘Regional Program for the Reduction of Environmental Depletion and Vulnerability’ (PREVDA) in Belize, 
Guatemala, El Salvador, Honduras, Nicaragua, Costa Rica , Panama and the Dominican Republic (US$20 
million).  
The CCAD also provided: i) technical support to the GEF project ‘Strengthening of environmental integration for the establishment of the Environmental Plan for the Central American Region’ (PARCA II) (U S$4 million) to strengthen 
the capacities of indigenous communities to protect and manage their natural and cultural resources; and ii) financial 
cooperation for the ‘Consolidation of the Corazon Transboundary Biosphere Reserve’ (CTBR) project (US$12 
million) for strengthening of the national system of protected areas in Nicaragua and Honduras.  
Technical Advisor

where UNEP will be the s ervice provider  for a set of defined goals and activities in accordance to the approved 
programme ’s proposal.  UNEP will be accountable to CCAD for the use of funds and implementation of agreed
