126. The RPSC will comprise representatives from inter alia : i) FAO and CCAD  as the EE s; ii) CABEI  as the 
AE and EE ; iii) United Nations Environment Programme (UNEP) –  in a t echnical advisory role; iv) national -level 
CCAD liaisons; and v) the Regional Project Manager. The RPSC will primarily be responsible for providing 
technical, oversight and advisory support at the regional level. This will include: i) overseeing programme 
implementation; ii)  review and validation annual regional workplans and programme reports; iii) review and 
validation of the programme's fulfilment of environmental and financial objectives; and iv) approval of changes 
to the programme's targets, activities or timelines.  In addition, the RPSC will be informed about  the final 
selection of Partner Financial Institutions (PFIs) that will be done by CABEI following their internal regulations 
and criteria. The RPSC will also make a recommendation on the final selection of recipien ts of grants through 
the Grant facility  (these selection processes are described further below). The RPSC will be centrally placed to 
ensure consistent programme progress across  all seven countries. Biannual RPSC meetings will be held77 to 
take management -related and technical decisions, discuss the programme's main performance indicators and 
provide strategic guidance.  The RPSC will also be responsible for overseeing the RPMU.  In accordance with 
the AMA the Executing Entities will retain discretion over the use of proceeds and Programme implementation. 
EEs will have the final decision or a veto right over any recommendation or action taken by the RPSC.  
 Regional Project Management Unit  
127. The RPMU will be based at FAO’s  regional headquarters in Panama  and will be responsible for day -
to-day programme administration and execution, and the regional technical coordination of programme 
activities. It will ensure that programme execution p roceeds through clear work plans, terms of reference and 
carefully designed administrative arrangements that meet CABEI and GCF requirements. A full -time staff 
member will be recruited to fulfil the role of Project Manager (PM), who will head the RPMU and will be 
responsible for the daily on -the-ground implementation and management of the programme . The PM will be 
responsible for ensuring the programme achieves the targets set out in the Results Framework (Section E) to 
the required standards of CABEI and t he GCF and within the specified time and budget allocations. To achieve 
these targets, the PM will: i) report directly to the RPSC on programme management -related matters; ii) manage 
the programme in accordance with the specified workplans and allocated budget; iii) ensure that all programme  
interventions are implemented according to CABEI and GCF guidelines; iii) work closely with PEUs and national institutions and authorities to ensure that the programme 
is managed effectively and that the needs of all 
beneficiary groups are considered; and iv) oversee the efficient and effective information and knowledge 
transfer to relevant programme partners. The PM will be required to fulfil these roles for the duration of the 
programme.  
 
128. In addition to the PM, the RPMU will be comprised of the following individuals related to the regional 
management of the programme:  
• Financial and Procurement Officer;  
• Administrative Officer ; and  
• Accountant.  
 
129. Regional technical specialists on the RPMU will include a: i) Chief Technical Adviser; ii) Forestry Expert; 
and iii) Water, EbA and Resource Efficiency Expert. There will also be a Regional Environmental and Social 
Safeguards Officer, a Regional Gender Officer, a Regional Monitoring and Evaluation Specialist; and a 
Regional Communications Specialist represented on the RPMU.   A Regional Knowledge Hub Coordinator will 
be based in the CCAD Headquarters in El Salvador to ensure full integration of the programme results into the 
Regional Environmental Observatory.  
  
National Advisory Committees  
130. An NAC will be established in each of the seven participating countries. Each NAC will be comprised 
of a: i) NAC National Coordinator; ; i i) national climate change office/department representative; iii) Ministry of 
Environment r epresentative; iv) Ministry of Agriculture representative; v) Ministry of Planning representative; 
and vi) representative from Local Government. The RPMU will strongly advise governments to nominate local 
representatives for the slots allocated for ministr ies in the NAC. The responsibilities of the NACs will include 
national programme guidance, planning and oversight, as well as supervision of national financial execution. 
The NACs will work closely with CABEI  and the RPMU to coordinate activities under Output  4 of the programme. 
They will also participate in fostering national access to the three financial facilities  established by the

 programme . NACs will receive project proposals to the EbA Grant Facility during bi -annual project selection 
rounds and will coordinate with national ministries to ensure alignment with national policies, adaptation plans 
and, where applicable,  respective regulations and laws. They will also, in coordination with the National Proj ect 
Execution Units (PEUs), prioritise a selected number of projects in order to act as a first filter, before passing 
such proposals to the RPMU.  
 
National Project Execution Units  
131. National Project Execution Units (PEUs) will be established in the target catchments of each of the 
participating countries. Each unit will be comprised of: i) a National Project Execution Unit Coordinator (technical 
lead); ii) two  EbA, water and resource technical advisor s; iii) a Financial Officer to provide guidance on the three 
financial facilities ; iv) an Administrative Officer; v) a Monitoring and Evaluation (M&E) Specialist; vi) a 
Communications Specialist; and vii) two researchers (one junior and one senior) to build country -specific 
evidence bases of the adaptation activities for the regional knowledge hub. PEUs will be responsible for daily 
execution of the programme in the respective target catchments, as well as communication and capacity -
buildin g activities with participating local governments, financial institutions, civil society organisations, farmers 
and communities. They will provide monitoring, reporting and evaluation services, as well as technical 
assistance and execution services. The Eb A, water and resource experts will provide technical assistance to 
local governments, farmers and communities on implementing EbA, water - and resource- efficient  interventions 
under the programme. The financial advisors will provide technical assistance to local financial institutions to 
support EbA, to learn how to use the financial mechanisms established under the programme , and for the 
valuation of natural assets. The PEUs will also be responsible for the identification of eligible projects for grant 
financing through the Grant facility . They will engage with local stakeholders at community, association, 
cooperative and municipal levels to formulate finance applications to be submitted to the NACs, which will  
subsequently  make national selections and pass their recommendations to the RPMU. The M&E Specialists 
will define and implement the monitoring plan for the programme , and will collect the data required at national 
levels to track and measure the targets and indicators defined in the programme' s results framework  
 
Intermediary Financial Institutions .  
132. CABEI network IFIs will be the main counterparts to channel GCF funds  and CABEI Co -financing  from 
the Lending Facility towards end beneficiaries. The IFIs will receive the funding for the Lending Fac ility from 
CABEI via two funding streams: via on- lending (full recourse) for co- financing from CABEI, and via limited 
recourse loan for GCF funds: both funding stream will be blended at IFI level and then on- lend to Partner 
Financial Institutions (PFIS) that will further on- lend to end beneficiaries . The IFIs are also the key partner for 
further leveraging of EbA finance via the intended Guarantee facility (see paragraphs 53 and 54).  
 Partner Financial Institutions  
133. PFIs in each country will be identified  through a process following  CABEI´s and I ntermediary Financial 
Institutions internal rules and regulations , and through eligibility criteria that CABEI  will approve via a no-
objection.  As its sole Executing Entity, CABEI will have final decision on the selection of each IFI, PFIs and end 
borrower to be financed under the Lending Facility. The final selectio n will be communicated to the  RPSC . If 
there were a case in which there is no CABE I-accredited institution within the target catchment area, accredited 
institutions will be provided with technical assistance to offer an institutional loan to another non- accredited 
financial institution that can then directly lend to the end beneficiary.  The PFIs will work closely with the PEUs 
to ensure interventions financed directly by the programme , and using the mechanisms established by the 
programme, are well -aligned with programme objectives. The functioning of the financial facilities  and all 
institutions involved is further described below. Using the internal structures of CABEI, PFI´s will be monitored 
through audits, analyses of relevance, sustainability, and cost -effectiveness of all credit lines, thereby ensuring 
they are framed within the specific objectives of the programme.  
PFIs will be selected on the basis of  the following criteria (to refined and confirmed by CABEI during inception of the 
programme). The criteria will apply throughout the period in which the PFIs participate in the programme . 
 PFIs must be duly licensed in the respective countries and have at least three years of operation.  
 The PFIs must be and remain in compliance with applicable laws and regulations issued by the local authorities as certified by independent external auditors on an annual basis and by quarterly returns provided 
by PFIs to the local regulatory authorities  
 In case the initial eligibility of the PFIs falls to such a date that their year -end audits have been already 
completed and do not cover this requirement, then the PFIs would be required to submit a management letter 
in an ac ceptable format confirming their compliance with applicable laws and regulations issued by the 
authorities.


---
*Page 52*
---

G 
 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.2.1 | PAGE 50 OF 96 
   
  PFI’s board of directors and managers must be considered “fit and proper ” by IFI s. It must have qualified and 
experienced management, adequate organization and institutional capacity for its specific risk profile.  
 The PFI must have well defined policies and written procedures for management of all types of financial risks 
(i.e. credit, o perational, liquidity and market risks).  
 Operational self -sufficiency: The operational self -sufficiency of the PFI must be over 100% for the preceding 
financial year.  
 The PFI must have adequate liquidity (based on the acceptable prudential ratios dictated by the national 
financial regulatory body of each country).  
 The PFI must have positive profitability and acceptable risk profile (based on the acceptable prudential ratios 
dictated by the national financial regulatory body of each country).  
 The PFI must  classify its assets and off -balance- sheet credit risk exposures (at least four times per year) and 
make adequate provisions.  
 The PFI must have adequate portfolio quality, with PAR>=90 of at most 5% of total gross loans.  
 The PFI must have adequate internal audits and controls for its specific risk profile.  
 The PFI must have adequate management information systems, allowing it to separately and individually track all loans made with the EbA facility funds.  
 The PFI must agree to engage in financing commer cial farms, households, agroindustry and other 
enterprises.  
 The PFI must agree to undergo intensive mandatory technical assistance  
Where PFIs are not complying with the above defined minimum requirements, these can adhere to the TA facility to 
establish respective institutional capacities.  
 
134. The objective of financial facilities  is to enable financing —  through both loans and grants —  of proven 
EbA solutions and resource-  and water -efficient technologies for vulnerable communities in the Dry Corridor 
and Arid Zones. Eligibility criteria for different activities of the three financial facilities are defined in the 
respective sections . 
 
135. Further information regarding the operation of the financial facilities  is presented in Annex 23: 
Operational Guidelines.

As observed:  
• CABEI will act as trustee for GCF funds for the overall Programme  
• For the Lending facility CABEI will 1) channel GCF funds and provided limited recourse loans to the selected 
IFIs, each 1 per country and 2) on- lend its co -financing funds to these same IFIs .  the IFIs will each select  up 
to 4 PFIs to on- lend the blended f unds (GCF  and CABEI co- financing funds) in a two- staged approach: i) a 
first eligibility check based on a simplified format; and ii) a final selection and lending volume decision based on a fully -fledged due diligence process, including an initial gap analysis of PFI capacity for EbA lending. End 
beneficiaries can apply for EbA loans directly with the respective PFIs.  
• The RPSC will be informed periodically about programme progress also through the selected PFIs in line with 
CABEI´s regulations, as well as for EbA, water - and resource- efficient  project s that can be financed under the 
financial facilities . The RPSC will also be provided with information on monitoring, reporting and verification 
(MRV) requirements to be complied with by PFIs and other programme  entities in line with CABEI´s internal 
regulations.  
• National PEUs will work with local stakeholders to identify eligible project s to be financed by grants through the 
grant facility . They will compile programme proposals and filter out those that do not fulfil minimum 
requirements; the remainder will be passed to the NACs.  
• NACs will undertake selection of eligible projects  in each country according to national priorities and will pass 
their selection to the RPMU.  
• The RPMU will undertake a selection of al l projects proposed by each NAC and will pass their selection to the 
RPSC.  
• The RPSC will make a preliminary  selection of eligible projects at the regional level and will issue the list of 
grant recipients and  final approval will be taken by CABEI.  
• For loa n applications, beneficiaries can apply for loans directly through PFIs.  .

137. The seven participating countries  are extremely vulnerable to climate change impacts and  have 
experienced severe  agricultural and economic  losses because of  progressively more frequent and intense

138. The COVID -19 pandemic is imposing additional  negative impacts  on healthcare systems and 
economies  globally, further reducing the capacity of already -vulnerable Dry Corridor and Arid Zone 
populations to respond to extreme climate events. With existing limit ed technical and financial capacities, 
these populations will require even greater relief interventions as their COVID -19 recovery efforts 
increase existing debt, while climate threats continue to build.   
 
139. To overcome observed and predicted climate threats  in the Dry Corridor and Arid Zones it is necessary 
to strengthen the technical and institutional capacity for EbA and to introduce novel financial mechanisms to 
fund adaptation. Currently, governments of the seven participating countries have insufficient  resources, 
knowledge and technical capacity to promote innovative natural resource- based adaptation interventions. The 
resources required to effect a paradigm shift in the region's approach to natural resource management exceed 
those that can be supplied through public sector financing. The economic situations of the participating 
countries in the Latin America and the Caribbean (LAC) region are outlined in Table 2 below.

140. Although countries in the region are classified from lower -middle to high income based on GDP per 
capita, their national budgets are constrained, with poverty eradication and sustainable development hav ing 
been prioritised for funding over CCA. For example, less than 3% of the national budget has been committed 
for mitigation, adaptation and risk management related to climate change in Guatemala, El Salvador, Ni caragua 
and Honduras in recent years.  
 
141. Across the LAC region,  there is a high dependency on non- reimbursable international support to meet 
CCA goals. Where the availability of international finance has been limited, countries have had to increase 
internati onal debt or claim loans in order to respond to climate change impacts. There is a considerable 
challenge to be met in that the finance available for CCA is far below what is required to meet adaptation needs. 
For example, a UNDP IFF study91 in 2011 estimat ed an increase in the annual cost of  adaptation in Costa Rica 
for the biodiversity sector  alone from US$60 million in 2015 to US$76 million by 203092. Simila rly, in the 
Dominican Republic, annual adaptation costs for the tourism sector are predicted to increase from US $16 
million in 2015 to US$57  million by 2030 . Aggregate estimates for the LAC region place the cost of adaptation 
to a 2.5°C rise in temperature at 1.5– 5.0% of the region's GDP. The total annual cost for adaptation across all 
sectors in the LAC region is predicted to increase under both wet and dry climate scenarios up to 2050 (Figure 
12). Safeguarding water supplies, implementing flood protection measures and effective management of 
coastal zones will comprise the bulk of this cost93.

142. Given this constrained fiscal space, there are limited opportunities to implement climate change 
adap tation interventions in the region without additional assistance. The seven target countries have already 
extended their largest possible contributions to finance adaptation and further support is needed to fully address 
the climate problem. While scope ha s been identified for partial loan financing at the regional level, the extent 
of public debt in the individual countries, as well as their sovereign credit ratings (most graded as speculative 
with significant risk), constrain their borrowing capacities wi th regards to global recommended borrowing limits. 
As a result, the countries are unable to take on more sovereign debt to finance adaptation directly, instead 
relying on alternative financial mechanisms. In addition, and at a local scale, the most vulnera ble farmers are 
often considered to be too high a risk for loans because of their small farm sizes and limited collateral which is 
unfavourably viewed by banks using conservative practices. This limits these individuals'  access to financial 
resources needed for climate change adaptation.  
 
143. To overcome these constraints, the proposed programme is requesting loan finance to establish a 
credit line for EbA in the region, with additional guarantees to de- risk investments and support uptake of EbA 
financing by s mall holder and commercial farmers. In addition to the loan components, GCF grant financing is 
requested to establish and administer the credit line and to provide technical assistance for the implementation of EbA. Grant finance is also required to establ ish a trust fund by CABEI that will support bottom- up 
implementation of EbA in the most vulnerable communities as well as to support direct implementation of 
climate change adaptation interventions in the most vulnerable communities that are not in a posit ion to access 
loan finance.  
B.6. Exit strategy and sustainability  
144. The sustainability and replicability of the proposed programme  interventions will be ensured through 
six elements in the programme design, namely: i ) operations and maintenance; ii) building long- term community 
ownership; iii) integrating EbA into catchment management and planning; iv) incentivising private sector 
investments into EbA; v) developing financial instruments for EbA; and vi) monitoring an d evaluation. The 
details of each of these elements are described below.  
 
145. Post -programme  operations and maintenance . Relevant operations and maintenance (O&M)  costs  
for programme interventions  during and post programme implementation  are detailed in Annex  21: Operations 
Manual.  Programme stakeholders are committed to undertaking O&M not only during the programme,  but also 
after programme completion.  For example, the regional knowledge hub that will be established under the 
programme will be maintained through the integration and linking of its content with the existing  Regional  
Environmental Observatory  (OAR) managed by the CCAD. Because the knowledge hub will be web- based and 
linked to the OAR, it will not require physical maintenance post programme  closure. However, the technical


---
*Page 57*
---

G 
 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.2.1 | PAGE 55 OF 96 
   
 specialists engaged to maintain the OAR will ensure the sustainability of the hub and the updating of its 
information b eyond the programme implementation period. I n addition, the knowledge hub will benefit from 
other centralised information generation projects at the OAR, such as GIZ and NASA programmes, ensuring 
ongoing consolidated outputs.  Other aspects of the programme requiring O&M include financial products an d 
services, EbA interventions , and water - and resource- efficient  technologies.  These are described in detail in 
the O&M plan (Annex 21) .  
 
146. Building long -term community ownership . By engaging closely with target communities — 
particularly environmental and women's organisations — throughout the programme design and 
implementation process, the programme will instil a sense of ownership in these communities, which will 
contribute to gender equality and the long- term sustainability of programme interventions af ter programme  
closure. Sub- activities 1.2.6 and 3.1.4 specifically target local cooperatives and women's organisations, 
respectively, by: i) establishing a training of trainers'  programme for the implementation of EbA and water -
efficient technologies; and ii) strengthening the technical capacity of community organisations, including 
women's groups, in revising and disseminating information products to support the adoption of EbA and other 
climate -resilient practices. Community involvement in programme interventions is also particularly important in 
Sub-activity 1.1.1 — establishing a community -level monitoring and evaluation committee in each municipality 
— as well as in Sub- activity 1.3.3 — establishing livelihood training projects in each municipality for farmers 
and rural communities on locally -appropriate natural resource- based businesses and climate- resilient 
livelihoods. Communities will further be closely involved in the co- devel opment of all site- specific intervention 
plans to integrate EbA measures into the seven target catchments (Activity 1 .1). 
 
147. Integrating EbA into catchment management and planning . Training, monitoring, evaluation and 
the curation of lessons learned and best  practices from programme activities will provide national - and local -
level decision- makers with relevant knowledge and information to adopt climate- resilient policies and incentives 
in the long- term. Output 3 is specifically designed to ensure the information generated through programme  
activities is disseminated across the region to increase the capacity of institutions to implement EbA 
interventions, building a strong knowledge- sharing platform. Activity 3.3 will particularly enhance capacity of 
local-level policy makers to integrate climate change adaptation and the valuation of natural capital into local 
policies, which will enhance the sustainability of proposed interventions beyond the programme's lifespan.  
 
148. Integrating EbA into local and national pol icies and regional strategies. The programme will 
support the integration of EbA into local policies focusing mainly on land use management plans, water 
management plans and water management organizations mainly through technical assistance and trainings 
under components 1 and 3. The programme  will also provide inputs directly to the NAP processes currently 
under development for most countries in the region and focusing on the sectoral policies for agriculture, water 
security and ecosystems and forestry which have been prioritized by countries in their NAP processes. At the 
regional level, the results from the programme gathered in the Regional Observatory will serve as a direct input 
for the update and strengthening of the regional strategies for Environment, Climate Change, Water Resources 
and Agriculture all under the SICA mandate.   
 
149. Incentivising private sector investments into EbA.  The programme will leverage private sector 
investments into EbA through capacity -building and awareness -raising interventions targeting the private 
sector. Activi ties under  Output 3 will build the private sector 's awareness of the potential benefits of EbA, while 
Output 1 and 4  will provide f inancial products and technical assistance for businesses to implement EbA. For 
example, training commercial farmers on susta inable EbA practices ( Sub- activity 1.2.2) will demonstrate the 
benefits of EbA for ecosystem- based businesses and facilitate private sector investments in these practices as 
well as in restoration/conservation of ecosystems on which these businesses rely i n the long- term. The financial 
instruments established under Output 4 will further support these farmers to access finance for such 
investments.  By creating a framework for the valuation of ecosystem services, the programme will further 
facilitate the quantification of long- term economic benefits of large- scale EbA interventions and contribute to 
the long- term incentive of investing into EbA in th e region.   
 
150. Developing financial instruments for EbA .  The provision of financial mechanisms under Output 4 
of the programme  for the implementation of EbA and water -efficient technologies, and the comprehensive 
monitoring of the benefits of these systems, will ultimately result in end beneficiaries who are implementing 
EbA and other adaptation measures displaying a lower risk p rofile than individuals who are not. The programme

151. Monitoring and evaluation . Lessons learned from programme activitie s will be collated by an M&E 
Specialist in the Regional Project Management Unit  and made available to development planners through a 
regional knowledge hub. In addition, the programme will establish a community -level monitoring and evaluation 
committee in each municipality and build the capacity of these committees to monitor the biophysical, social 
and economic conditions in their local catchment. This capacity building will be complemented by assessments 
of the level of climate vulnerability, risks and opportunities (Sub- activity 1.1.1). Lessons learned from the M&E 
will be made available to community -level monitoring and evaluation committees to improve the sustainability 
of the interventions while the committees' local knowledge will, in turn, inform the M&E. In addition, monitoring, 
reporting and verification (MRV) systems established under Activity 4.3will enable PFIs to monitor and assess 
the implementation of EbA and resource-  and water -efficient investment projects , as well as to monitor and 
document their  respective economic and ecosystem benefits , thereby  encouraging and facilitating further 
uptake across  the region.

C.3 Capacity building and technology development/transfer  
C.3.1 Does GCF funding finance capacity building activities?  Yes ☒      No ☐ 
C.3.2. Does GCF funding finance technology development/transfer?  Yes ☒      No ☒ 
 
152. Capacity building will be provided through several programme activities. Activity 4.3 will provide technical 
assistance (TA) to strengthen technical capacity of CABEI network as well as non- regulated financial institutions 
(partner financial institutions) to access and channel funds for small - and large- scale EbA investments. Under 
this activity, selected partner financial institutions will receive capacity building services for the adequate 
promotion and managing of EbA finance to end beneficiaries, such as data management for the measurement, 
monitoring, reporting and verification (MRV) of climate risks and adaptation strategy impacts or effects, the 
implementation of EbA -oriented awareness raising and marketing campaigns with end beneficiaries and the 
development of financial and non- financial products and services geared towards EbA investments. Where 
applicable, the programme will support the integration of external data management solutions to enhance EbA -
oriented MRV systems with existing IT infrastruc ture such as core banking systems. Activity 1.2 will train 
commercial farmers on sustainable EbA practices, including silvopasture , agroforestry and sustainable land 
management (SLM) (US$73,500 under sub- activity 1.2.2) as well as local communities on EbA and water -
efficient technologies, including visits to demonstration sites to facilitate farmer to farmer knowledge exchange 
(US$14 7,000 under sub- activity 1.2.7). Local communities will also be trained on locally appropriate natural 
resource- based businesses and climate- resilient livelihoods (Sub- activity 1.3.3; US$276,500).  
153. Policy - and decision- makers at the municipal level will be trained on climate change impacts, including the 
impacts of gender inequality, and the role of EbA in mitigating climate change impacts to support political and technical decision- making for climate resilience in the Dry Corridor and Arid Zones (Sub- activ
ity 3.1.1; 
US$147,000). This will be supported by building the capacity of these institutions to use the protocols and 
criteria for the adoption of EbA and implementation of economic incentives for SLM (Sub- activity 3.3.3; 
US$275,250). Finally, the technic al capacity of community organisations, including women's groups, and local 
champions will be strengthened for developing, revising and disseminating information products to support the 
adoption of EbA and other resilient practices (Sub- activity 3.1.4: US$ 147,000).  
154. Technology transfer will be conducted through Activity 3.1, where a regional knowledge management hub 
will be established (US$247,500). Output 2 will also include on -the-ground implementation of EbA activities as 
well as water - and resource  efficient technologies (US$ $26, 106,263).  
 EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA  
D.1. Impact potential  
 
155. The proposed programme will contribute to the achievement of GCF's fund- level adaptation objective 
of increased climate- resilient sustainable development by facilitating w ide-scale investment in EbA and water -
efficient technologies across the Dry Corridor of Central America and Arid Zones of the Dominican Republi c. In 
achieving this obj ective, the programme will contribute to several indicative assessment factors in the GCF 
Performance Measurement Framework to ensure that GCF funds achieve the desired impact in the region. This 
will include contributions to fund -level impa cts A1.0 — increased resilience and enhanced livelihoods of the


---
*Page 61*
---

G 
 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.2.1 | PAGE 59 OF 96 
   
 most vulnerable people, communities and regions and  A4.0 — increased resilience of ecosystems and 
ecosystem services.  
 
156. In addition to the fund- level impact s, the programme will contribute to three fund- level outcomes. The 
specific contribution to each outcome is presented below. Additional details  of how the programme interventions 
interconnect to achieve these fund- level impacts and outcomes are presented in Section B.2. — Theory of 
Change.  
 
157. A5.0 — Strengthened institutional and regulatory systems for climate- responsive planning and 
development . The programme will build the institutional capacity of local governments in the seven countries 
to mainstream EbA and other climat e-resilient practices into local development plans and policies. This will 
include facilitating the establishment and operationalisation of financial incentives to promote investment in 
climate change adaptation.  
 
158. A7.0 — Strengthened adaptive capacity and  reduced exposure to climate risks . The adoption of EbA 
and water -efficient technologies across the target sites will reduce the impacts of climate change on vulnerable 
communities, particularly regarding water scarcity. For example, the combination of improved land- use 
practices and the adoption of natural resource- based businesses and alternative climate- resilient livelihoods — 
with technical and financial support provided through the programme — will improve the adaptive capacity of 
smallholder farming c ommunities under future climate conditions, reducing the impacts of shifting rainfall 
patterns and more regular droughts.  The hydrological modelling undertaken for the basins in Honduras, Costa 
Rica and the Dominican Republic allow for an estimation of the impact potential of this programme obtained 
from the implementation of combined EbA interventions at the catchment level. The results of this analysis are 
detailed in Annex 2, but in general terms, the analysis concludes that a combination of interventions results in 
substantial improvements for all key hydrological variables in the catchment areas. Soil humidity increases from 
large losses under the climate change base scenario to positive values under  EbA combinations; this results 
in benefits for water  availability both for agriculture and forests that draw water from the top soil layers. The 
changes in water production (surface water) and percolation (water recharge) show even greater benefits from 
the implementation of this combination of EbA measures .  These values are key for ensuring water availability 
throughout the year from a scenario of significant water deficit under climate change conditions. Lastly, the 
implementation of this combination of EbA measures also shows very positive impacts on soi l erosion rates . 
 
159. A8.0 — Strengthened awareness of climate threats and risk -reduction processes . The proposed 
programme includes a strong focus on knowledge and awareness as key concepts for building the resilience 
of vulnerable communities. In particular, the programme will build an evidence base for the effectiveness of 
EbA and disseminate this information to local decis ion-makers, private sector investors (including commercial 
farmers) and local communities.  
 
Adaptation Impact  
160. The proposed programme 's contribution to climate- resilient su stainable development will directly 
benefit ~ 1,084,441 people ( 30% w omen) across sev en catchment areas — one in each of the participating 
countries  through the  proposed interventions . Approximately 134,694 of these people will also directly benefit 
from the provision of financial mechanisms under Output 4  (lending and guarantee facilities) . The programme 
will also indirectly benefit 1,364 ,044 people across the region by facilitating increased access to finance for 
natural resource- based businesses, mains treaming EbA and other adaptation practices into national policies 
and building the capacity of governments to implement EbA. The financial mechanisms established under this 
programme  will make ~ US$189.2 million available for investment in EbA and water -efficient technologies in the 
region, introducing the potential for the restoration of ~1 42,500 hectares of degraded agricultural land through 
the implementation of sustainable land use practices . In addition to the technical support and establishment of 
financial mechanisms, the programme will actively : i) restore ~ 7,800 hectares of forested areas ; ii) protect 
~4,100 hectares of forest; iii)  establish 36 tree nurseries; i v) establish ~ 1,900 hectares of agroforestry systems; 
v) establish ~100 km of firebreaks, living barriers  and superficial drainage solutions, respectively ; and vi ) 
develop ~5,300 hectares of sustainable fuelwood sources. Finally, at least 100 community buildings will be 
equipped w ith rainwater harvesting systems , 156 community -level rainwater reservoirs will be installed, 43 half -
orange kilns  will be made communally available, and 38 community -level solar -powered water pumping stations 
will be installed .

 D.2. Paradigm  shift potential  
 
161. The proposed programme will catalyse  a paradigm shift in the regional approach to catchment and 
water demand management in the Dry Corridor of Central America and the Arid Zones of the Dominican 
Republic. This shift will see governments promoting large- scale EbA, and the private sector (incl uding 
commercial farmers and entrepreneurs in rural communities) having access to the financial resources and 
technical skills required to implement impactful adaptation interventions. The transformative effects of the 
programme will be achieved through several innovative approaches.  
 
162. The proposed EbA approach is inherently scalable,  not only within the targeted catchments , but also 
across the entire Dry Corridor and Arid Zones. The programme has been specifically designed to create an 
enabling environment  that capitalises on the scalability of EbA, focusing on three core areas of support: 
financial; policy; and technical. The details of these three areas of support are provided below.  
 
Creation of an enabling environment for investment in EbA and water -efficient technologies  
163. The proposed programme  will diversify financial systems in the region to create an enabling 
environment for investment in EbA and water -efficient technologies. This will be based on the establishment of 
several financial instru ments with varying entry -points to make climate finance more accessible to vulnerable 
communities. In particular, the programme  will establish: i) a trust fund  by CABEI  to support bottom- up 
promotion of local EbA activities through non- reimbursable financi ng; ii) an EbA lending facility to channel funds 
for small - and large- scale EbA investments at farm- , enterprise-  and household- level; and iii)  a guarantee facility 
to further de- risk lending to the target beneficiary groups for PFIs . The enabling environment for investment will 
be further enhanced by building the technical and institutional capacity of PFIs and nonregulated financial 
institutions to finance and re- finance EbA and water -efficient technologies through all three of the proposed 
financial inst ruments.  
 
Contribution to the regulatory framework and policies  
164. The enhanced local evidence- base for EbA that will be created through the knowledge hub and the 
monitoring and evaluation of programme activities will support national and local -level decision -makers to 
implement policies and incentives for EbA, in particular through improved valuation of natural capital. Decision-
makers within local government institutions will be trained to assess and update existing regulatory frameworks 
and policies to faci litate the mainstreaming of EbA — including making provision for incentives programmes 
targeting the adoption of EbA practices.  Furthermore, training and technical capacity -building activities are 
included in all programme components. By providing technica l support to local governments for the adoption 
and implementation of climate- resilient land management practices, the programme will help mainstream 
climate adaptation into local -level development planning. This will include the development of catchment -level 
land use plans, which will guide the adoption of EbA and sustainable land management practices beyond the 
scope of the programme's on-the-ground interventions.  
 
Technical support for the enhancement of regional knowledge and learning  
165. The proposed programme  includes several strategies for knowledge- sharing and learning. This support 
will include the establishment of a regional knowledge hub through which tailored knowledge products will be 
created and disseminated. This, combined with the strengthened  policy frameworks described above, will 
catalyse a shift in how decision- makers approach land -use and development planning. In addition, the social, 
economic and envi ronmental impacts of programme interventions, including the cost -effectiveness of EbA, will 
be measured through ongoing monitoring and evaluation. These findings will be disseminated to policymakers 
and decision- makers to catalyse further uptake of EbA and water - and resource- efficient  technologies, as well 
as the enabling financing mechanisms and economic incentives. The programme will also build awareness of 
the benefits of EbA and the associated financing mechanisms with private investors, as well as loc al and 
national governments.  
 
Upscaling of adaptation  
166. The programme will catalyse the autonomous upscaling of EbA interventions and water -efficient 
technologies by combining policy instruments (e.g. economic incentives such as payments for environmental 
services) with accessible financial mechanisms (e.g. commercial loans ,  microfinance and guarantee funds). 
These mechanisms will transform the financial landscape in the region to enable vulnerable groups to access 
climate finance. Furthermore, the programme  will promote the development of climate -resilient, natural


---
*Page 63*
---

G 
 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.2.1 | PAGE 61 OF 96 
   
 resourc e-based businesses and alternative livelihoods at the community level. These businesses and 
alternative livelihoods will be directly linked to EbA activities and the water -efficient technologies promoted by 
the programme ensuring long- term sustainability. Through comprehensive monitoring, reporting and verification 
of the benefits realised from the investment in EbA and other adaptation measures, the programme will 
showcase EbA finance as an attractive alternative for investors, resulting in the expansion of these operations 
outside the programme’s target areas. Indeed, the target programme areas represent only ~6% of the potential 
demand for EbA finance in the whole of the Dry Corridor and Arid Zones.  In addition, the regional replication 
potential of the p rogramme’s interventions is supported by the fact that similar interventions have been identified 
as appropriate  adaptation solutions in countries across the region.  For example, an analysis of the Latin 
America and Caribbean Region98 determined that, while it is difficult to prescribe specific national actions based 
on regional analysis, a general strategy for  adaptation,  agricultural development and poverty reduction should 
be based on: i) sustainable management of resources, including dissemination of pro ven technologies for 
farmers; ii) improved access to resources, including policies that offer incentives for sustainable land 
management; and iii) increased competitiveness of smaller farms, including loweri ng barriers to entry for small 
businesses.  Simila rly, a number of initiatives  across the region have implemented interventions similar to those 
proposed in this programme, such as: i) restoring and reforesting degraded ecosystems to protect water 
resources and stimulate aquifer recharge99; ii) introducing innovative agroforestry practices , and improving 
irrigation and resource technologies100; iii) establishing a risk -sharing facility to support climate- smart agriculture 
investments101; and iv)  strengthening capacity in financial institutions to dev elop EbA loans and other 
services102. The combined impact of the programme ’s interventions will be to catalyse EbA beyond the 
programme' s target catchments —  incorporating elements of sustainability, scalability, replicability, knowledge-
sharing, the creation of an enabling environment for private sector investors , and policy improvements in the 
programme design.  
D.3. Sustainable development  
 
167. The proposed programme  will contribute to the achievement of nine of the 17 UN Sustainable 
Development Goals (SDGs). The main contributions of the programme will be to: SDG 6 – Clean water and 
sanitation; SDG 11 –  Sustainable cities and communities; SDG 13 –  Climate action; and SDG 15 –  Life on land. 
In addition, the programme will contribute to: SDG 1 –  Poverty reduction; SDG 7 –  Affordable and clean energy; 
SDG 8 – Decent work and economic growth; SDG 9 – Industry, innovation and infrastructure; and SDG 10 – 
Reduced inequaliti es. The expected positive environmental, economic, social and gender impacts of the 
programme are outlined below , as well as the contribution of programme activities  to the region’s recovery from 
the COVID -19 pandemic.   
 
Environmental  co-benefits  
168. The cons ervation and restoration of ~ 119,200 hectares of degraded and climate- vulnerable 
ecosystems — through reforestation, forest management, agroforestry systems  and sustainable fuelwood 
practices  — as well as the use of water - and resource- efficient  technologi es will result in several environmental 
co-benefits. These co- benefits are listed below.   
• EbA interventions, including the conservation and/or restoration of forests and agroforestry systems, will act as 
natural buffers, controlling and mitigating the impact of floods, droughts, extreme temperatures and wildfires on 
ecosystems in selected sites.  
• Conserved and restored ecosystems will provide habitats for a range of terrestrial species, thereby improving 
biodiversity on programme sites.  
• EbA interventions under the programme will enhance soil fertility through accumulation of organic matter in 
revegetated areas,  as well as enhancing carbon storage capacity and reducing soil erosion at programme  sites. 
Conserved and revegetated areas will further enhance water infiltration and soil moisture retention, improving

 water quality and flow regimes  in selec ted sites. The use of water -efficient technologies under the programme  
will also contribute to improved water quality and availability by decreasing consumption.   
 
Social co -benefits   
169. The proposed programme is expected to yield substantial social co -benefits, mostly contributing to 
social development by improving water and food security of vulnerable communities in  the region.  These co-
benefits are listed below.   
• Through the adoption of water -efficient technologies such as rainwater harvesting sys tems, households will 
have increased access to clean water for domestic use as well as for food production. This will also improve 
the targeted communities' health by reducing the risk of water -borne diseases.  
• Increased productivity of agricultural systems  as a result of improved water supply will lead to enhanced food 
security and nutritional health. The targeted communities' food security will also be improved in terms of 
quantity, quality and diversity of food products through the adoption of agroforestr y methods.  
• The use of  agroforestry species according to their suitability under future climate conditions and the promotion 
of sustainable fuelwood practices will enhance the sustainability of local livelihoods, contributing to cultural 
preservation in th e region.  
• The programme will reduce inequality and poverty through increased financial inclusion and improved economic 
opportunities — especially for women. This will be achieved through interventions such as loans and risk 
transfer facilities for EbA and water -efficient technologies, as well as with the promotion of natural resource-
based businesses and climate- resilient alternative livelihoods . Through its participatory and gender -sensitive 
approach, the programme will specifically empower women and cont ribute to social cohesion.  
 
Economic co -benefits  
170. Improved access to water and the promotion of EbA will have several economic co- benefits for 
vulnerable communities in the Central  American Dry Corridor  and the Dominican  Republic's  Arid Zones.  These 
are listed below.   
• The implementation of water -efficient technologies  — such as drip irrigation and rainwater harvesting — in rural 
communities will improve agricultural practices, increasing agricultural productivity and associated income.  
• Short -term work opportunities will be created for the implementation of ecosystem restoration and protection 
activities under Output 2 . Long- term employment opportunities will also be generated by the need for ongoing 
operations and maintenance of ecological infrastructure established by the programme.  
• The programme  will provide opportunities to develop sustainable natural resource- based businesses and 
alternative livelihoods in ecotourism and in the collection of non- timber forest products , taking climate change 
scenarios and market demand for these products and ser vices into account.   
• Increased access to credit will further reduce financial barriers to implementing adaptation measures for the 
targeted rural communities.  
• The implementation of restoration activities and establishment of agroforestry  systems  under the programme  
will yield a net present value (NPV) of  ~US$15.8 million  worth of benefits which would accrue  to rural 
communities over a 50-year period103. 
 
Gender -sensitive development impact  
171. Women in Central America tend to hold traditional, unpaid gender roles associated with domestic 
responsibilities like motherhood, child-  and elder -care as well as household management. These roles generally 
also include water management and collection. Dur ing times of water scarcity or following natural disasters, the 
burden of finding, collecting and storing water increases, with women bearing most of this additional 
responsibility. The programme has been designed to ensure that interventions are implement ed in a gender -
sensitive manner. The in- depth understanding of water -related challenges for women in the region (described 
further in Section G.2) makes inclusion of gender considerations in programme  design and implementation 
critical for the long- term su stainability of programme  interventions. By building on this understanding of water -
related challenges among women using gender -sensitive learning methods for adaptation, the proposed 
programme  will not only empower women within their communities and promote gender equality , but will also 
ensure the effectiveness of programme interventions.

 172. The COVID -19 pandemic is having  severe negative impacts  on healthcares systems and 
economies  globally , including  further  reducing the capacity of  already -vulnerable Dry Corridor and Arid 
Zone populations  to respond to extreme climate events . With  existing  limited technical and financial 
capacities,  these populations  will require even greater relief interventions as their  COVID -19 recovery 
efforts increase existing  debt, while climate threats continue to build.  There are a number of  solutions in 
priority sectors104 that can be integrated into countries’ recovery plans , depending on their respective 
socio -economic and political circumstances.  One of these high impact opportunity areas is to make 
ecosystems, food and rural livelihoods resilient.  The fragmentation and loss of natural habitats facilitates 
the spread of infec tious diseases, and the conservation of ecosystems to improve their connectivity can 
dramatically reduce this risk. In addition, parallel threats posed to ecosystem health and food security  by 
climate change require cost -effective national interventions , while securing the availability of food will 
directly impact the population’s  ability to cope with future disease outbreaks.  EbA is vital in this recovery 
process as EbA measures  are cost -effective and facilitate the sustainable provision of ecosystem services 
that support the economic development of local populations while reducing loss from natural disasters , 
improving people’s  ability to cope with climate change and disease.  The promotion and facilitation of EbA 
by this programme will allow participating countrie s to undergo economic development that is not only 
climate resilient , but also provides benefits that improve the ability to cope with disease, lessening the 
potential impact of fut ure outbreaks. The  programme will also reduce these countries ’ economic burdens  
of post -COVID -19 recovery by providing external funding that will aid in the recovery of critical sectors , 
including  food production and water security , as well as tourism — the latter being related to landscape 
restoration.  
D.4. Needs of recipient   
Vulnerability  
173. The Dry Corridor region of Central America and the Arid Zones of the Dominican Republic are extremely 
vulnerable to the impacts of climate change (see Sections B.1 and B.5)105. A large proportion of people in these 
densely populated regions live in poverty, relying on subsistence agriculture and plantation forestry for their 
livelihoods. Rising temperatures, shifting rainfall patterns and the increased intensity of droughts are predicted 
to have several impacts on these vulnerable rural populations (see Section B.1), including: i) decreased 
harvests of important crops; ii) shifting suitability of land area for important crops; iii) increased frequency of 
wildfires; iv) increased frequency of pest outbreaks; and v) increased flooding after extreme rainfall events. 
These impacts will result in, inter alia, increased poverty, food insecurity and migration from rural areas106. 
Women in rural communities will be disproportionately affected as they are responsible for, inter alia , water 
collection, food security and unpaid family care. Through the proposed programme , the vulnerability of rural 
communities in the Dry Corridor will be reduced. This will be achieved through improved catchment and water 
management, as well as increasing rural communities' access to financial resources to fund adaptation 
interventions. The programme  will engage women's organisations to ensure that women have access to 
training, financ ial mechanisms and knowledge projects that will increase their resilience to climate change.

Economic and social development  
174. In the last decade, the Latin America and Caribbean (LAC) region —  under which the seven beneficiary 
countries fall — has achieved an average annual economic growth rate of 3.2%. This growth rate contributed 
to an overall reduction in poverty and expansion of the middle class107. Current ly, the seven beneficiary 
countries are classified as low middle to high income, with medium to high Human Development Index (HDI) 
scores (Table 2 , Section B.5). Nonetheless, ~40% of households in the LAC region remain vulnerable and 
economically insecure,  with a notable wealth gap existing between urban and rural populations. This economic 
insecurity is compounded by climate change impacts, including extreme rainfall and droughts. Economically 
insecure households — including vulnerable rural communities — are often slow to recover effectively from 
climate impacts108. Rural communities have little or no access to adaptation finance, which limits their adaptive 
capacity. By improving access to financial resources for adaptation interventions, the adaptive capac ity of rural 
communities in the Dry Corridor will be increased. With increased financial and technical capacity, communities 
will be able to withstand the negative impacts of severe droughts.  
 
Absence of alternative sources of financing  
175. The national budgets of Dry Corridor countries are limited, and poverty eradication and sustainable 
development have been prioritised for funding over CCA. There is a high dependency on non- reimbursable 
international support to meet CCA goals across the LAC region where the finance available for CCA is far below 
what is required to meet adaptation needs.  The added burden of the COVID -19 pandemic on the economies of 
these countries will leave even fewer funds available for CCA  in the participating countries . In the absence of 
donor support , the countries have limited financial resources to: i) implement large -scale EbA interventions at 
the landscape level; ii) implement small -scale water - and resource -efficient  technologies at community - or 
 
107 Báez et al., 2017. Shaking up economic progress: Aggregate shocks in the Latin America and the Caribbean. International Bank for Reconstruction 
and Development/The World Bank. Available online at: https://openknowledge.worldbank.org/bitstream/handle/10986/28892/121664- WP-Final -Report -
Aggregate- Shocks -and-Economic -Progress -English- web.pdf?sequence=1&isAllowed=y  
108 Báez et al., 2017.


---

 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.2.1 | PAGE 65 OF 96 
   
 household -level; iii) e stablish effective financial mechanisms that develop climate -resilient, natural resource -
based livelihoods; and iv) strengthen policies, institutions and knowledge- sharing for the implementation of EbA 
and economic incentives for EbA.  The proposed programm e will improve access to financial resources for 
private sector and community -level adaptation interventions, as well as mainstream climate change adaption 
into government planning and policy -making. This mainstreaming will enable the uptake of large- scale  EbA and 
other adaptation measures across Dry Corridor countries.  
 
Need for strengthening institutions and implementation capacity  
176. At the local level, vulnerable rural communities do not have the financial resources, knowledge base, 
or technical capacity to: i) develop, implement, and maintain EbA and other adaptation measures; ii) co- ordinate 
the frequent, cross -sectoral, multi -stake holder engagement that is necessary for EbA and other adaptation 
interventions; and iii) capitalise on EbA interventions by generating new private income streams from the 
ecosystem goods and services produced by restored climate- resilient ecosystems. The proposed programme  
will provide access to technical and financial resources, knowledge products and government support 
structures that will enable rural communities to adapt effectively to climate change in the Dry Corridor.  
 
Country -specific allocation  of GCF  funds  
177. Owing to the different socio- economic, development  and environmental contexts of the different 
participating countries, their  respective need s for climate finance differ. With regards to the financial 
mechanisms  being established under Output  4 of the programme , the participating countries will likely 
have different existing capacities for the uptake of EbA finance, meaning that some will be able to absorb 
higher levels of finance than others. There will also be different levels of demand for EbA finance in each 
country. The Regional Project Steering Committee will take into account all of these factors to agree on  
concentration limits to be finally decided upon by CABEI and the financial institutions for investing GCF 
Proceeds in a single country under the lending and guarantee facilities . The initial disbursement of the 
loan will be equally distributed among the 7 countries and this will serve as a basis for decisions on 
allocations of subsequent loan disbursements.  With regards to GCF grant finance (Component 1), on -
the-ground interventions to be implemented in the target municipalities in each country were selected 
based on extensive stakeholder engagement, availability  of area for implementation and appropriateness 
of interventions. A breakdown of grant proceeds distributed per country is provided in Annex 17.   
D.5. Country ownership  (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)  
Alignment with regional and national policies and strategies  
178. Climate change adaptation is recognised as a priority in the Central American region and has been  
integrated into several regional and national policies and strategies. Specifically, the programme is closely 
aligned with several pol icies and strategies of the Central American Integration System (SICA)109, including 
those listed below.  
 
• The Regional Environmental Strategy Framework (2015– 2020)110 provides a framework and objectives for the 
regional integration of actions on, inter alia: i) climate change and risk management; ii) biodiversity; iii) water 
resource management; and iv) finance for environmental management.  
• The Regional Climate Change Strategy (2010)111 sets out projections for the impacts of climate change and 
variability  on the region and provides a framework for regional responses to climate change.  
• The Regional Strategy for the Conservation and Sustainable Use of Biodiversity in Mesoamerica (2003)112 
articulates priorities for the regional integration of conservation and biodiversity management efforts.  
• The Regional Strategic Program for Forest Ecosystems Management (2014)113 aims to support the sustainable 
management and protection of forest ecosystems by promoting regional cooperation, through decentralised 
governance and  through  recognising the multifunctional nature of these forest s. 
 
109 The Central American integration System (SICA) is a regional institution that facilitates diplomatic and economic integration bet ween Central 
American states. The members of the organisation are Belize, Costa Rica, El Salvador, Guatemala, Honduras, Nicaragua,  Panama and the Dominican 
Republic. https://www.sica.int/sica/sica_breve_en.aspx

 • The Regional Agro -environmental and Health Strategy of Central America (2009 –2024)114 aims to promote 
sustainable, intersectoral management of agricultural ecosystems, with a focus on sustaina ble land 
management, climate change, biodiversity, agro- environmental businesses and healthy lifestyles.  
• The Food and Nutrition Security Policy for Central America and the Dominican Republic (2012 –2032)  promotes

• The Regional Strategy for Sustainable and Climate- adapted Agriculture for the SICA region (2017)115 sets out

efficient and sustainable management of water resources.

 National Adaptation Plan  (nd) 
 
Capacity of the Accredited and Executing Entities to deliver  
 
180. The Central American Bank for Economic Integration (CABEI) will be the Accredited Entity (AE) for this 
programme. CABEI has supported economic and social development in the region since its establishment in 
1960, including channelling more than US$ 600 mill ion to support MSMEs in the agricultural sector. They have 
work ed extensively in all the target countries and are acting as  the AE for  the GCF project ' Productive 
Investment Initiative for Adaptation to Climate Change'  (CAMBio  II)118 which was approved in 2018. CABEI also 
served as the Executing Agency on the original CAMBio119 project funded by GEF, which aimed to assist 
MSMEs in contributing to sustainable development and the protection of biodiversity through their products and services.  
 
181. Through a network of intermediate financial and microfinance institutions, CABEI has achieved a 
comprehensive regional footprint, including having a significant presence in rural areas that will be targeted by 
this programme. In addition, through their lar ge portfolio of projects and programmes, CABEI has developed 
strong relationships with local and national decision- makers. The extensive experience and proven track record 
of the AE will enable them to support and equip the Executing Entity to effectively execute the programme and 
contribute to its successful implementation.  
 
182. The programme will be co -executed by t hree entities: CABEI, FAO and CCAD.  FAO has been working 
for more than ten years to expand climate investments for the agricultural sectors, building its partnership with 
the Global Environment Facility (GEF) and the Green Climate Fund (G CF) with a view to fostering climate-
resilient development trajectories. As an accredited entity to the GCF , FAO has the capacity and expertise to 
mobilize, through its funding proposals, large flows of climate finance for climate change mitigation and 
adaptation initiatives and to act as an implementation partner for grants granted under the GCF  Readiness  
Support Programme. As FAO's GCF  portfolio is growing exponent ially, the Organization is prepared to assist 
countries whose ambitions for climate action are growing. FAO acts as an implementing entity in relation to 
GCF  grants for small and medium- sized projects (up to USD 250 million) with low and medium levels of 
environmental and social risk  and assists Member States in the development and implementation of country -
led projects.   The Central American Commission for Environment and Development (CCA D). This entity has 
implemented a number of  initiatives similar to the proposed programme in the region, using funding from  the 
GEF, the European Union, USAID, as well as through bilateral agreements with individual countries. In addition, 
it has acted as the main regional coordination entity for t he development and implementation of the Regional 
Environmental Strategy for Central America.  The Council of Ministers, which acts as the highest authority within 
the CCAD, will ensure that the  that programme  implementation proceeds  ensuring alignment wit h the national 
priorities and visions of each of the participating countries.   
 Stakeholder Engagement  
 
183. The development of this programme was initiated by the Council of Ministers  of the CCAD, which 
includes members from each of the seven target countries. In addition to engaging with the CCAD, regional 
and national stakeholder consultations have been conducted throughout programme development. Initial 
consultations took place in J une– July 2016 in six countries120. Through these national workshops, priorities and 
focus areas for the programme were identified. The draft Concept Note was circulated, and further consultations  
were conducted in October –November 2017 with representatives f rom each country. A regional workshop was 
also held in October 2017 to obtain input on the draft Concept Note from representatives of governments and 
NGOs in the region. These inputs contributed to refining the programme concept and incorporating lessons 
learned from previous initiatives in the region.  
 
184. Further consultations took place at the International Central American Dry Corridor Symposium in 
October 2018 and in each of the target countries from December 2018 to March 2019. These consultations

agricultural practices in programme areas, the panorama of offered financial and non- financial products and 
services potentially adjusted for EbA finance as well as appetite for participation in the programme.  
 
D.6. Efficiency and effectiveness  
Cost -effectiveness  and efficiency of programme  interventions  
186. The programme targets interventions on multi -stakeholder levels in order to prepare for EbA 
mainstreaming and upscaling by combining forces between public and private actors in selected areas. While 
US$84.3 millio n in grants are geared towards concrete and tangible benefits for an estimated total of  2,228,147  
direct and indirect  beneficiaries (outputs  1-3) via the strengthening of stakeholders’ capacities to better 
understand, develop and implement EbA solutions,  a further US$102.8  million in debt finance and US$ 30 
million as guarantee facility leveraging further US$ 129,974,200 will build upon such created capacities , with  
an estimated number of 134,694  direct beneficiaries  and 83,544 indirect benefici aries (total of 2 18,238  for 
lending  and guarantee facilities ). Smallholder farming units , rural community projects  and commercial farms  
are expected to access loans  to promote EbA in their regions121. The senior loans that make up the debt  finance 
will be supported by a guarantee fund, which will de- risk the loans, creating incentives for financial institutions 
to engage in EbA finance, for which there exist no or only limited offerings as of today.  While the grant solutions 
provide the bas is for the establishment and expansion of a body of knowledge  in target regions, the debt 
solutions will be managed fully self -sustained on a commercial basis, targeting potential further investment into 
the mechanisms created.  Hence, the programme forsees – considering the overall costs considered throughout 
all components, as well as direct (financial) and indirect (economic) benefits resulting from their implementation 
– an FIRR of 10. 1% with a NPV of US$  353,851,655 and an E IRR of 17 .5% with an NPV of US$ 2,005,009,949 
(both NPV discounted at an average of issued public bond rates as alternative investment).  
 
Application of best practices  
187. This programme draws on best practices from ground- breaking projects in the region and further afield. 
One of these is the Microfinance for Ecosystem- Based Adaptation (MEbA) project which informed the selection 
of appropriate EbA solutions for the proposed programme. Phase one of the project (MEbA I) was implemented 
in Colombia and Peru, while activities were expande d to Ecuador, Costa Rica, El Salvador, Dominican 
Republic, Senegal and Benin during phase two (MEbA II). EbA solutions drawn from MEbA  will be supported 
by implementing locally appropriate water - and resource- efficient  technologies, such as rainwater harvesting 
systems and half -orange kilns, for adapting to the increasing impacts of climate change, which were selected 
through consultations with local communities. Furthermore, effective methods for increasing commercial and 
smallholder farmers'  access to fin ancial resources for adaptation were drawn from the MEbA project as well as 
the Productive Investment Initiative for Adaptation to Climate Change (CAMBio II) and projects. These methods 
were applied to the design of activities under Component 2 , in particu lar, to ensure the sustainable use of 
financial resources for adaptation over the long term. Lessons learned from numerous baseline investments 
into climate change adaptation in the seven participating countries in the Central American Dry Corridor and 
Arid Zones of the Dominican Republic were also used to guide the design of all programme interventions. Such 
lessons include appropriate mechanisms for ensuring that all programme activities are implemented in a 
participatory, gender -sensitive and sustainable way. Details on all best practices and lessons learned relevant 
to the programme are presented in Section 10 of Annex 2: Feasibility Study.  
 
188. The primary international best practices and lessons learned identified for the programme include:

 • increasing commercial and smallholder farmers' capacity and access to finance for implementing EbA solutions

and resources for adaptation and support capacity -building interventions;  
• undertaking r egular monitoring and evaluation of adaptation interventions during the implementation phase to 
ensure that the most effective solutions are being implemented and that an adaptive management approach is 
being applied that considers changing climatic condit ions; and  
• engaging and collaborating extensively with all relevant programme stakeholders within each of the seven 
participating countries will encourage buy -in from national - and local -level decision- makers therefore 
contributing to the sustainability of proposed adaptation interventions over the long term.

122 This policy  uptake scorecard will have four levels and will measure the extent of use of the incentives for EbA in relevant local governm ent policies 
and plans.  
Level 0: Incentives not integrated meaningfully into municipal policy.  
Level 1: Incentives narrative wove n through the draft municipal policy –  including for the payment of ecosystem services (PES) and the establishment of 
water funds –  but not yet operational.  
Level 2: Action plans have been developed for implementation of incentives for EbA –  including PES and water funds –  with communities aware of the 
availability of incentives, but no projects operating under the incentive programme yet.  
Level 3: Incentive programmes fully operational, with budgets allocated to implement the incentives and at least one EbA project under implement ation 
under the incentive programme.

124 The indicator scale is based on four -step criteria of capacity assessment for each stakeholders group. Each question is scored from 0– 2, with: 0 = 
not at all, 1 = partially; and 2 = to a large extent/completely. An overall score is calculated, with a maximum score of 8 gi ven four criteria.  
i) Are the stakeholders aware of the current and expected impacts of climate change and the availability of climate- resilient practices available 
to adapt to these impacts?  
ii) Do local institutions have access to the EbA and water/resource- efficient technology protocols?  
iii) Do local institutions have the capacity to monitor and evaluate the state of ecosystems and the effectiveness of EbA interventions at the 
landscape level?  
iv) Do local institutions have the capacity to train farmers on the adoption of EbA and water/resource- efficient technologies?  
These four criteria will be elaborated, reviewed and validated at inception phase of the programme.

128 Within the MEbA II project an institutional capacity scorecard has been developed that assesses the capacity of a given insti tution in five main 
dimensions and expresses findings over 100 points in total, implemented with 13 institutions in Latin America and Sub- Sahara Africa:  
i) EbA solutions: does the institution have a basic understanding of concrete EbA and efficient water management solutions and is it able to 
distinguish and verify the application of these solutions by target end beneficiaries (survey -based)?  
ii) Information management: is internal information management structured in a way that enables the assessment of loan applications based on EbA and water/resource- efficient technology protocols (e.g. via MEbA II EbA capacity and EbA verification indicators) and spur ongoing 
learning to enable autonomous and effective EbA by target end beneficiaries?  
iii) Risk management: is the institution in a posi tion to integrate climate and ecosystem risk management (identification, measurement, monitoring 
and mitigation of these risks) into daily operations, including incorporating business continuity and contingency plans?  
iv) Products & services: is the portfolio of financial and non- financial products sufficiently sophisticated to integrate EbA and water -efficient 
technology -focused products and services relevant to PFI’s areas of operations?  
v) Marketing: is the marketing management incorporating all aspects needed to raise awareness of target end beneficiaries regarding the 
importance and potential impact of climate change, ecosystem services and water scarcity  
Questions are scored between 1 and 10 points with different weights applied; 20% percentiles define 5 segments of development status of an 
institution. [we do not have it as that – we use it to steer TA and close gaps] Each question is scored from 0—2, with: 0 = not at all, 1 = partially; and 
2 = to a large extent/completely. An overall score is calculated, w ith a maximum score of 8. These four criteria will be elaborated, reviewed and 
validated at inception phase of the programme.  rates more aligned

Activity 1.1.  Develop site -specific 
intervention  plans for the 7 target 
catchments to integrate EbA measures through a participatory 
process with municipal authorities, 
local communities, and other 
stakeholders.  
 A community -based approach will 
be adopted to identify site- specific 
interventions in each of the sev en 
municipalities. A community -level 
monitoring and evaluation 
committee will be established and 
capacitated in each municipality to 
monitor biophysical, social and 
economic conditions in their local 
catchment areas as well as to 
assess the level of climat e 
vulnerability, risks and 
opportunities. Baseline 
assessments conducted by these 
committees – with support from 
regional M&E specialists – will 
underpin the refinement of site-
specific  intervention plans,  
focussing on the selection of 
appropriate land for  the 
implementation of interventions 
identified through stakeholder 
consultations during the 
programme preparation phase. 
The outcomes of these 
assessments will be fed into the 
Regional Knowledge Hub(Activity 
3.1). The suite of identified EbA 
interventions  includes the 
conservation and restoration of 
natural forest, as well as the 
introduction of agroforestry, 
silvopastoral and 
agrosilvopastoral systems, along 
with sustainable fuelwood plantations. Specific consideration 
will be given for connectivity of 1.1.1  Establish a community -level 
monitoring and evaluation 
committee in each 
municipality and build the 
capacity of these 
committees to monitor the 
biophysical, social and 
economic conditions in their 
local catchment areas as 
well as to assess the level 
of clim ate vulnerability, risks 
and opportunities.  
1.1.2  Hold community engagement workshops in 
each municipality to 
develop a framework for 
site-specific intervention 
plans in each of the seven targeted catchments.  
1.1.3  Draft site -specific 
intervention plans for each of the seven targeted 
catchments.  
1.1.4  Hold a stakeholder workshop to validate and 
get stakeholder buy -in for 
site-specific intervention 
plans in each of the seven targeted catchments.
