Activity  1.2. Provide technical 
assistance to municipal 
authorities,  farmers and rural 
communities for the implementation of EbA practices 
as well as water - and resource-
efficient technologies  
 Effective implementation of  EbA 
practices and water -/resource -
efficient technologies requires 
training for both the recipients and 
the local -level authorities that 
provide support services to the 
recipients. Under the proposed 
programme, protocols will be 
developed for a range of 
sustainable pract ices, including : i) 
conservation and restoration of 
forested areas; ii) establishment 
existing agroforestry systems; iii) 
development of sustainable 
fuelwood source; iv)  water -
efficient technologies; and v)  
resource- efficient technologies 
that reduce fuelwood demand. To 
facilitate/promote the adoption of 
these protocols, cooperation 
agreements will be developed 
with commercial farmers and 
training will also be provided to 
build their technical capacity to implement sustainable practices. 
In addition to the direct training 
provided to commercial farmers, a 
training of trainers programme will be established in each country to 
facilitate long- term knowledge 
transfer to vulnerable 
communities. To increase the 
reach of the training programmes, 
trainers will be selected from a range of organisations, including 
local departments of environment, 
CBOs, water committees, women's organisations and local  
cooperatives within each target country. Formal training 
workshops will also be arranged 
within each municipality, which 
will include site visits to demonstration sites established 
under Output 4. 
 1.2.1  Develop cooperation 
agreements with 
commercial farmers for 
forest restoration and 
sustainable land 
management.  
1.2.2  Train commercial farmers 
on sustainable EbA practices, including 
silvopasture, agroforestry 
and SLM.  
1.2.3  Develop protocols for the 
implementation of  
sustainable EbA practices, 
including the: i) 
conservation and 
restoration of forested 
areas; ii)   establishment 
existing agroforestry systems; and iii) 
development of sustainable 
fuelwood source.  
1.2.4  Develop protocols for the 
adoption of water -efficient 
technologies by households 
(e.g. rainwater harvesting 
systems), by communities 
(e.g. water intakes) and by smallholder and commercial 
farmers (e.g. (drip irrigation, 
solar water pumping)  
1.2.5  Develop protocols for the 
adoption of resource-
efficient technologies tha t 
reduce fuelwood demand, 
(e.g. efficient charcoal kilns, 
fuelwood drying, efficient biomass stoves).  
1.2.6  Establish a training of trainers programme for representatives of local 
departments of 
environment, CBOs, water 
committees, women's 
organisations and local 
cooperatives within each 
target country for EbA and 
water -efficient technologies 
based on the protocols 
developed under sub-
activities 1.2.3 –1.2.5.  
1.2.7  Organise training 
workshops linked to 
community meetings within 
each target municipality to train local communities on 
EbA and water -efficient 
technologies, including 
visits to demonstration sites 
to facilitate farmer to farmer 
knowledge exchange.  • Cooperation agreements with

 Activity 1.3. Provide technical 
assistance to farmers and rural 
communities for the development 
of natural resource- based 
businesses and alternative climate -resilient livelihoods.  A community -based approach will 
be taken to improve the climate-
resilience of vulnerable, natural 
resource- based livelihoods in the 
target municipalities. This will involve the establishment of technical training programmes  to 
farmers and rural communities on locally appropriate natural 
resource- based business 
opportunities and climate- resilient 
practices that will improve the 
sustainability of natural resource-
based livelihood s. This training 
will be underpinned by a detailed 
livelihood assessment that will 
identify climate risks impacting the 
livelihoods of local communities, as well as potential alternative 
practices that will reduce this 
vulnerability. The results of this 
assessment will be used to 
develop livelihood action plans for each municipality identifying at 
least three natural resource- based 
businesses and/or climate-
resilient livelihoods options per municipality. Targeted training 
programmes workshop will be 
designed for each livelihood 
option, taking into consideration 
the local context and inputs from 
community consultations to customise the training for each 
municipality. These programmes 
will then be rolled out through community training workshops, 
with one works hop per livelihood 
being held per target municipality.  1.3.1  Conduct a livelihood 
assessment within each of 
the seven target 
catchments, focusing on 
climate risks and alternative 
options.  
1.3.2  Conduct community 
engagement workshops to 
develop livelihood action plans for each target 
community based on the 
assessments conducted in 
1.3.1.  
1.3.3  Establish livelihood training 
programmes in each 
municipality for farmers and 
rural communities on locally 
appropriate natural 
resource- based businesses 
and climate- resilient 
liveliho ods as identified in 
the livelihood action plans.  • 7 livelihood action plans  
• 108 training workshops

communities across the seven 
target catchments.  
 On-the-ground EbA interventions 
will be implemented on public land 
across each of the target 
municipalities. Interventions are 
listed below.  
• Conservation of existing forest 
ecosystems to maintain the 
provision of ecosystem 
services.  
• Active restoration of partially 
degraded forest ecosystems, 
including assisted natural 
regeneration and enrichment 
planting.  
• Establishment of agroforestry, 
silvopastoral and 
agrosilvopastural systems.  
• Establishment of fire breaks.  
• Establishment of mixed- use 
plantations using nat ive 
species with multiple ecosystem services, including 
non-timber forest products.  
• Establishment of sustainable 
fuelwood plantations to reduce 
pressure on natural forests.  
• Soil conservation measures.  
 
These interventions will serve as 
demonstration site s to promote 
the uptake of EbA practices 
through the financial mechanisms 
established through Output 1.

E.7. Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements  
189. The Project Manager (PM) will be responsible for the overall monitoring of programme  progress against 
the outcome- based indicators during implementation, as well reporting programme progress to the Regio nal 
Project Steering Committee (RPSC) . For on- the-ground monitoring of programme interventions, a Regional 
Monitoring and Evaluation (M&E) Specialist, supported by seven National M&E Specialists (one per beneficiary 
country), will be employed to develop practical guidelines for — and operationalise — a performance monitoring 
framework  to track the programme's progress towards achieving its targets. The Regional M&E Specialist will 
primarily be responsible for: i) establishing a performance monitoring framework to define biannual targets for 
the programme  to meet the mid -term and final targets defined in the Logical Framework (Section E.3– 5 of this 
Funding Proporsal); ii) measuring the indicators at least 1– 2 times per year to evaluate the progress of the 
programme  in meeting the targets and the application of gender -disaggregated indica tors; iii) reporting to the 
PM and RSPC on the performance of the programme according to programme  indicators; and iv) participating 
in the production of reports. Given the scale of the programme , National M&E Specialists will be procured in 
each beneficiary country to support the Regional M&E Specialist in monitoring the performance of programme  
interventions at a national level. Specifically, the National M&E Specialists will be responsible for: i) following 
guidelines developed by the Regional M&E specia list to conduct performance monitoring for the programme, 
aimed at meeting the targets defined in the programme  document by the end of the implementation phase; ii) 
measuring the national indicators at least 1 –2 times per year to evaluate the progress of t he programme in 
meeting the targets and indicators; iii) reporting to the NAC on the performance of the programme  according to 
programme indicators; iv) ensuring that all activities under the programme comply with the Programme ’s ESMF 
process and procedure s; v) supporting environmental screening of sub activities and help to follow the activity 
specific Environmental Management Plans (EMPs); and vi) participating in the production of reports to the 
RSSPC and RPMU. Both the Regional and National M&E Speciali sts will be supported by a Gender Officer 
(GO), who will be employed on a part -time basis to oversee and monitor the application of gender -
disaggregated indicators.  
 
190. The Regional M&E Specialist and GO will perform regular visits — at least once a year to e ach country 
— randomly selecting municipalities to monitor on- the-ground implementation of programme interventions. 
They will use these visits to cross- check the information provided by PFIs and beneficiaries in programme 
monitoring reports. During each fi eld visit, the Regional M&E Specialist and GO will collect qualitative data on 
the outcomes and difficulties experienced during implementation through focus group discussions with different 
beneficiaries, as well as through in- depth interviews with key stakeholders. Using this information, the Regional 
M&E Specialist, supported by the National M&E Specialists, will compile six -monthly programme  reports to be 
shared with the RPMU and RPSC. These reports will outline programme progress in each country as well  as 
regionally, and will allow feedback and advisory support from the RPMU and RPSC in the case of potential 
programme delays or other challenges that may arise during implementation. Annual programme progress 
reports and annual gender reports will also be produced to be sent to the GCF Secretariat with information 
regarding programme  progress measured against expected outputs.  
 
191. CABEI, as the Accredited Entity (AE) will perform supervision missions from year two of programme  
implementation onwards, selecting three countries each year to visit and assess the status of programme  
implementation. This process will allow CABEI to maintain oversight throughout the programme 's lifetime. In 
addition, CABEI's Credit Supervision Department — which oversees the destination and use of funds disbursed 
to PFIs — will monitor the programme 's compliance with policies and procedures, as well as the alignment 
between implementation and programme objectives.  
 
192. As per the Accreditation Master Agreement (AMA), an independent cons ultant will be contracted by 
CABEI to conduct a Mid- Term Evaluation (MTE) and the Terminal Evaluation (TE) of the programme . Both 
evaluations will comply with GCF guidelines and international standards and will make use of qualitative as well 
as quantitati ve data analyses, including interviews with relevant stakeholders, data provided by the Regional 
and National M&E Specialists and GO, participative workshops and focus groups with beneficiaries to promote 
participative learning. The MTE will provide an ass essment of programme performance against its targets at 
the programme's mid- point. This will be a formative exercise and will include analysing whether the programme  
is on track, what problems and challenges the programme is encountering, and what correcti ve actions are 
required, so that the programme can achieve its intended outcomes by programme completion in the most 
efficient and sustainable way. The RPSC and RPMU will be informed of all MTE findings and will develop a


---
*Page 86*

G 
 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.2.1 | PAGE 84 OF 96 
   
  
 
 
 
    
 
    
 
  
       
 
 
     
 
  
 
  
 
 management response to the evaluation recommendations along with an implementation plan. It will be the 
responsibility of the P M to monitor whether the agreed recommendations are being implemented during the 
remainder of the programme's operational life.  
 
193. The TE will take place at the end of programme implementation and will be an independent  assessment 
of programme performance ag ainst standard evaluation criteria (e.g. strategic relevance, effectiveness, 
efficiency, likelihood of impact and sustainability). This assessment will be done based on documented 
evidence, stakeholder interviews and, in most cases, a field mission. In addition to an assessment of expected 
results and impacts, unexpected outcomes will be evaluated, including unexpected co- benefits, best practices 
and lessons learned. The draft TE report will be sent to programme stakeholders for comment and subsequently 
disclosed publicly.

 RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT  
F.1. Risk factors and mitigations measures (max.  3 pages )  
 
195. There are no United Nations Security Council (UNSC) sanction regimes currently under implementation 
in any of the seven countries involved in the programme. In addition, no individual, entity or other group listed 
on the UNSC sanctions list, including the consolidated list, will be involved in any manner with the programme  
or its activities, either as a counterparty, implementer or beneficiary . 
196. The identified risks to the proposed programme's  implementation and sustainability, associated impacts 
and mitigat ion measures are described in detail below, with summaries provided in the following two tables. 
Risks were classified according to a risk matrix (Table 4), with a summary presented in Table 5 which shows the risk level based on the probability and impact of the risk factors. The mitigation potential is based on the 
mitigation actions detailed below and is combined with the risk level to determine overall risk after mitigation.

Selected Risk Factor 1   
Category  Probability  Impact  
Technical and operational  Medium  Medium  
Description  
Behaviour change does not occur and the implementation of proposed programme  activities  (including operations 
and maintenance of those established under Output 2) cease after conclusion of the programme, because rural 
communities are unwilling to invest in EbA interventions. Ecosystem degradation and unsustainable practices 
continue.  
Mitigation Measure(s)  
The programme  will facilitate long -term change in land -use practices and uptake of EbA by making financial 
instruments available to support investment into EbA. In addition, the programme  will engage directly with communities

 as well as with local and national governments to create an enabling environment for EbA interventions, including 
through:  
• incorporating comprehensive engagement with communities for planning, implementation and monitoring of EbA 
initiatives (for example Activities 4 .1 and 1.1); 
• providing t raining for communities, commercial farmers and decision- makers to develop capacity for supporting and 
implementing EbA and to build a common understanding of the benefits of these approaches (for example Activity

decrease the likelihood that this risk will occur (Section B.6).  In addition, an operations and maintenance (O&M) 
manual  is provided in Annex 21 which further details how interventions will be maintained post -programme

Selected Risk Factor 2   
Category  Probability  Impact  
Technical and operational  Medium  Low 
Description  
High turnover of staff in implementing institutions, frequent changes in government bodies and limited institutional 
memory results in a disruption and/or delays in the programme implementation and may jeopardise the sustainability 
of the programme .  
Mitigation Measure(s)  
Decisions, best practices and lessons learned will be documented throughout the programme  to support institutional 
memory that will sustain programme  activities. This memory will also be strengthened through the regional knowledge 
hub that will be established under Activity 3.1. Protocols for EbA will be developed in both English and the main local 
languages to guide new staff who become involved in Eb A implementation during and after the programme. 
Furthermore, through the programme, PFIs and communities will be trained and engaged in the implementation as 
well as the monitoring and evaluation of EbA as well as water - and resource -efficient  interventio ns, thereby 
strengthening the capacity of local communities and institutions to plan and implement EbA. Where possible, the RPSC 
will make use of established regional and national structures to capitalise on well -established practices and systems 
that are familiar to government staff.  
Selected Risk Factor 3   
Category  Probability  Impact  
Technical and operational  Medium  Low 
Description  
Overlapping or misalignment between the proposed programme  activities and those being implemented by other 
departments or organisations in the programme  area.  
Mitigation Measure(s)  
The proposed programme  has been designed with extensive stakeholder engagement and desktop research to ensure 
that there is alignment with any past and ongoing initiatives in the region. Regular engagements will be conducted with relevant individuals/organisations to prevent du plication of effort, support collaboration and ensure that current and 
future projects  are aligned with the proposed programme's interventions. Coordination mechanisms — including the 
regional knowledge hub which will facilitate the sharing of lessons learned — will be established at the regional and 
national levels to ensure synergy among efforts of government and other national and regional stakeholders.

Selected Risk Factor 5   
Category  Probability  Impact  
Technical and operational  Medium  High 
Description  
Limited technical capacity among PFIs, government authorities and local communities to channel funds for and 
implement EbA interventions.  
Mitigation Measure(s)  
Training and technical assistance will be provided for relevant PFIs, government authorities and local communities that 
will be involved in programme activities. In particular, a technical assistance facility will be established to facilitate 
financial institutions' abilities to access funds for EBA interventions. In addition, technical capacity of government 
authorities and local communities will be strengthened to improve their abilities to: i) implement EbA protocols in a 
scientifically rigorous manner; and ii) monitor and evaluate the success of these interventions.  
Selected Risk Factor 6   
Category  Probability  Impact  
Technical and operational  Low Medium  
Description  
Natural disasters undermine the: i) implementation of EbA  interventions and result in economic loss and/or damage 
to the interventions; and ii) restoration and sustainable management of forests, wetlands and agro -systems.  
Mitigation Measure(s)  
The programme 's EbA and water/ resource -efficient  interventions will target areas that have been identified as being 
vulnerable to climate change impacts. For example, EbA will be implemented in sites that are prone to drought as a measure to reduce the risk or physical impact of this type of climate- related natural disaster. Water/ resource- efficient  
solutions will also increase the resilience of these communities to droughts. Furthermore, the programme will integrate 
information on local climate change risks and impacts into training for PFIs and communi ties to ensure that forecasts 
are taken into consideration during the planning and implementation of EbA interventions. The RPMU will also oversee 
the incorporation of forecasts into programme  planning and implementation.  
Selected Risk Factor 7   
Category  Probability  Impact  
Technical and operational  Low Medium  
Description  
Social tensions may arise as existing power relations within the community might disrupt equal opportunities to use 
and benefit from water - and resource -efficient  technologies.  
Mitigation Measure(s)  
The programme 's activities have been designed to be transparent and will maintain a broadly consultative approach 
throughout the implementation period. The beneficiaries of the water - and resource- efficient technologies have been 
identified through local stakeholder consultations. In addition, the activities have been designed to ensure that all 
individuals are afforded access to the technologies, which will be installed in public facilities — for example rainwater

 harvesting in schools — rather than in private households. Mechanisms for resolving conflicts and addressing disputes 
and complaints will be established to ensure that the programme's activities are transparent and that the programme  
mana gement team can be held accountable by beneficiary communities.  
Selected Risk Factor 8   
Category  Probability  Impact  
Governance  Medium  Low 
Description  
Changes  in political leadership,  which  may result  in delays  in implementation.  
Mitigation Measure(s)  
Regular engagement with non -political actors  — such as government agency officials, local authorities, staff and local 
community members  — will be prioritised during programme  implementation.  Additionally, formulation of local 
ordinances, mainstreaming of programme interventions in local planning and budgeting systems will ensure that 
political issues will have negligible impact on the sustainability of programme interventions.  Finally, support from 
regional bodies such as the CCAD will promote continued political support.   
Selected Risk Factor 9   
Category  Probability  Impact  
Governance  Medium  Medium  
Description  
Insufficient coordination, collaboration and information -sharing between countries and implementing partners at a 
regional level and between relevant government departments, research institutions and private sector entities within 
the countries. This can result in the limited participation of relevant institutions and agencies, reducing the timely 
delivery and effectiveness of the programme . 
Mitigation Measure(s)  
Detailed implementation arrangements have been designed to ensure effective coordination of programme  activities 
between regional stakeholders. These arrangements are outlined in Section B.4. The relevant institutions were 
involved in the design of the programme activities and have agreed on all roles and responsibilities. Coordination 
between them will be supported by regular meetings/engagements between implementing partners  to facilitate 
information sharing.  
In addition, programme countries, relevant government departments, research institutions and the private sector will 
be encouraged to share information through a number of technologies and systems that will be established or 
strengthened by the programme. Technologies and syste ms will be selected based on the most appropriate 
approaches to sharing information in both the relevant regional and local contexts. Examples include:  
• a regional knowledge hub to facilitate sharing of best practices and lessons learned across countries and sectors 
as well as to provide policy - and decision- makers with relevant information regarding EbA;  
• cooperation agreements with stakeholders to promote an understanding of roles and responsibilities within the 
programme and facilitate effective participation; and  
• training workshops for local authorities, community organisations and partner financial institutions, among others, 
to ensure a shared understanding of EbA and water/ resource -efficient  strategies.

Selected Risk Factor 11  
Category  Probability  Impact  
Credit  Low Medium  
Description  
Deteriorating eco nomic situation of end beneficiaries and in consequence payment capacity  as well as  lack of 
management capacity at the level of PFIs and IFIs can lead to increased credit risk of funds provided by GCF and 
CABEI. This increased credit risk not only can affect the funds provided by GCF and CABEI, but also put the overall 
success of the programme at risk. Hence the potential impact is considered ‘medium, while the probability is considered 
as ‘low’ as the financing mechanisms –  three financial facilities –  build upon proven and established structures and the 
programme as a whole is targeting the increase of end beneficiaries’ resilience , lowering their cr edit risk.  
Mitigation Measure(s)  
The mitigation of credit risk within the programme relies upon mitigation measures on three different levels:  
1. Eligibility of IFIs, PFIs and end beneficiaries: CABEI will define strict eligibility criteria for all actors involved 
down- stream of its provision (co- finance) and management (GCF funds) of funds , hence the selection of 
counterparts will be used to limit the credit risk  
a. IFIs: only institutions forming part of CABEI’s already accredited network of IFI will gain access to the 
programme; these institu tions  regularly  lead their respective markets in terms of credit risk 
management capacity (as expressed per their ratings)  
b. PFIs: CABEI’s minimum eligibility criteria will ensure that only the most solid institutions  will ga in 
access to the programme; further, by assigning part of the PFI’s credit risk  to IFIs (via the on -lending 
stream of funds), these IFIs will ensure to strengthen the selection of low -risk PFIs  
2. Promotion of EbA finance: targeting the investment into EbA solutions that increase resilience and hence lower 
credit risk of end beneficiaries as main objective of the programme ties the activities of the programme directly 
to concrete measures to mitigate credit risk  
3. Introduction of solid MRV system: via the introduction of proven digital toots, will enable a centralized 
monitoring of credit risk tendencies in resulting portfolios and sub- portfolios, with the aim to introduce early 
warning routines as well prioritize contingency  plan activities, following methodologies UNEP’s MEbA project   
 GCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS  
G.1.  Environmental and social risk assessment   
 
197. The environmental and social risk associated with the programme  was evaluated in accordance with 
GCF's Environmental and Social Safeguards (ESS). According to the assessment, the programme is 
considered to be Category I -2 or Medium Risk. This categorisatio n is supported by an Environmental and Social 
Assessment and associated Management Framework (Annex 6). The assessment and management plan 
comply with GCF requirements by:  i) defining the context and baseline of each target area in which the 
interventions will be implemented; ii) identifying all negative and positive impacts associated with each of the 
proposed interventions; and iii) providing a management plan to describe how any adverse impacts identified 
during risk screening will be accounted for or mi tigated throughout the programme implementation period. The 
primary environmental and social risks that may be associated with the programme  — as identified by the 
assessment — are presented below.  
 
198. Overall, the environmental and social risks assessed to b e presented under this programme are minor 
and can be mitigated and/or effectively managed with the implementation of an appropriate strategy. The 
programme is expected to result in net -positive environmental and social benefits in the long term, which 
include: i) supporting economic security and environmental benefits by facilitating access to micro- finance and 
grant facilities for the implementation of small -scale EbA interventions; and ii) increasing water security through 
improved catchment management and the provision of water -efficient  technologies and systems. However, 
several risks have been identified that, if not adequately managed, will reduce the resilience of certain 
communities or result in adverse environmental, social or economic impacts. The se potential impacts are 
detailed below.  
 
Environmental impacts


---
*Page 92*
---

G 
 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.2.1 | PAGE 90 OF 96 
   
 199. Several potential environmental impacts have been identified as being associated with the 
implementation of the proposed activities. These impacts are, however, avoidable and can be mitigated 
effectively through appropriate management measures. The main environmental impacts include:  
i) a possible increase in the use of pesticides and fertilisers in the EbA options, which may create both environmental 
and social risks by polluting natural resources;  
ii) an increase in the emission of short - and long- lived climate pollutants, by the execution of protocols and EbA 
measures, especially if the implementation of agroforestry systems includes the use of chemically sourced nitrogen 
fertilisers;  
iii) methodological approaches to assessing ecosystem services may generate biases at the time of assessment, affecting the perceived importance of specific ecosystems, therefore impacting on biodiversity and habitats; and  
iv) unintended promotion of deforestation for fuelwood to be used in the furnaces and stoves being marketed under the 
programme.  
 
200. The environmental impacts listed above can be effectively managed by, for example: i) the 
dissemination of specific guidelines on the use of fertilisers; ii) awareness -raisin g on strategies for the 
sustainable harvesting of fuelwood; and iii) clear methodological approaches to assessing ecosystems. For 
further information on specific management and mitigation strategies relating to each activity, please refer to 
Annex 6: Envir onmental and Social Management Framework (ESM F).  
 Social impacts  
201. The potential for adverse social impacts under the programme has been assessed as more likely than 
environmental impacts. These adverse impacts largely pertain to the potential for economic and cultural 
displacement, adverse economic impacts resulting from debt accrued through the use of financing mechanisms and restrictions on land use. The main potential social impacts include:  
i) non-recognition or alteration of Indigenous People's livelihood strategies, cultural practices, religious beliefs or 
governance structures;  
ii) economic displacement and an increase in economic insecurity in the event that credit conditions are not suitable for small producers, or that interest rates are not commensurate with the return rate of EbA interventions;  
iii) increased restrictions on land- use for small scale farmers, Indigenous Peoples or other marginalised groups;  
iv) unequal opportunities for indigenous peoples and other marginalised groups to benefit from the programme  
interventions, particularly if the opportunities are not promoted in an accessible, culturally appropriate and inclusive manner; and  
v) limited inclusion of all stakeholder priorities given the large geographic extent, diverse stakeholder groups and range of sociocultural contexts.  
 
202. These impacts are all manageable or avoidable and can be effectively reduced and/or mitigated 
through the implementation of appropriate management strategies. For further information please refer to 
Annex 6: ESMF .  
 Indigenous Peoples  
203. Potential adverse impacts on Indigenous Peoples are a critical concern for the GCF and although no 
unavoidable impacts on Indigenous Peoples have been identified in the risk screening, certain interventions 
may result in unintended impac ts. However,  it is important to note that the programme has been designed to 
be open and inclusive and it will consider the languages, dialects and cultural practices of all beneficiary groups throughout implementation to ensure equal access and fair distr ibution of programme 
benefits. In addition, 
because adaptation measures have been designed with an ecosystem- based approach that inherently respects 
Indigenous Peoples' cultural relationship with their territory, it is assumed that Indigenous Peoples will not be affected. CABEI — as the AE — has experience in facilitating the inclusion of Indigenous Peoples in GCF -
financed projects and will maintain oversight.  
 Grievance Redress Mechanism  
204. The established CABEI Grievance Redress Mechanism (GRM) will be imple mented for this 
programme. The mechanism is approved by the GCF and has been used for a prior GCF project. The GRM will be used at all stages of the programme and will provide an accessible, rapid, fair and effective response to 
concerned stakeholders, esp
ecially any vulnerable groups who may lack access to formal legal systems or 
support. For further information please refer to Annex 6: ESMF .

*Page 93*
---

G 
 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.2.1 | PAGE 91 OF 96 
   
 G.2.  Gender assessment and action plan  
 
205. During programme preparation, a gender assessment was conducted to understand the differential 
vulnerability to, and impact of, climate change on women and men in the Dry Corridor and Arid Zones of the 
seven participating countries. From the findings of this assessment, a nd an analysis of the proposed 
programme solution, a gender action plan (GAP) was developed. The process to produce these components 
included a desktop review of available literature, as well as stakeholder and beneficiary consultations held in 
Guatemala, Honduras, El Salvador, Nicaragua, Costa Rica, Panama and Dominican Republic. The assessment 
does note that gender -disaggregated data is limited, particularly regarding indicators and information on land 
tenure, participation in decision- making, livelihood activities, and access to credit and finance.  
 
206. Traditional gender roles and stereotypes in the programme area, as well as the resulting asymmetrical 
power differences they produce, create a culture of social exclusion that leaves women vulnerable to the 
impacts of climate change, with reduced capacity or opportunity to adapt. The patriarchal and sexist roles, 
norms and stereotypes create inequalities in social relations and cultural institutions, wherein men maintain 
'productive', income- generating roles an d women perform mostly 'reproductive', unpaid duties. Notably, this 
manifests in women's: i) reduced knowledge of information systems (such as early warning or forecasts); i) low 
participation in training; iii), limited access to finance and credit; iv) in equitable management and control of 
natural resources and land; v) greater exposure to violence; vi) limited participation in decision- making 
institutions; and vii) exclusion from accessing benefits and public resources for development. In addition to 
undermining their agency, capabilities and safety, this system limits the realisation of women and girls' rights, 
which is particularly evident in rural and indigenous contexts where gender inequalities are most pronounced 
and baseline vulnerability (for all genders) is highest.  
 
207. The profile of migration from the region has changed in the past decade, with women increasingly 
becoming active agents in the migration process, rather than passive actors relative to the husbands or male 
family members' decisions. T his is notable because migrant women face additional risks to men because of the 
same gender conditions that shape their vulnerability at origin. This can manifest in isolation, lack of social 
networks, precarious employment conditions, unsafe living envir onments, and exposure to violence, 
exploitation and even enslavement.  
 
208. Although potentially more relevant to mitigation activities, the assessment also notes that female 
environmental and rights activists in the region are particularly targeted by extract ive industries  (mining, 
hydroelectric, monocultures and/or oil) for violence, stigmatisation and criminalisation. These conditions, 
wherein women experience discriminatory, violent and exclusionary practices, are unfavourable to their 
continued participati on and representation in activist spaces, which may result in unfavourable biases and even 
further inequality.  
 
209. Regarding climate change, consultations conducted for the assessment described the effects of 
droughts and floods on women, which include: i) mu ltiplication of the 'reproductive' day, wherein women's 
burden of work increases; ii) negative impacts on agriculture and fishing for smallholder families, particularly for female- headed households; iii) increased migration and displacement; iv) decreased forest cover reducing 
access to resources like water, food, fuels, and non-
timber forest products (including herbs and medicinal 
plants) which has additional implications for household health; v) reduced opportunities for women in income-generating work su
ch as agriculture or handcrafts (as a result of reduced resources); vi) reduced access to 
clean drinking water, which has further opportunity costs for women and girls' exposure and time spent 
collecting water; vii) negative impacts on women's largely -marg inalised physical and mental health due to 
stress, hunger and malnutrition, heatstroke and kidney disease, and 'household' diseases such as Zika and 
mites.  
 
210. The assessment describes how the conditions and vulnerabilities described above will influence 
programme implementation, particularly noting how social exclusion may preclude women from participating 
meaningfully in decision- making processes and accessing the benefits of the programme, such as training and 
economic empowerment. To mitigate against biasi ng against women as programme  beneficiaries during 
implementation and subsequently, the gender action plan outlines several interventions in response. These 
interventions will help ensure the programme does not unintentionally contribute to perpetuating or worsening 
gender equality an d gender -based climate vulnerability in the Dry Corridor and Arid Zones. The GAP seeks to


---
*Page 94*
---

G 
 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.2.1 | PAGE 92 OF 96 
   
 ensure women are empowered and enabled to meaningfully contribute to, and benefit from, programme  
activities and that stakeholders' actions and programme  processes make considerations for the needs, interests 
and vulnerabilities of women. The GAP includes clear targets, gender -sensitive design features and quantifiable 
performance indicators to promote women's participation and benefits from the programme.  
 
211. The actions  of the GAP include:  
• developing women- oriented financial products and services to finance EbA investments;  
• capacity -building and awareness -raising amongst stakeholders on the importance and implementation of gender 
mainstreaming/gender equality;  
• strengthening the technical capacities of women's organisations at the local level; and  
• ensuring the active participation and empowerment of women producers and women's organisations in programme  
processes.  
 
212. Implementation arrangements of the programme also include a Gender Officer under the RPMU and a 
gender expert in each of the PEUs.  
 
213. The full gender assessment and programme- level gender action plan are included as Annex 8.  
G.3. Financial management and procurement  
 
214. CABEI, through its current structure, including its Representation Offices in each country of the 
Programme, the Office of Compliance, the Office of Environmental and Social Safeguards, the Office of 
Supervision of Credit Operations, and the Internal Audit Office, will oversee, moni tor and follow up the 
implementation of the Dry Corridor Programme. It is through this structure and the legal obligations derived 
from the contracts between CABEI and the IFIs, and the contracts between CABEI & FAO and CABEI & CCAD 
that CABEI can guarantees that the programme will be implemented under CABEI’s policies and procedures . 
In particular:  
• The Private Sector Division , through CABEI’s Representation Office in the countries will be responsible 
for the coordination of actions and activities regarding the follow up, monitoring and oversight of the 
programme; the management and process of the disbursements needed by the programme executing 
entities, the IFIs and the PMU to carry out the programme ’s planned activities.  
• The Credit Division, through its Office of Credit Operations Supervision will be responsible for leading 
the oversight process of the physical and financial execution of the programme.  
• The Office of Environmental and Social Sustainability  will be responsible for designing, c ompleting and 
validating the I -BCIE questionnaire, as well as generating the calculation and Final Evaluation Report ex 
ante, which is an integral part of the intervention's credit opinion, which is presented with the operation 
to the CABEI approval author ities, as well as accompany ing the supervision process with the  Office of 
Credit Operations Supervision.  
• The CABEI Independent Evaluation Office is responsible for the validation of the I -BCIE questionnaire, 
the development of the mid -term and ex post cal culation and Final Evaluation Report.  
• CABEI’s Procurement Unit , in coordination with CABEI’s Office in the countries, will oversee the 
procurement activities required by the programme , to ensure that the implementation is in line with 
CABEI´s Policy for Procurement of Goods, Works, Services and Consulting with Bank resources and its 
application rules. The purpose of these regulations is to establish the guidelines and procedures that the 
executing entities must comply with for procurement in the execution of operations.  
• The CABEI’s Integrity and Compliance Office will be responsible for  analyzing and propos ing a course 
of action of the reports/allegations related to prohibited practices to an Integrity Committee, in accordance 
with CABEI´s internal regulati ons and the applicable terms to the different contractual relationships with 
counterparties.   
• The Finance Division  through its Partnerships and International Cooperation Department is responsible 
for acting as the GCF focal point for the follow up of the programme  execution and coordinating the

 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.2.1 | PAGE 93 OF 96 
   
 development and delivery of all reports agreed with GCF in order to guarantee a proper monitoring and

215. Regarding the Fiduciary Compliance, CABEI is an International Multilateral Development Bank, who 
has policies and procedures in place regarding antifraud/anticorruption, AML/CFT and sanctions in accordance 
with CABEI´s nature and are currently in force and in accordance with current international standards.    
 
216. Policy and Procedural Manual is applied and observed in all active and passive operations, and in 
those involving procurement of goods and services in which the Bank interacts or is the counterpart of  another 
natural or juridical person. CABEI´s Money Laundering Prevention Policy, Manual and procedures are currently 
in force and in accordance with current international standards. This Policies and Manuals will be applied in 
order for the programme and its activities to conform to any UNSC sanctions programs that may be applicable, 
also to prevent that no entities or individuals involved in the programme as counterparties or implementers are 
listed on any UNSC sanctions lists.  
 
217. CABEI shall neither accept  nor maintain a commercial relationship or operation if it suspects that funds 
of a client or potential client may originate from criminal activities.  
 
218. CABEI has a Risk Rating System, which is comprised of comprehensive and periodic reviews of all 
private and non- sovereign sector operations.  
 
219. CABEI uses Sentinel (World Check) for the screening of all counterparties. We use Google in order to 
review our d irect counterparties and PEP. Additionally, CABEI has implemented a swift sanctions screening to 
monitor all incoming and outgoing CABEI transactions and has acquired banker’s almanac.  
 
220. On the other hand, CABEI has implemented several mechanisms (web, ema il, voice mail, among 
others) in which reports and allegations of irregularities, unethical situations, the commission of prohibited 
practices or regulatory breaches in the use of its funds or resources administered by CABEI can be submitted. 
Reports about  environmental and/or social issues or damages related to CABEI´s projects can be reported as 
well. Any natural or juridical person, including CABEI´s staff members may submit a report. Anonymously 
reports are permitted.  
 
221. The Integrity and Compliance Offic e is in charge of analysing and proposing a course of action of the 
reports/allegations related to prohibited practices to an Integrity Committee, in accordance with CABEI´s 
internal regulation and the applicable terms to the different contractual relationships with counterparties.    
 
222. As demonstrated by its status as an Accredited Entity to the GCF, CABEI has extensive expertise in 
working with international funds and has a good track record in implementing projects using international 
financial management practices. For the proposed pr ogramme , CABEI will be responsible for the fiduciary 
aspects and will be accountable for all financial and investment activities.  
 
223. The procurement of goods and services (including consultants' services) for activities financed by the 
GCF will be carried out following CABEI's standard practices and procedures — including its procurement and 
consultants' guidelines —  which are aligned to those of the GCF, as well as to all national laws. Further details 
on the procurement of goods and services, as well as CAB EI's applicable procurement procedures are provided 
in the Procurement Plan in Annex 10.  Procurement executed by EE’s will  abide to  each EE’s regulations, that 
can be found in annex 29. The EEs will be accountable to the sound management of funds to the AE  while 
applying its internal  control of procedures, guaranteeing the adequate internal control for operability of 
administrative areas .

G.4.  Disclosure of funding proposal  
Note: The Information Disclosure Policy (IDP) provides that the GCF will apply a presumption in favour of disclosure 
for all information and documents relating to the GCF and its funding activities. Under the IDP, project and 
programme funding proposals wi ll be disclosed on the GCF website, simultaneous with the submission to the Board, 
subject to the redaction of any information that may not be disclosed pursuant to the IDP. Information provided in 
confidence is one of the exceptions, but this exception should not be applied broadly to an entire document if the 
document contains specific, segregable portions that can be disclosed without prejudice or harm.  
 
Indicate below whether or not the funding proposal includes confidential information.  
 
☒ No confidential information:  The accredited entity confirms that the funding proposal, including its annexes, may be 
disclosed in full by the GCF, as no information is being provided in confidence.  
☐ With confidential information: The accredited entity decl ares that the funding proposal, including its annexes, may 
not be disclosed in full by the GCF, as certain information is being provided in confidence. Accordingly, the accredited 
entity is providing to the Secretariat the following two copies of the fundi ng proposal, including all annexes:  
  full copy for internal use of the GCF in which the confidential portions are marked accordingly, together with 
an explanatory note regarding the said portions and the corresponding reason for confidentiality under the 
accredited entity's disclosure policy, and  
  redacted copy for disclosure on the GCF website.  
The funding proposal can only be processed upon receipt of the two copies above, if containing confidential 
information.

Secretariat ’s assessment  of FP174 
Proposal name:  Ecosystem -based Adaptation to increase climate resilience in 
the Central American Dry Corridor and the Arid Zones of the 
Dominican Republic   
Accredited entity:  Central American Bank for Economic Integration (CABEI)  
Country/(ies):  Costa Rica, El Salvador, Guatemala, Honduras, Nicaragua, 
Panama and Dominican Republic  
Programme/programme size:  Large  
I. Overall assessment of the Secretariat   
1. The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following 
remarks:  
Strengths  Points of caution  
The programme  design , featuring  three  different  
financing  facilities (loan facility, grant facility and 
guarantee facility) , will encourage participation of 
the private sector in ecosystem- based adaptation 
(EbA ) type of investments and diversify financial 
systems in the Central American region to create 
an enabling environment for investment in EbA 
and water /energy -efficient technologies  at scale.  The implementation arrangements include 
several different entities and therefore  result in 
complex contractual obligations and  higher 
project management costs .  
The technical assistance facility is tailored to 
increase technical capacities of local governments, 
farmers and non -regulated financial institutions  
to monitor and assess the results of  the 
implementation of EbA and water/energy -
efficient investments  as well as document the 
economic and ecosystem benefits  of these 
investments . GCF and CABEI will be  lending to different entities 
and therefore GCF  will be taking higher risk s. 
Checks and balances will be further defined in the 
term sheet .  
Target catchments  were selected following a 
rigorous vulnerability assessment that included 
hydrological modelling of the three watersheds  
and extensive consultations with key

2. The Board may wish to consider approving this funding proposal with the terms and 
conditions listed in the term sheet and addendum XVIII, titled “List of proposed conditions and 
recommendations”, respectively.  
II. Summary of the Secretariat ’s assessment  
2.1 Programme  background  
3. Background/history of the programme  to inform assess ment of  the funding 
proposal:  The term "Dry Corridor" emerged in the last three decades to describe the increasing 
frequency and intensity of droughts in the region. With more than one million families in the 
Central American Dry Corridor and the arid zones of the Dominican Republic  reliant on 
agriculture for their livelihoods, the impacts of increasingly severe droughts over the past five 
years have resulted in the need for considerable increase in climate finance in the region . In 
2014 alone, intense drought resulted in an estimated U SD 465 million worth of agricultural 
losses across the region, with more than 2.5 million people experiencing food insecurity. From 
2015 to 2018, food insecurity has increased annually in the region, with indigenous populations, 
women and rural communities  the most likely to suffer from chronic hunger and malnutrition. 
By the last quarter of 2018, 2.2 million people had experienced crop losses that year, with 1.4 
million urgently needing food assistance. These impacts are expected to continue to worsen 
unde r future climate scenarios.  
4.  EbA measures, such as forest conservation, reforestation, planting of  agroforestry 
systems, improved irrigation systems and instalment of vegetative barriers , result in substantial 
improvements for all key hydrological variable s in watershed areas  where such agriculture 
activities take place . Furthermore, EbA measures result in s oil humidity improvements and 
benefits for water availability for both agriculture and forests . The changes in water production 
(surface water) and percolation (water recharge) show even greater benefits from the 
implementation of a combination of EbA measures. The Dry Corridor and a rid zones experience 
significant water shortages, and EbA  is seen as key to ensuring water availability throughout the 
year. Lastly, the implementation of this combination of EbA measures also shows very positive 
impacts on soil erosion rates.  
5. The programme  will address the following barriers : i) access to credit for adaptation 
interventions among vulnerable populations; i i) access to financial resources for the 
implementation of EbA, water - and energy -efficient technologies among the private sector; iii) 
knowledge and understanding of climate change impacts as well as appropriate adaptation 
measures among decision -makers, communities and commercial farmers; iv) limited 
integration of EbA and other adaptation measures into sectoral policies; v) technical capacity within governments and local communities to implement EbA and other adaptation measures; 
and vi) development of e conomic incentives for investment in sustainable natural resource 
management and EbA.  
6. Without comprehensive adaptation interventions, agricultural losses will continue to 
increase, along with ecosystem loss, water shortages, poverty and food insecurity. These impacts will ultimately increase the exposure and sensitivity of communities in the Dry Corridor and arid zones who are already extremely vulnerable to climate change.  
7. Climate objective: The Central American Dry Corridor and the a rid zones of the 
Domi nican Republic are among the world's most vulnerable tropical regions to the impacts of 
climate change , with  the Dominican Republic, El Salvador, Guatemala, Honduras and Nicaragua 
recogni zed as being among the 16  most vulnerable countries in the world to extreme climate 
events for the period 1998 –2017. Currently, the Dry Corridor encompasses ~64  per cent  of the 
municipalities in Central America and is expected to extend to ~85  per cent  by 2050 . Climate


---
*Page 117*
---

 
GCF/B. 30/02/Add. 06 
Page 117 
 
 
projections in the region indicate that, by the end of  the century, temperatures will increase by 
3–3.5  °C under Representative Concentration Pathway 4.5 and by 6 –7 °C under Representative 
Concentration Pathway 8.5. At the same time, mean annual rainfall is programme d to decrease 
by 11  per cent  on average. Th ese changes will be compounded by further changes in the El 
Niño -La Niña  cycle, which will result in prolonged droughts as well as more frequent and 
extreme rainfall events across the region. If average global temperatures reach 1.5  °C above pre -
industrial  levels, extreme El Niño events are likely to happen twice as often , approximately 
every 10 years as opposed to every 20 years , and have severe consequences for agriculture and 
food security  in the region.  
8. The hydrological model ling undertaken for the programme  areas has demonstrated that 
a combination of adaptation interventions, such as forest conservation, reforestation, transition 
to agroforestry systems, introduction of irrigation systems and vegetative barriers,  would result  
in substantial improvements for all key hydrological variables in the selected catchment areas . 
In response to the above- described climate risks, GCF financ ing will be used to create a 
paradigm shift in the way EbA investments are deployed at a regional level in the Dry Corridor 
and arid zones. This shift will see governments of the seven participating countries—Costa Rica, 
the Dominican Republic, El Salvador, Guatemala, Honduras, Nicaragua and Panama —promoting 
large -scale EbA  initiatives . In addition , sma llholder  farmers  and commercial farmers, as well as 
entrepreneurs in rural communities, will have increased access to the financial resources and 
technical skills required to implement EbA interventions.  
9. Together, these interventions will catalyse the autonomous upscaling of EbA and water - 
and energy -efficient technologies by combining technical capacity -building, policy instruments 
and accessible financial mechanisms. This approach will transform the financial landscape in 
the region to enable vulnerable groups to access climate finance and implement climate -
resilient measures to strengthen their adaptive capacity. The proposed programme  will directly 
benefit ~522,200 people across the target catchments th rough on -the-ground interventions, 
with ~286,000 of these people also able to access loans for the implementation of adaptation measures through financial mechanisms established under the programme . 
10. Financing information and  environmental and social safeguards ( ESS)  category:  A 
combination of GCF loan and grant finance, a guarantee, and  a senior loan provided as co -
finance will be used to establish three financial facilities at the regional level, namely: i) a grant facility to support bottom -up selec tion and promotion of local EbA activities through non -
reimbursable financing (US D 28 million); ii) an EbA lending facility for small - and large -scale 
EbA investments at farm -, enterprise - and household -level (US D 102 million); and iii) a 
guarantee facilit y that will provide an additional layer of security to financial institutions in 
order to promote EbA finance (US D 30 million). The facilities will be managed by CABEI with a 
blended financing structure, where funds in the form of senior loans are channelled via CABEI 
and intermediary financial institutions (IFIs) to partner financial institutions ( PFIs ) towards 
end beneficiaries . The loan facility is structured for GCF to assume the credit risk of the P FIs 
using a limited recourse loan. By taking this addi tional risk  compared to CABEI which  is lending 
to larger IFIs,  GCF will lower the interest rate to the PFIs and ultimately the end borrowers, 
improving access to affordable finance for smallholders in the target catchments. The guarantee facility will enco urage the IFIs and PFIs to mobilize more of their own resources, while also 
allowing them to lend to more vulnerable borrowers who  would not otherwise be able to meet 
their collateral requirements.  Total GCF financing is USD 84.2 million in the form of grants and 
USD 60 million in the form of loans, with co -financing from countries of USD 51.2  million in the 
form of in -kind and USD 4 2.8million in the form of senior loan , thus bringing the co -financing 
ratio to 1: 0.51 . The programm e environmental and social safeguards  category is IS -2. The entity 
has submitted relevant documentation ( an environmental and social management framework ) 
as part of the f unding proposal package.

2.2 Component -by-component analysis 
11. The programme  will target seven catchments for programme  interventions , one in each 
of the participating countries (except for the Dominican Republic,  which will target two  
catchment areas) . The programme 's regional approach will create an enabling environment for 
scalable and sustainable catchment and water demand management, facilitating large- scale 
investment in and implementation of EbA and water -efficient technologies. The total grant 
request of US$84.3 million will be used in the following ways: i) US$10.0 million  will be used to 
strengthen the technical capacity of local government, farmers and rural communities to 
implement EbA and other adaptation measures through the activities comprised in output 1; ii) 
US$ 28.8 million will be used to implement large- scale ad aptation interventions — including 
EbA, water - and resource -efficient technologies — in rural communities across the region 
throughout output 2; iii) US$28 million will form the grant facility through which vulnerable 
communities can access on -grants to fo ster the creation of a business ecosystem around EbA 
available technologies as per activity 1.2.; iv) US$6.3 million will be used to generate and share information on climate change adaptation and finance across the region, and mainstream it into 
local and  national policies within the output 3; v) US$6.4 million will be used to set up the 
financial mechanisms and strengthen the technical capacity of CABEI accredited and non -
regulated financial institutions to access and channel funds through them throughout  output 4; 
vi) US$3.8 million will be used for overall management and monitoring of programme activities 
(Independent evaluations and PMC costs); and vii) US$0.7 million is requested for contingency.  
The EbA lending facility (EbA credit line) will be estab lished and operationalised with the GCF 
senior loan provision of US$ 60 million and CABEI US$ 42.8  million co -finance together being 
offered to PFIs as blended finance mechanism at below -market conditions for small - and large -
scale EbA investments at the far m-, enterprise - and household -level.  
Component 1: Mainstreaming of EbA, water -efficient technologies and natural resource -based 
businesses into selected catchments (total cost: USD 121 million; GCF cost: USD 72.6  million, or  60 
per cent ) 
12. The component includes the mainstreaming of EbA and water - and energ y-efficient 
technologies through further strengthening of technical capacities of local governments, farmers and rural communities. A community -based approach will be adopted to identify site -
specific interventions in each of the 36 municipalities targeted  by the programme during its first 
year . This will be supported by the establishment of community -level monitoring and 
evaluation committees, which will promote country ownership and sustainability of programme 
interventions. The technical capacity of these committees will be strengthened to enable the 
monitoring of biophysical, social and economic conditions in their local catchment areas, as well 
as to assess the level of climate vulnerability, risks and opportunities.  
13. Technical assistance will also be p rovided to farmers and rural communities in the 36 
municipalities for the implementation of on -the-ground EbA practices, including silvopasture, 
agroforestry and sustainable land management1. It will support the establishment of natural 
resource -based busi nesses (NRBs), which will support the implementation of tree nurseries and 
the manufacturing, distribution or repair of water -efficient technologies  as well as the  non-
destructive use of ecosystem goods and services provided through EbA, such as businesses 
developed around the ecotourism industry  or non -timber forest products.  
14. The technical assistance under this component will also support the integration of EbA 
in policy and regulatory frameworks at the local level focusing mainly on water management 
regulations and water management community structures as well as local land -use planning

knowledge to do so.

instruments. Country -specific implementation protocols will be developed for: i) the 
implementation of sustainable EbA practices; ii) the adoption of water -efficient technologies; 
and iii) the adoption of energy -efficient technologies that reduce fuel wood demand.  
15. A USD 28 million grant facility will be established to support site -specific EbA and 
water -/energy -efficient technologies for the most vulnerable communities, which  are unable to 
access loan finance. Based on approved eligibility criteria, a selected set of interventions will be 
financed through this facility. On -the-ground EbA -oriented grant support will benefit 
community -based institutions including mancomunidades ,2 municipal associations, farmers’  
cooperatives, solution providers and /or civil society organiz ations. Eligible programmes will 
include community -level investments, the establishment of water funds, seed funding for 
payment for ecosystem services and support for the adoption of selected EbA practices and/or 
water - and energy -efficient technologies.  
16. Under component 1, a regional knowledge hub will also be established. The knowledge 
hub will act as a repository for EbA information, includi ng, but not limited to, all protocols 
developed by the programme as well as the best practices and lessons learned collected through 
the programme's monitoring and evaluation processes  and previous and ongoing 
complementary programmes. This will include gu idance on how to access opportunities for 
accessing finance provided through the programme, with specific focus on women's economic 
empowerment. Researchers will be hired in every country to assist with the synthesis of 
findings from catchments, programme advances, impact s and experiences learned for 
dissemination across the region.  
17. Lastly, this component will support the preparation of a methodology for the valuation 
of ecosystem services and development of account ing profiles for natural capital for integ ration 
into development plans in the Dry Corridor and a rid zones. The methodology will draw on best 
practices and lessons learned from ongoing initiatives, including the: i) "incentivo forestal" in 
Guatemala; ii) payment for environmental services in Costa  Rica a nd Panama; iii) Water Fund of 
the city of Quito in Ecuador; and iv) water funds established in cities in Costa Rica, the Dominican Republic and Guatemala. Ultimately, the valuation of ecosystem services will be used 
to promote the establishment of e conomic incentives for EbA investments such as  water funds 
and payments for environmental services.  
18. The f inal activity under this component includes large scale EbA landscape interventions 
financing through grant support. Financing will be provided to the most vulnerable 
communities in  the 36 municipalities3 that lack the capacity to access financing and equipment 
to implement adaptation interventions and cannot receive loans through the loan or grant facilities. Such landscape interventions include: i) est ablishing one tree nursery per municipality 
focusing on native species and fuelwood trees (36 total); ii) establishing 4,160 ha of forest 
protection zones across the seven catchment areas; iii) protecting 1,820  ha of natural forest in 
major recharge areas and riparian zones, including assisted regeneration;  iv) restoring 5,170 ha 
of natural forest areas across the seven catchments; v) restoring 820 ha of native pine forests in 
Guatemala, Honduras and Nicaragua; vi) establishing 1,293 km of agroforestry systems using 
diversified living fence arrangements in basic grain crops; vii) establishing 612 ha of 
agroforestry systems for natural shade in coffee plantations; viii) establishing 1,280 km of 
silvopasture systems using diversified living fence arrangements; ix) establishing 1,280 ha of 
silvopasture systems across the 36 target municipalities; x) establishing 5,305 h a of sustainable 
fuelwood and timber plantations across the 36 target municipalities; xi) establishing 129 km of firebreaks for forests and plantations; xii) constructing 118 km of living barriers for soil 
conservation around agricultural plots;  and xiii) constructing 118 km of superficial drainage for

soil conservation  xiv) and establishing  109 rainwater harvesting systems , 156 community -level

Component 2: Financing and implementation for EbA as well as water - and energy -efficie nt 
technologies (total cost: USD 139 million ; GCF  cost : USD 96.4  million , or 70 per cent ) 
19. Under this  component the proposed programme  will establish and operationali ze 
financial mechanisms to facilitate investment in EbA practices, as well as the adoption of water - 
and energy -efficient technologies. This component will focus on providing grants and blended 
finance for EbA investments as well as water - and energy -effici ent technologies. Such 
mechanisms will promote bottom -up identification of adaptation interventions and  at the same 
time  de-risk financial mechanisms available for financing adaptation.  
20. Under this component, the EbA lending facility will be established and operationali zed 
with the GCF senior loan provision of US D 60 million and the CABEI US D 42.8  million co -finance 
offered to PFIs as  a blended finance mechanism at below -market conditions for small - and 
large -scale EbA investments at the farm -, enterprise-  and household -level.  By providing a 
blended financing mechanism composed of concessional finance from GCF in combination with 
a market rate senior debt as co -financing from CABEI , incentives are created for finan cial 
institutions on different levels to engage in EbA finance . IFIs in CABEI's established network will 
manage the EbA lending facility via the establishment of trusts for direct on -lending4 to local 
PFIs  such as cooperatives, small banks, NGOs and rural community banks, among others . PFIs in 
turn will on -lend to end beneficiaries. Investment s will be targeted towards community -level 
investment, rural micro, small and medium -sized enterprises  or other institutions that can 
foster EbA, as well as water - and energy -efficient technologies, for end beneficiaries across 
different sectors.  
21. A guarantee facility will be linked to the EbA lending facility to secure IFIs ' on-lending of 
CABEI’s co -financing funds a s well as  mobili ze additional lending from IFIs’ own resources and 
additional public and private investors’ resources by mitigating against credit risks associated 
with commercial EbA f inance. CABEI will manage the guarantee facility as a window in its 
recently established Guarantee Fund . The g uarantee facility will cover end beneficiary loans 
which lack real guarantee means ; where end beneficiaries are unable  to provide full coverage, 
for example  via real estate or movable assets , the guarantee facility will cover the missing 
participation and cover  up to 75  per cent  of the resulting loss in case of non -payment. Hence, i t 
enables more end beneficiaries to access EbA finance as well as increases  security of collateral 
provided from PFIs to IFIs in  the form of generated EbA lending portfolio.  
22. Last ly, to facilitate access to and operation of the facilities, these  will be accompanied by 
a technical assistance facility that will develop capacity within municipalities and PFIs to 
monitor and assess the implementation of EbA and water/resource -efficient  investment 
projects . Technical assistance will be a mandatory condition for PFIs to access the EbA lending 
facility. This will ensure that capacities, especially for adequate monitoring, reporting and 
verification systems, are in place to manage the EbA finance. Such technical assistance will focus 
on developing capacity within municipalities and PFIs to monitor and assess the 
implementation of EbA and water/energy -efficient investment programme s as well as to 
monitor and document the respective economic a nd ecosystem benefits. Where possible, 
information and c ommunication technologies will be introduced to provide data analytics and 
satellite data insights into the EbA lending portfolio and climate impacts on the portfolio.  
 
4 "On-lending " in this context refers to the process whereby selected PFIs will receive money via two mechanisms: 
from CABEI co -financing throu gh a senior loan as well as from IFI trust structures at below -market rates. They will 
then lend this money with its associated rates (on -lend) to farms, enterprises and households for implementation of 
EbA and other adaptation measures.
