III. Assessment  of performance  against investment criteria  
24. The programme  will combine investments in water supply and irrigation, agroforestry, 
silvopastoral and forestry systems, and farmer and institutional capacity -building  for climate 
resilience.  
3.1 Impact potential                                                           Scale: Medium to high  
25. The proposed programme 's contribution to climate -resilient sustainable development 
will directly benefit 1,084,441 people (50  per cent  women) across seven catchment areas. While 
USD 84.3 million in grants are geared towards concrete and tangible benefits for an estimated 
total of 949,747 beneficiaries via the strengthening of stakeholders’ capacities to better 
understand, develop and implement EbA solutions, a further US D 102 .8 million in debt finance 
will build upon such created capacities, with an estimated number of 766,634 d irect 
beneficiaries and 475,556 indirect beneficiaries (total of 1,08 4,441 ).The programme  will also 
indirectly benefit 1, 368 ,044 people across the region by facilitating increased access to finance 
for NRBs , mainstreaming EbA and other adaptation practices  into national policies and building 
the capacity of governments to implement EbA . 
26.  The programme  will reduce climate change vulnerability for farmers and producers’ 
organizations by improving ecosystem services, primarily water regulatio n. Impacts of climate 
change have been described in the proposal based on the existing information of regional 
clim ate projections and downscaled through hydrological models at the basin level for three 
catchments in Costa Rica, Honduras and the Dominican Republic. The results of the hydrological 
modelling developed show a consistent trend in the impacts of climate cha nge in substan tially  
decrease of precipitation that leads to reduced water yield, reduced water infiltration, 
groundwater storage and increased erosion risk. Models used for the simulation of various 
climate  change  impacts in the target locations and sugge sted adaptation  interventions provide 
for positive impacts over these variables when applied over climate change scenarios showing 
the capacity of these interventions to dramatically reduce the potential impacts of climate 
change.  
27. Furthermore, t he programm e includes the development of site -specific intervention 
plans through a ctivity 1.1. ( sub-activity 1.1.3) for each of the seven catchment areas as a first 
step in the implementation of the programme. Two draft sample site -specific intervention plans 
were d eveloped for Costa Rica and Honduras , and a methodology  to develop site-specific 
adaptation plans was  also clearly elaborated in the f unding proposal . Those site plans will be 
validated during the initial stages of implementation in consultation with key a ctors in both 
countries.  
28. The allocation of grants will  be based on th e impact and activities identified in the site 
specific adaptation plans,  and will follow a clear set of principles set out in the f unding proposal, 
which will ensure that activities to be funded contribute to a cohesive catchment approach 
targeting actions and reflect the principles that reduce pressure on ecosystems and the services 
they provide, as well as protecting, restoring or using biodiversity and ecosystems in a sustainable manner.  
29. With the financial support under this programme  it is estimated that the following  will 
be the  impact on the ecosystems and ecosystem services in the region : i)~142,500 ha of


---
*Page 122*
---

 
GCF/B. 30/02/Add. 06 
Page 122 
 
 
degraded agricultural land restored through the implementation  of sustainable land use 
practices;  ii) ~7,800 ha of forested areas  restored ; iii) ~4,100 ha of forest  protected; i v) 36 tree 
nurseries established ; v) ~1,900 ha of agroforestry systems established ; vi) ~100 km of 
firebreaks, living barriers and superficial drainage solutions  established ; and vi i) ~5,300 ha of 
sustainable fuelwood sources  developed . Finally, at least 100 community buildings will be 
equipped with rainwater harvesting systems, 156 community -level rainwater reservoirs will be 
install ed, 43 half -orange kilns will be made communally available, and 38 community -level 
solar -powered water pumping stations will be installed.  
3.2 Paradigm shift potential                                                         Scale: High  
30. The proposed programme  will catalyse a paradigm shift in the regional approach to 
catchment and water demand management in the Dry Corridor of Central America and the a rid 
zones of the Dominican Republic. This shift will see governments promoting large -scale EbA  
investments as we ll as increased private sector participation, including commercial farmers and 
entrepreneurs in rural communities  who would now have access to the financial resources and 
technical skills required to implement impactful adaptation interventions. The transf ormative 
effects of the programme  will be achieved through several innovative approaches  anchored into 
the programme  design.  
31. The programme  has been specifically designed to create an enabling environment that 
capitali zes on the scalability of EbA, focusin g on three core areas of support: financial,  policy 
and technical. The programme  will diversify financial systems in the region to create an 
enabling environment for investment in EbA and water -efficient technologies. This will be based 
on the establishment of several financial instruments with varying entry  points to make climate 
finance more accessible to vulnerable communities . The enabling environment for investment 
will be further enhanced by building the technical and institutional capacity of PFIs and non-
regulated financial institutions to finance and re -finance EbA and water -efficient technologies 
through all three of the proposed financial instruments.  
32. The enhanced local evidence base for EbA that will be created through the knowledge 
hub and the monitoring and evaluation of programme  activities will support national and loca l-
level decision -makers to implement policies and incentives for EbA, in particular through 
improved valuation of natural capital.  
33. Lastly, the programme  will catalyse the autonomous upscaling of EbA interventions and 
water -efficient technologies by combin ing policy instruments (e.g. economic incentives such as 
payments for environmental services) with accessible financial mechanisms (e.g. commercial loans, trust funds, microfinance and guarantee funds). These mechanisms will transform the 
financial landsca pe in the region to enable vulnerable groups to access climate finance. 
Furthermore, the programme  will promote the development of climate -resilient NRBs  and 
alternative livelihoods at the community level.  
3.3 Sustainable development potential                                             Scale:  High  
34. The programme  will have significant sustainable development potential, with 
environmental, social and economic  benefits . Some of the environmental co -benefits include 
conservation and/or restoration of forests and agroforestry systems that will act as natural 
buffers, controlling and mitigating the impact of floods, droughts, extreme temperatures and wildfires on ecosystems in the selected sites. Conserved and restored ecosystems will provide 
habitats for a range of terrestrial species, thereby improving biodiversity i n programme  sites.  
35. Social co -benefits will mostly contribute to social development by improving  the water 
and food security of vulnerable communities in the region. This includes improve ment of  the


---
*Page 123*
---

 
GCF/B. 30/02/Add. 06 
Page 123 
 
 
targeted communities' health by reducing the risk of water -borne diseases , increased 
productivity of agricultural systems, and reduced  inequality and poverty through  increased 
financial inclusion of those participating in the proposed financing schemes.  The programme  
will provide opportunities to develop sustainable NRBs  and alternative livelihoods in 
ecotourism and in the collection of non -timber forest products .  
3.4 Needs of the recipient                                                Scale: Medium to high  
36. In the last decade, the Latin America and Caribbean region —under which the seven 
beneficiary countries fall —has achieved an average annual economic growth rate of 3.2  per 
cent . This economic insecurity is compounded by climate change impacts, including extreme 
rainfall and droughts. Economically insecure households, including vulnerable rural 
communities, are often slow to recover effectively from climate impacts. Rural communit ies 
have little or no access to adaptation finance, which limits their adaptive capacity. By improving access to financial resources for adaptation interventions, the adaptive capacity of rural 
communities in the Dry Corridor will be increased. With increa sed financial and technical 
capacity, communities will be able to withstand the negative impacts of severe droughts.  
37. The n ational budgets of Dry Corridor countries are limited, and poverty eradication and 
sustainable development have been prioriti zed for f unding over CCA . There is a high 
dependency on non -reimbursable international support to meet CCA  goals across the Latin 
America and Caribbean  region where the finance available for CCA  is far below what is required 
to meet adaptation needs. The added burd en of the COVID -19 pandemic on the economies of 
these countries will leave even less funds available for CCA  in the participating countries.  The 
proposed programme  will provide access to technical and financial resources, knowledge 
products and government support structures that will enable rural communities to adapt 
effectively to climate change in the Dry Corridor.  
3.5 Country ownership                                               Scale: Medium to high  
38. CCA  is recogni zed as a priority in the Central American region and has been integrated 
into several regional and national policies and strategies , including  the Regional Climate Change 
Strategy (2010), Regional Strategy for the Conservation and Sustainable Use of Biodiversity in Mesoamerica, Regional S trategic Program for Forest Ecosystems Management, and Regional 
Agro -environmental and Health Strategy of Central America, among others .
  
39. In addition to this regional alignment, the programme  contributes to achieving national 
priorities and aligns with numerous policies in each of the seven target countries . A summary of 
how it meets each countr y’s national priorities is provided in table 3 of the f unding proposal . Of 
the seven  participating countries, three  have submitted draft country programmes to GCF as  
part of readiness grant deliverables to date, and two  of these  countries  (Guatemala and 
Honduras ) feature the current proposal in their c ountry programme. The f unding proposal is 
also part of the e ntity work programme of the AE , CABEI.  
40. All no-objection letters in support of this proposal have been provided.  
3.6 Efficiency and effectiveness                                    Scale:  Medium to high  
41. The AE provided  a comprehensi ve demand and supply analysis to justify the loan 
request as well as  the programme ’s financial models,  which  foresee direct financial and indirect 
economic benefits resulting from implementation of the programme ’s activities. The demand 
and supply analysis include s details on the absorption capacity at the country level and 
establish es a comparison with the numbers of expected loans and financial entities through this


---
*Page 124*
---

 
GCF/B. 30/02/Add. 06 
Page 124 
 
 
programme, which  provide s sufficient evidence that there is growing interest to engage in green 
finance by the financial institutions and that the financial infrastructure to serve the expected 
demand for EbA finance does exist , but could be strengthened. The financial internal rate of 
return  of the programme  stands at  10.3 per cent , net present value  (NPV ) at USD 491,083,311  
and an  economic internal rate of return  at 17.3 per cent , with an NPV of US D 2,255,887,065  
(both NPV discounted at an average of issued public bond rates as alternative investment).  
42.  While the grant solutions provide the basis for the establishment and expansion  of a 
body of knowledge in target regions, the debt solutions will be fully self -sustained on a 
commercial basis, targeting potential further investment into the mechanisms created. The 
senior loans that make up the debt finance will be supported by a guar antee fund, which will de -
risk the loans, creating incentives for financial institutions to engage in EbA finance, for which 
there exist s no or only limited offerings as of today.  
43. The deployment of the loan facility will follow a phased approach along the project 
implementation period to ensure the cohesive and meaningful impact of the financed activities 
as well as proper vulnerability assessments and identification of priorities for other vulnerable regions within the Dry Corridor where the intervention could be scaled up. This phased 
approach will also allow for more time to strengthen capacities of the financial institutions and 
to promote the financial mechanism with communities and producers. The first phase will be  
the launching of the facility, the second will be  the replicating phase and the third one will be 
the scale -up of the facility. Each of these phases will have eligibility criteria for beneficiaries and 
predefined areas of loan coverage that will also be guided by a number of milestones that will 
need to be met in order to release the amount for the following phase.  
44. The programme  has a co -financing ratio of 1:0.5 4. The target countries will make in -
kind contributions and CABEI will contribute grants and loans. Although the co -financing ratio 
is not high, t he programme  expects to leverage  significant funding from IFIs and PFIs  primarily 
from the USD 30  million  guarantee provided by GCF that will cover the credit risk of 25 –75 per 
cent  of PFI loan portfolios. It should also be noted that GCF is lending to the PFIs, and the PFIs 
will in turn lend to the end beneficiaries . Therefore,  the PFIs will  take the end beneficiary credit 
risk for the full programme  loan amount.    
45. The programme  will reach 1,084,441 direct beneficiaries and 1, 364,044  indirect 
beneficiaries for a total of 2,4448,485  beneficiaries. This equates to USD 120/beneficiary for the 
programme  and USD 79/beneficiary for GCF.  
IV. Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies  
4.1 Environmental and social safeguards   
46. Programme background . The programme aims to overcome the barriers to the 
adoption of climate adaptive approaches by strengthening the capacity of vulnerable people, 
including smallholder and commercial farmers, as well as entrepreneurs in the rural 
communities of the target a reas. The programme aims to contribute to the conservation and 
restoration of the degraded landscapes by introducing EbA approaches and other adaptation measures. It also aims to unlock financing for the adoption of sustainable resilient practices. The 
environmental co -benefits of the programme include the reduction of adverse impacts of floods, 
droughts, extreme temperatures and wildfires on the ecological habitats due to the buffering 
effect of the EbA interventions that could result in an overall increas e in terrestrial biodiversity. 
Improvements in soil fertility, water infiltration and soil moisture retention are also expected to reduce erosion and enhance water quality and availability. The promotion of natural resource -
based activities and alternative  livelihoods , including the adoption of water -efficient


---
*Page 125*
---

 
GCF/B. 30/02/Add. 06 
Page 125 
 
 
technologies , have expected social co -benefits of increased access to clean water, reduced 
exposure to waterborne diseases and improved food security.  
47. Environmental and social risk category and safegu ards instrument.  The 
programme is classified as Category B or Medium Risk by the AE. The Secretariat confirms the 
environmental and social risk categori zation since the potential risks and impacts are generally 
avoidable and can be mitigated effectively th rough the implementation of appropriate 
management measures. The AE has prepared an Environmental and Social Management 
Framework (ESMF) which contains the relevant legal and institutional frameworks, general 
environmental and social baseline in the target  countries, and provisions for stakeholder 
engagement and a grievance redress mechanism (GRM) . 
48. Compliance with GCF environmental and social safeguards (ESS) standards.  
Subparagraphs (a) –(h) below  summarize the programme’s compliance with  GCF ESS standards 
and requirements:  
(a) ESS 1: Assessment and management of environmental and social risks and 
impacts.  An ESMF has been prepared for the programme where the potential 
environmental and social impacts have been identified, assessed and ranked on their 
significance. The ESMF has also defined feasible mitigating measures that can be used to 
avoid and manage the environmental and social impacts posed by the programme. The 
ESMF also contains a detailed plan for the monitoring of critical environmen tal and 
social impacts and risks during the implementation of the programme ; 
(b) ESS 2: Labour and working conditions.  The programme will provide employment 
opportunities for the local populations and indigenous communities with a number of 
labourers involved in the implementation phase of the programme. The ESMF 
considered the potential risks and impacts of the activities on working conditions, terms 
of employment, workers ’ organization, non -discrimination, equal opportunit ies, child 
labour, and forced labour of direct, contracted and third -party workers. The ESMF 
provided for the development of a Labor Management Guide and a GRM  to address 
labour and working condition concerns of workers from the local population and of 
contractors ;  
(c) ESS 3: Resource efficiency  and pollution prevention. Through promotion of the EbA 
options and water -efficient technologies, the programme encourages sustainable 
restoration and management of forests, wetlands and agroforestry landscapes including 
ensuring sustainability of availabl e water. Nevertheless, the programme may result in 
the use of pesticides and fertiliz ers that may pollute land and water resources. The 
programme aims to address these impacts with the dissemination of specific guidelines 
on the use of pesticides and ferti lizers. The ESMF also indicates the development of an 
integrated pest management and agrochemicals use guidelines  for the programme 
including the establishment of an efficient solid and liquid waste management module for the programme ; 
(d) ESS 4: Community health, safety and security.  The inadequate handling of pesticides 
and fertili zers may likely expose communities to hazardous materials which can be 
harmful to their health and safety. However, as the ESMF indicates that an integrated 
pest management and agro chemicals use guidelines will be developed, the probability of 
the community being  exposed to these risks is low. The ESMF also makes proposals for 
mitigating public health risks from potential contamination of water in collection 
systems and the developme nt of an Emergency Response Plan should there be a need to 
develop one as assessed during programme execution ;  
(e) ESS 5: Land acquisition and involuntary resettlement. The programme does not 
envision the acquisition of land in the implementation of its activ ities. Nevertheless, the 
programme identifies the possibility for land use restrictions in implementing


---
*Page 126*
---

 
GCF/B. 30/02/Add. 06 
Page 126 
 
 
conservation and restoration of the forest areas in the target basins. The programme 
may also lead to modification of current livelihood sources and/or reduction of income 
as a result of  adopting sustainable practices and proposed EbA protocols. To mitigate 
this, the programme will implement inclusive consultation and decision -making 
process es such that affected communities will be kept well informed with  the view to 
them being agreeable on the proposed interventions. Further, the programme will develop an economic compensation strategy should the need arise;  
(f) ESS 6: Biodiversity conservation and sustainable management of living natural 
resources. The programme activities are expected to enhance biodiversity through 
restoration, reforestation and adoption of sustainable EbA interventions. Nevertheless, 
the establishment of new mixed plantations of native species and agroforestry systems 
including the development of sustainable sources of firewood can potentially negatively 
affect the ecosystem if improperly managed and implemented. For instance, mixed 
species plantations and restoration of the agroforestry systems may induce increased 
use of agrochem icals. Uncontrolled sourcing of firewood for charcoal may also 
exacerbate deforestation and expansion of agricultural lands instead of increasing reforestation. To mitigate these unwanted outcomes , trainings related to biodiversity 
conservation, efficient resource management and pollution prevention will be 
implemented. Technical support on sustainable practices such as on the use of organic 
fertili zers, vermicomposting, soil conditioning and other agrosilvopastoral management 
practices will be encouraged. Further, should significant risks and impacts related to biodiversity be identified, the ESMF provides for a Biodiversity Management Plan that 
can be prepared and implemented ; 
(g) GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy and ESS 7: Indigenous peoples.  The program me will 
affect indigenous peoples in some of the countries in the Dry Corridor and a rid zones 
where the programme is to be implemented. The AE recogni zes that there is a risk of 
non- recognition or alteration of indigenous people's livelihood strategies, cultural 
practices, religious beliefs or governance structures, increased restrictions on land  use 
for indigenous peoples and unequal opportunities for indigenous peoples. Where 
indigenous people s are potentially affected by the project, the AE has indicated that it  
will require an Indigenous Peoples Plan (IPP) to be developed that will mitigate 
potential adverse effects on indigenous peoples and to improve positive impacts. The 
guidelines contained in the ESMF for these IPPs importantly describe the process of 
conti nuous free, prior and informed consent (FPIC) consultations and the participation 
of indigenous peoples (including both men and women) in the project implementation 
and operation processes and emphasizes that FPIC will be used in all cases, even where the legislation refers only to "consultation". Also, any IPP must develop a culturally 
appropriate strategy that clearly describes measures to enable indigenous peoples to 
take advantage of the opportunities generated by the project and sustainably preserve 
and manage the use of natural resources on which they depend. The GCF Independent 
Redress Mechanism and the Secretariat’s indigenous peoples’ focal point will be available for assistance at all stages, including before a claim has been made, as required 
by p aragraph 70 of the GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy ; and  
(h) ESS 8: Cultural heritage.  Some of the regions where the programme will be 
implemented may have cultural value and  may be implemented  in communities that 
have various cultural practices. Therefore, the p rogramme ensures that this  tangible and 
intangible cultural heritage is adequately assessed and will not be adversely affected. 
The ESMF provides for continued access to any cultural heritage that may be affected by 
the programme including continued access to these sites and the use of cultural 
practices and religious beliefs during implementation.


---
*Page 127*
---

 
GCF/B. 30/02/Add. 06 
Page 127 
 
 
49. Implementation arrangements. CABEI as the AE will be responsible for overseeing the 
implementation of the programme. A Regional Project Management Unit (RPMU) will be based 
at one of the e xecuting entity’s (EE) regional headquarters in Panama and will be responsible 
for the daily project administration, execution and regional technical coordination of project 
activities. Various technical specialists will be bas ed at the RPMU including a Regional 
Environmental and Social Safeguards Officer. Each country is expected to have a national officer 
on Gender, Social Engagement and Safeguards, who will have the responsibility of ensuring that the ESMF is put in place in -country.  
50. Stakeholder engagement.  The programme has conducted an extensive community 
engagement with various stakeholders that enabled them to opine on the various components 
of the programme and how the programme is going to affect them from all perspectiv es during 
programme development. It has also developed a stakeholder engagement plan which serves as 
the programme’s participation strategy and guidance for stakeholder participation and 
consultation throughout the life of the programme.  
51. Grievance redress mechanism (GRM).  The AE has an established a GRM  which 
provides for avenues where stakeholders and potentially affected communities can provide 
feedback or grievances, and receive responses, with regard to the implementation of the 
programme. The ESMF also  provides for the establishment of a four -tier activity level GRM to 
receive complaints and claims, collect, store and distribute information, resolve issues with corresponding follow -up, monitoring and reporting and finally inform and communicate to 
conce rned stakeholders.  
4.2 Gender policy   
52. The AE has submitted a gender assessment and gender action plan ; therefore , it 
complies with the requirements of the GCF Gender Policy . The gender assessment was 
completed based on a desk review, primary data collection and stakeholder consultation at various levels including women and other vulnerable groups.   
53. Cultural practices , patriarchal values  and existing customary and statutory law s on 
marriage, divorce, inheritance, custody and land tenure have significant implications on access, 
use and control of resources by women and men. Feminization of poverty, sociocultural and 
traditional beliefs, inadequate basic social services, low repre sentation of women in politics  and 
violence against women, among others, are existing barriers to achieving gender equality and women’s empowerment throughout the Dry Corridor. Social and cultural norms in D ry Corridor 
countries play a key role in creating  greater vulnerability and gaps in the realization of women's 
and girls' human rights and especially in rural and indigenous contexts. Women and girls 
especially continue to assume primary responsibility for unpaid family care and domestic work , 
and climat e change is increasing the workload of women and girls as they are responsible 
mainly for the search for food, water and biomass for energy on a daily basis. About 60  per cent  
of the population of the Dry Corridor lives in extreme poverty and women are 
disproportionately represented in the poorest of the poor. The most severe poverty is located in 
El Salvador , Guatemala, Honduras and Nicaragua  and has an amplified impact in rural areas. The 
feminization of poverty is very significant and is exacerbated by t he lack of access to services 
including health, education, water and transportation This limit s entrepreneurship 
opportunities for women and is being exacerbated by the impacts of climate change on 
agriculture, livestock and fisheries. Women face discrimin ation in access to resources and 
control of land and property and this limits access to financing, training programs,  and 
membership in farmers' cooperatives and local development organizations and their role in 
decision -making. The participation of women producers at the local level within producer 
associations, irrigation organization s, neighbo uring boards  and water committees is limited and 
their presence in positions of social and political leadership is still clearly a minority.


---
*Page 128*
---

 
GCF/B. 30/02/Add. 06 
Page 128 
 
 
54. Women and girls are mig rating to escape the extreme poverty derived from drought , 
loss of crops  and livestock and , above all,  lack of access to food. Sex disaggregated data is 
limited , including information on land tenure, participation in decision -making, the level and 
characteristics of the types of agricultural and livestock activities they carry out, their 
participation in activities and their access to credits or other financial activities, among other 
important aspects. There is a worrying increase and sharpening of violenc e against women , 
including slavery and sexual torture, throughout the D ry Corridor region amid a culture that 
tolerates and normalizes violence against women. In general, there are few institutions that 
offer protection to women and girls in situations of gender -based violence  (GBV) . Forced 
pregnancies of adolescent girls and forced and early marriages and unions are also common in 
many areas within the Dry Corridor. There is a migration crisis stemming from poverty, 
violence and the impacts of climate chan ge.  
55. The AE has provided a gender action plan and therefore complies with the requirement 
of the G ender Policy of GCF . The gender action plan provides activities that address the 
challenges faced by women and includes baseline  data , indicators, targets, timelines and 
budgets. The programme will include seven  full-time national gender experts and one regional 
gender expert to ensure gender issues are integrated throughout the project, including gender 
expertise in decision -making bod ies. Activities in the gender action plan  are designed to address 
gender gaps in a number of areas. One focal area is designed to generate enabling conditions for women´s access to financial schemes, which facilitates access to credit for women producers 
and women's cooperatives to help strengthen women's entrepreneurship initiatives at the local 
level and with CCA  and mitigation activities. This is complemented by building  the capacity and 
awareness of public officials in the municipalities involved in the  programme on the importance 
of the gender approach and how to incorporate it into agricultural public policies and take 
specific actions to strengthen the capacities of municipality technicians on social  and 
environmental resilience and gender issues. Pro tocols will be developed to address the specific 
barriers women face in  the adoption of efficient water technologies and ensure the inclusion of 
measures to ensure effective involvement of women in water committees. The programme will 
also develop actions with municipalities to facilitate the legalization of property cooperatives 
and women's community organizations and strengthen the technical capacities of women's 
organizations at the local level. Measures will be taken to ensure the active participation o f 
women in producers'  organizations. In general the programme will generate spaces of 
empowerment for women producers that ensure the inclusion of their priorities into decision -
making. The programme will promote quota systems for the representation of wom en in 
community and producer organizations related to the programme. T o complement these 
actions, the programme will generate community dialogue spaces, which  will include 
discussions among men about positive masculinities. Finally, the programme will main stream 
gender and sex disaggregated data into monitoring systems and decision -making. The 
programme will be conducting more country and programme -specific needs assessments to 
collect more information and incorporate these into country and programme -level gender 
analys es and action plans.  
56. Since GBV has been identified as a critical challenge, the programme is strongly 
encouraged to take measures to minimize the risk of increased GBV as a result of project 
intervention and develop measures to ensure that wom en have equal access to the grievance 
mechanism that will provide all female beneficiaries with a clear and safe process for reporting 
incidents of GBV. The AE is also encouraged to conduct a full analysis of the enabling 
environment (inc luding  international conventions ratified, legal, policy and institutional 
support framework, national strategic development plans, etc.) that demonstrates  the 
commitment to the pursuit of gender equality of the countries involved in this programme.    
57. In general, the gende r action plan  includes measures designed to address barriers to 
gender equality through addressing biased norms, increase women’s engagement in decision -
making, reduce gender gaps and support progress towards a more gender -equal society.

4.4 Fiduciary   
58. The EE s in the project are FAO, the Central -American Commission for Environment and 
Development (CCAD) and  CABEI , which will also perform the role of AE in the project.  
59. As an EE of the project, FAO will play a role in the execution of capacity -building, 
technica l assistance and implementation of the EbA  demonstration sites throughout the region 
for outputs 2 and 4 of the programme. CCAD, on the other hand, will be involved in the political 
coordination among participant countries through the direct execution of o utput 3 , which  is 
focused on gathering and disseminating key results and analys es derived from the execution of 
the project with the aim of generating evidence to inform and mainstream adaptation into key 
national policies and regional strategies. Addition ally, t he United Nations Environment 
Programme (UNEP) will provide technical advice to CCAD for the development of technical 
knowledge products on financial mechanisms for EbA, methodologies for ecosystem services 
valuation and technical guidelines for EbA  implementation.  
60. In its role as AE, CABEI will be responsible for overseeing the implementation, financial 
management, evaluation, reporting and closure of the project . As an EE, CABEI will be 
responsible for the set -up and management of the three financi al facilities of the project under 
component 1 , with FAO as EE for the technical assistance facility supported by UNEP as 
technical advisor.  
61. The programme will have a Regional Project Steering Committee (RPSC) comprising  of 
representatives from FAO, CCAD, CABEI  and UNEP and the regional project manager  (PM) . The 
RPSC will primarily be responsible for providing technical, oversight and advisory support at the regional level,  which will include: i) overseeing project implementation; ii) review and 
validation  of annual regional workplans and project reports; iii) review and validation of the 
project's fulfilment of environmental and financial objectives; and iv) approval of changes to the 
project's targets, activities or timelines. In addition, the RPSC will b e informed about the final 
selection of PFIs , which will be done by CABEI following internal regulations and criteria , as 
well as make a recommendation on the final selection of recipients of grants through the g rant 
facility. In its central role, the RPSC  will ensure consistent project progress across all seven 
countries. The RPSC will also be responsible for overseeing the Regional Project Management 
Unit (RPMU).  
62. The RPMU will be based in FAO’s regional headquarters in Panama and will be 
responsible for the day -to-day project administration and execution and the regional technical 
coordination of project activities. It will ensure that project execution proceeds through clear 
workplans, te rms of reference and carefully designed administrative arrangements that meet 
CABEI and GCF requirements. For the duration of the project, a PM will head the RPMU and will be responsible for the daily on -the-ground implementation and management of the proj ect. The 
PM will be responsible for ensuring the project achieves the targets set out in the Results 
Framework to the required standards of CABEI and GCF and within the specified time and 
budget allocations. The PM will: i) report directly to the RPSC on p roject management -related 
matters; ii) manage the project in accordance with the specified workplans and allocated budget; iii) ensure that all project interventions are implemented according to CABEI and GCF 
guidelines; iv ) work closely with Project Execu tion Units (PEUs) and national institutions and 
authorities to ensure that the project is managed effectively and that the needs of all beneficiary 
groups are considered; and v) oversee the efficient and effective information and knowledge 
transfer to rele vant project partners.

4.5 Results monitoring and reporting  
68. The Theory of Change clearly explains how the results chain will cascade from the goal 
statement of the proj ect to the proposed activities and properly articulates the relevant 
interlinkages among logic levels, barriers and risks. The " if, then, because"  logic is clearly 
formulated in the diagram provided in the funding proposal, and the stated results and outcomes are defined in a manner that are adequately supportive of meeting the ultimate 
project goal.  
69. As an adaptation project, the intervention expects to benefit 1,084,441 direct and 
1,364,044 indirect beneficiaries, of which women comprise 30  per cent  of the  direct and 40 per 
cent  of the indirect beneficiaries as per the gender -disaggregated metrics for the GCF core 
indicator.  
70. The logical framework is assessed by the Secretariat to have applied results 
measurements against the GCF results management framework /performance measurement

considerations. Comments and requests for final minor revisions have been communicated to the AE , and the Secretariat  expect s that there will not be any difficulties from the AE side to 
reflect these suggestions for further improvements.  
71. The implementation timetable has been provided in a format that would enable 
progress assessment during the implementation period, with the milestones and deliverables 
properly integrated to determine implementation performance in concrete terms.

72. The Accreditation Master Agreement was signed with the Accredited Entity on 6 
September 2017 (the “ AMA ”), and it became effective on 8  November 2017.  
73. The Accredited Entity has not provided a legal opinion/certificate confirming that it has 
obtained all internal approvals and it has the capacity and authority to implement the 
programme. It is recommended that, prior to submission of the F unding Proposal to the Board 
(a) the Accredited Entity has obtained all its internal approvals and (b) the Fund has received a 
certificate or legal opinion from the Accredited Entity in form and substance satisfactory to the Fund confirming that all final internal approvals by the Accredited Entity have been obtained 
and that it has the authority and capacity to implement the programme. 
 
74. The proposed programme will be implemented in seven countries, out of which in six 
countries GCF is not provided with privileges and immunities, while in one country an 
agreement on privileges and immunities has been concluded but has not entered into force yet. 
This means that, amongst other things, GCF is not protected against litigation or expropriation 
in these countries  which risks need to be further assessed.  
(a) The Republic of Costa Rica : The Secretariat sent a draft agreement together with a 
background note on privileges and immunities to the NDA in May 2016. The Secretariat 
met with AILAC delegations during COP 22 in N ovember 2016. In March 2019, the 
Secretariat has sent a formal reply to the ‘Note form costa Rica’, in English and Spanish, informing that English is the working language for future negotiations. To date, the 
negotiations are still ongoing.  
(b) The Dominican Republic : The Secretariat met with the Dominican Republic delegation 
during COP 21 in December 2015 after which a draft agreement with background note 
on privileges and immunities was sent in January 2016. The Secretariat sent the latest 
draft agreement to gether with background notes on P&Is to the Counsel to the President 
and the Ambassador to the United States in October 2017. In December 2020, the 
Secretariat sent another latest draft agreement to the Vice Minister of the Ministry of 
Environment and prop osed having a conference call.  However, no formal response has 
been received so far.  
(c) The Republic of El Salvador : The Secretariat sent a draft agreement together with 
background notes on P&Is to the NDA in September 2015. The Secretariat met with El 
Salv ador delegation during COP 21 in December 2015, after which the draft agreement 
with background note was resent to the NDA. In October 2018, the Secretariat met with NDA to provide information about P&I Agreement and the rationale for P&Is and 
clarified qu eries regarding the importance of signing P&I Agreements. However, no 
formal response has been received so far.  
(d) The Republic of Honduras : The Secretariat met with the Honduras delegation during 
COP 21 in December 2015 and a draft agreement and background note on privileges and 
immunities were sent in December 2015. The Agreement on Privileges and Immunities 
was concluded and signed on 17 March 2016, but the Agreement is not in force yet. The 
Secretariat sent an inquiry regarding the entry in force of the a greement in July 2020. 
However, no formal response has been received so far.  
(e) The Republic of Guatemala: The Secretariat sent a draft agreement together with 
background notes on privileges and immunities to the NDA in September 2015. The 
Secretariat met wi th a representative of Guatemala during COP 22 in November 2016. 
Latest draft agreement together with background notes on privileges and immunities 
were sent to the Foreign Minister in October 2017. However, no formal response has 
been received so far.  
(f) The Republic of Panama: The Secretariat sent a draft agreement together with 
background notes on privileges and immunities to the Minister of Presidency in


---
*Page 132*
---

 
GCF/B. 30/02/Add. 06 
Page 132 
 
 
September 2017. In October 2018, the Secretariat met with the NDA and provided 
information about the d raft agreement and its rationale. The Secretariat has sent a 
follow up email in November 2020; however, no formal response has been received so 
far.   
(g) The Republic of Nicaragua: The Secretariat sent a draft agreement on privileges and 
immunities together w ith background notes to the NDA in April 2018. The NDA has 
confirmed receipt of the Agreement but indicated that it would need to pass through the 
review and approval of the Congress, which is a lengthy process. The NDA requested the 
Secretariat to provide a non -official Spanish translation of the document in October 
2018 to expedite the process; however, no formal exchanges has occurred so far.  
75. The Heads of the Independent Redress Mechanism (IRM) and Independent Integrity 
Unit (IIU) have both expressed th at it would not be legally feasible to undertake their redress 
activities and/or investigations, as appropriate, in countries where the GCF is not provided with 
relevant privileges and immunities. Therefore, it is recommended that disbursements by the 
GCF are made only after the GCF has obtained satisfactory protection against litigation and 
expropriation in the countries, or has been provided with appropriate privileges and immunities.  
76. The proposed programme envisages the implementation of a lending facili ty to be 
financed by a portion of the GCF funding and by the Accredited Entity’s co -financing, with such 
resources channelled through intermediary financial institutions (IFIs) in the host countries that will provide downstream loans to smaller partner fin ancial institutions (PFIs), which in 
turn will provide sub -loans to the final borrowers. In respect to its own co -financing, the 
Accredited Entity will enter into (full recourse) loan agreements with the IFIs. However, with respect to the GCF funding, a li mited recourse loan instrument is proposed between CABEI and 
the IFIs  for purposes of conserving and passing down the GCF concessionality to the PFIs and 
the final sub -borrowers. However, since the Accredited Entity  has not used a limited recourse 
loan i n its prior operations,  the applicable terms and conditions for such on -lending to the PFIs 
may be subject to further changes or revisions prior to the execution of the FAA (and thus be 
subject to the completion of the legal due diligence by the Secretari at).   
4.7 List of proposals conditions (including legal)

made  subject to the following conditions:

and substance satisfactory to the GCF Secretariat, within 120 days after Board approval, 
confirming that the Accredited Entity has obtained all final internal approvals needed by 
it and has the capacity and authority to implement the proposed programme;  
(b) Signature of the funded activity agreement in a form  and substance satisfactory to the 
GCF Secretariat within 180 days from the date of Board approval , or the date the 
Accredited Entity has provided a certificate or legal opinion confirming that it has 
obtained all final internal approvals  whichever is la ter; and  
(c) Completion of the legal due diligence to the satisfaction of the GCF Secretariat.

Independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment  of 
FP174 
Proposal name:  Ecosystem -based Adaptation to increase climate resilience 
in the Central American Dry Corridor and the Arid Zones of 
the Dominican Republic   
Accredited entity:  Central American Bank for Economic Integration (CABEI)  
Country/(ies):  Costa Rica, El Salvador, Guatemala, Honduras, Nicaragua, 
Panama and Dominican Republic  
Programme/programme size:  Large  
I. Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel  
1. Earlier versions of this funding proposal were submitted for review at the twenty -eighth 
and t wenty- ninth meetings of the Board (B.28 and  B.29), and did not receive the endorsement 
of the independent Technical Advisory Panel (TAP). In its assessments, the independent TAP 
raised various concerns and provided specific actionable feedback, including:  
(a) Completion of a catchment -specific climate rationale for each of the seven catchments 
where the Ecosystem- based Adaptation ( EbA)  demonstration interventions and grant 
facility will be applied, sourcing historical observed data for each catchment, and using  
these data to validate the choice of model that is used to make future projections about 
key climate parameters ; 
(b) Completion of a catchment -specific EbA plan for each of these seven catchments;   
(c) Clear articulation of the adaptation impact of the project as  per the GCF investment 
criteria indicators; and  
(d) Clear articulation of the relationship between the grant portion of the project, to be 
implemented in the seven target catchments of the participating countries, and the loan 
and guarantee portion of the pro ject, to be implemented across the entire Dry Corridor 
of the seven countries.  
2. The core concern raised by the independent TAP in its previous  assessment s was that 
the funding proposal  did not provide  sufficient site-specific  data to support the series of grant -
based EbA interventions proposed . The current funding proposal  provides some additional 
information around the availability of climate data and proposes an  ex-ante  validation process 
for interventions , which  is intended  to address  the concerns  of the  independent  TAP. The 
validation process , if implemented robustly , could ensure that proposed EbA interventions are 
supported by the scientifically determined adaptation needs of the impacted communities, among other things. This solution is not the independent TAP’s  preferred outcome as made 
clear in previous assessments , but it may suffice to meet a minimum standard for endorsement  
in this case . However, the funding proposal  does  not provide enough information on how th is 
validation process will be implemented and how it can assure GCF  that grant funding will only 
be disbursed for interventions that are supported by the evidence -based needs of the 
catchments. For that reason, an e ndorsement here must be conditional on greater clarity 
regarding the proposed validation process and satisfaction that it is fit -for-purpose.   
1.1 Funding proposal overview


---
*Page 134*
---

 
 GCF/B.30/02/Add.0 6 
Page 134 
 
 
3. The term “Dry Corridor ” describes arid zones in parts of Central America and the 
Caribbean ( Costa Rica, El Salvador, Guatemala, Honduras, Nicaragua, Panama and Dominican 
Republic ). The Central American Bank for Economic Integration (CABEI) , the accredited entity 
(AE), states in the funding proposal  that these zones are experienc ing increasing frequency and 
intensity of agricultural droughts  and heatwaves , as well as an increase in the frequency and 
severity of extreme rainfall  events  and hurricanes  in the wet season , causing  storm  damage  and 
flash floods (in turn causing  reductions in aquifer recharge)  – because of climate change. The 
funding proposal  reports that more than one million families in the Dry Corridor are reliant on 
agriculture for their livelihoods and are susceptible to the impacts of increasingly severe 
droughts. In 2014 alone, intense drought resulted in an estimated USD 465 million worth of 
agricultural losses across the relevant region, with more than 2.5 million people experiencing 
food insecurity. From 2015 to 2018 food insecurity has increased annually in the region. By the 
last quarter of 2018, 2.2 million people had experienced crop losses that year, with 1.4 million 
urgently needing food assistance. According to the AE, these impacts are expected to continue to 
worsen under future climate scenarios.  
4. The funding proposal  aims to use a mix of implementation grants, capacity -building 
grants, financial guarantees and on -lending to further E bA measures such as forest 
conservation, reforestation, planting of agroforestry systems, improved irrigation systems and 
installation  of vegetative barriers. These interventions are expected to mitigate the impacts of 
climate change in the Dry Corridor. The  total financing scale of the proposed program me is USD 
268.4 with GCF funding expected to make up 65  per cent  of this amount ( USD 174.3).  
5. The funding proposal  seeks to address the above -described climate threats by focusing, 
in part, on seven targeted catchment areas (one in each of the countries in the Dry Corridor) and 
using a mix of grant and capacity -building approaches to promote EbA interventions in those 
catchments. Additionally, the funding proposal  aims to deploy loans and guarantees ( along with 
capacity grants) across the entire region to finance interventions that would improve water 
demand management in the Dry Corridor , among other potential adaptation benefits. The goal,  
as laid out in the funding proposal , is to provide smallholde r and commercial farmers, as well as 
entrepreneurs in rural communities across the seven countries,  with increased access to the 
financial resources and technical skills required to implement adaptation interventions. The 
specific financial interventions p roposed are as follows:  
(a) The total grant request of USD 84.3 million will be used in the following ways according 
to the AE : (i) USD 6.4 million will be used to set up the financial mechanisms and 
strengthen the technical capacity of CABEI -accredited and n on-regulated financial 
institutions to access and channel funds through them; ( ii) USD 28 million will form the 
grant facility through which vulnerable communities can access on -grants channelled 
through financial institutions; (iii) USD 10 million will be  used to strengthen the 
technical capacity of local government, farmers and rural communities to implement 
EbA and other adaptation measures; ( iv) USD 6.3 million will be used to generate and 
share information on climate change adaptation and finance acros s the region, and 
mainstream it into local and national policies; ( v) USD 28.8 million will be used to 
implement large- scale demonstration interventions, including EbA, water - and energy-
efficient technologies in rural communities across the region. These interventions 
represent a list of specific interventions previously agreed between the AE and each of 
the seven countries in the funding proposal ; and  (vi) USD 3.8 million will be used for 
overall management and monitoring of programme activities with an a dditional USD 0.7 
million for contingency;  
(b) An EbA lending facility would be established and operationalized with a GCF concessional loan of USD 60 million and approximately USD 43 million in market rate 
co-finance from CABEI. The financing is intended for small - and large -scale EbA 
investments at the farm, enterprise and household  level. The GCF funds would be


---
*Page 135*
---

 
 GCF/B.30/02/Add.0 6 
Page 135 
 
 
channelled through i ntermediary finance institutions  (IFIs) with at leas t one located in 
each of the seven Dry Corridor countries. In turn, the IFIs would on -lend to p artner 
financial institutions (PFIs) in the relevant country who would then directly on- lend to 
end beneficiaries ; and 
(c) A GCF -funded guarantee facility intended t o provide an additional layer of security to 
financial institutions to promote EbA finance ( USD 30 million). The guarantee facility 
would be made available to de -risk CABEI’s co -finance as well as that from other 
providers of non -concessional finance. The main aim is to increase  the appetite of IFIs to 
take on further financing to channel to PFIs by guaranteeing the PFI EbA finance 
performance.  
1.2 Impact potential                                          Scale : Medium  
6. There is evidence that the Central American Dry Corridor and the a rid zones of the 
Dominican Republic are among the world ’s tropical regions  most vulnerable  to the impacts of 
climate change, with Honduras, Nicaragua, Guatemala, the Dominican Republic  and El S alvador 
recogni zed as being among the 16 most vulnerable countries in the world to extreme climate 
events for 1998 –2017. Currently, the Dry Corridor encompasses approximately 64 per cent  of 
the municipalities in Central America and is expected to extend to  around 85 per cent  by 2050. 
Climate pro jections f or the region indicate that, by the end of the century, temperatures will 
increase by 3 –3.5°C under RCP4.5 . These changes could be compounded by further changes in 
the El Niño –La Niña cycle, which could result in prolonged droughts as well as more frequent 
and intense extreme rainfall events across the region.  
7. The independent TAP recognizes that targeted and catchment -specific EbA 
interventions  could reduce  hydrological vulnerability associated with increased droughts and 
periods of aridity. However, the key is for these interventions to be part of a n EbA plan that is 
tailored to the relevant catchment . The impact potential of EbA interventions is dependent on 
the specific climate challenges of each  catchment and whether such interventions are part of a 
reinforcing suite of actions that , in suitable local combination s, best mitigate those impacts.  
8. The original funding proposal  submitted in B.28 did not contain actionable catchment -
specific EbA plans f or the seven catchments where the demonstration interventions and the 
EbA grant facility would be applied. In the amended funding proposal  for B.29, the AE clarified 
its intention to develop site -specific intervention plans for each of the seven catchment areas as 
a critical first phase to ensuring that activities to be funded contribute to a cohesive catchment -
specific plan and landscape approach. The AE proposed that part of the grant funding request 
would  first be used to develop the catchment -specific E bA plans. In support of this approach, the 
AE further explained that some work has already commenced in some of the catchments, 
including using existing information on regional climate projections and downscaling through 
hydrological models at the basin le vel for three catchments. Two draft sample site -specific 
intervention plans were developed – for Costa Rica and Honduras – and these were provided to 
the independent TAP to illustrate the approach the AE intends to take.  
9. In its previous  assessment s, the independent TAP expressed its preference for first 
completing the climate rationale at the catchment scale for each country’s target catchment, 
followed by the development of a bespoke EbA catchment plan. The independent TAP 
appreciated  that the amen ded funding proposal  built in a sequencing as part of the programme 
whereby the catchment plans would be completed first , prior to most  interventions  being 
funded. H owever, the independent TAP remain ed concerned because of  (i) the gaps in the data 
and appr oaches proposed to validate interventions, and (ii) the request for grant funding for 
pre-selected demonstration projects without a persuasive case that these interventions will 
generate catchment -wide adaptation benefits or that they are critical elements  of a catchment -
specific EbA plan.


---
*Page 136*
---

 
 GCF/B.30/02/Add.0 6 
Page 136 
 
 
10. The current funding proposal  provides some additional information in support of the 
climate needs of the region and clarifies where the AE believes that a lack of historical climate 
data has constrained its a bility to fi ll in analytical gaps in support of its climate assertions. The 
AE stated that it encountered limited availability of site -specific climate information needed to 
generate more accurate and informative analyses of climate risks and vulnerabilit ies for 
resil ient development. The independent TAP’s  clear preference is that the use of climate models 
must be validated against observed historical data sets from meteorological stations, wherever 
these are available, and not against computer -generated data sets. However, the independent 
TAP understand s that in some circumstances this preferred approach may be difficult to achieve 
due to the lack of historical data. The AE asserts that the availability of historical observed meteorological data and site -specific climate change projections in this case are very li mited. 
CABEI  observe s in the funding proposal  that there are only 24 meteorological stations within 
the vast territory of the Dry Corridor  and note s that “o nly for three of the countries, there were 
stations within the prioritized pilot sites and just one of them (Honduras) had complete 
observed rainfall and temperature data for a period of over 30 years with less than 15% of 
discontinuity .” 
11. The funding proposal  also re-states that the catchment -specific plans will be completed 
first as part of the programm e’s initial phase and ha s now ma de clear that no grant -funded 
interventions will be approved prior to the finalization of the relevant catchment plan . The AE 
further assert s that no intervention will be funded  until it has been through a validation process 
whereby a proposed intervention is confirmed to meet the needs of the specific region in line 
with the catchment plans. The validation of actual site-specific interventions is to take place 
during the initial  stages of implementation  and E bA interventions will be validated from a wide 
range of identified EbA interventions that are adjusted to the specific climate risks and socio -
environmental context of the catchment. According to the AE, “the validation of th ese indicative 
pre-selected interventions will be based on the development of the site- specific plans that will 
include both a scientific analysis of specific climate projections  through the hydrological models, 
as well as sound stakeholder consultations t hat will allow to identify the most appropriate 
interventions.” The funding proposal  also provides a list of specific criteria that will be applied 
for the selection and validation of these EbA interventions , including:  
(a) Relevance to site- specific climate risks;   
(b) Positive impact potential over climate risks;  
(c) Familiarity and acceptance by stakeholders ; 
(d) Positive impact potential over most vulnerable sectors of the community ;  
(e) Positive impact potential on gender balance;   
(f) Technical feasibility of the intervent ion; 
(g) Compliance with social and environmental safeguards ; 
(h) Potential co -benefits of the intervention;  
(i) Minimal level of risks and constraints for implementation ; 
(ii) Potential for self -sustainability ; and  
(iii) Potential for sustained monitoring capacity .  
12. The independent TAP note s that the  list of validation criteria appears to be a reasonable 
starting point, but the effectiveness of a validation process will depend on (i)  the quality of the 
catchment plans themselves and (ii)  the manner and rigo ur by which the se criteria will be 
applied. The funding proposal  does not provide a clear sense or example of how the ex -ante  
validation of interventions will occur. The independent TAP could endorse a phased programme 
whereby complete and data- driven catchment plans are first developed and subsequently EbA


---
*Page 137*
---

 
 GCF/B.30/02/Add.0 6 
Page 137 
 
 
interventions are selected, but only if these are subject to rigorous validation, through a known 
process, against those plans. It is also critical that the process would prevent funding of 
proposed interventions that are not supported by the needs of the catchment. The funding 
proposal  does not provide enough information as to the operation of the validation process  for 
the independent TAP to make a determination of adequacy . Developing a detailed process for 
validatio n that is fit -for-purpose is thus a necessary first step and is included as a condition for 
the independent TAP’s endorsement of the proposal.  
13. With respect to  the USD 28.4 million grant request for the “ pre-identified EbA 
demonstration interventions ”, the concerns of the independent TAP were  acute . Under this 
component of the B.29 funding proposal , grants would be provided to communities in 36 
municipalities across the selected catchments that the AE believes cannot receive funding 
through the loan or grant facilities.1  
14. Although the activities listed in the B.29 funding propos al may offer localized 
environmental benefits, they were not  selected based on  a clear nexus to catchment -specific 
EbA plan s, and no site -specific argument was  made for how they w ill specifically  reduce the 
vulnerability of communities to climate change impacts . The current B.30 version of the funding 
proposal  has been amended so that these interventions are now deemed to be solely 
“indicative ” of the type of large -scale demonstration projects that the AE proposes to fund 
through grants. According to the AE, “the selected interventions remain as indicative and are 
subject to validation during the inception phase and in consultation with the spec ific 
communities.” The independent TAP appreciates that the demonstration interventions listed in 
the funding proposal  are no longer formally considered pre -selected and certain. The 
independent TAP agrees  that any intervention should first be validated ag ainst a site -specific 
catchment plan that , for each catchment , explains its specific climate challenges;  how the 
challenges are affecting ecosystem service provision ; how this make s communities vulnerable ; 
how these vulnerabilities to hazards can be mapped  spatially ; and what interventions are 
needed to maintain or restore these specific ecosystem services, to reduce vulnerability . The 
validation of the demonstration interventions should confirm the expected adaptation impact, 
underpinned by a climate ratio nale for each catchment, and accompanied by a clear EbA plan 
for each catchment. Only by having a robust validation process can the independent TAP be 
certain of the truly “indicative ” nature of these interventions.   
15. EbA loan program me – The funding proposal  envisages that GCF funding would also 
support a lending m echanism for EbA as well as water - and resource -efficient technologies  in all 
seven countries . This would be accomplished by providing a blended financing mechanism 
composed of conce ssional finance from GCF in combination with market rate debt as co -
financing from CABEI . The blended rate is intended to create incentives for regional and local 
financial institutions to engage in EbA finance. IFIs in CABEI ’s established network w ould 
manage the EbA lending facility via direct on -lending to local  PFIs  such as cooperatives, small 
banks, non- government organizations , rural community banks, among others. PFIs in turn 
would on-lend to end beneficiaries. The goal is for i nvestment s to be targe ted across the entire 
Dry Corridor/Arid Zone regions of the seven countries  (not just the seven catchments receiving

grant support) towards community -level investment, rural micro, small and medium- sized 
enterprises  or other institutions that can foster Eb A, as well as water - and energy- efficient 
technologies, for end beneficiaries across different sectors. The loan program me is not limited 
to the catchment areas in the grant portion of the funding proposal  and does not rely on the 
grant -funded interventions for its success.  In response to a question from the independent TAP 
on the link between the catchment -specific grants and the wider loans, the AE noted that the 
deployment of the loan facility will follo w a phased approach starting first in a select group of 
five municipalities in the identified catchments and expand ing from there geographically to 
cover additional municipalities. According to the AE, this phased approach will also allow for 
more time to strengthen capacities of the participating financial institutions during the initial 
stages and to promote the financial mechanism with communities and producers. The 
additional municipalities for the loan expansion are to be selected based on proper vulnerability 
assessments and climate impact projections as well as the analysis of demand and the potential 
for replication with the participating financial institutions to ensure economies of scale in the 
learning process and the internal processes already de veloped in the initial municipalities.   
16. The phased nature of the loan program me is critical from an efficiency and impact 
standpoint. To that end, the independent TAP has been informed by the GCF Secretariat that the 
specific performance thresholds for the  loan facility descri bed in the funding proposal  will also 
be reflected as conditions for GCF disbursement to be set in the funded activity agreement 
(FAA) . The FAA will include a disbursement schedule with milestones to be met , hence ensuring 
that GCF pro ceeds are disbursed in accordance with pre- agreed terms. The independent TAP 
concur s with this approach and view s this as a condition to endorsement.  
17. The loan program me would operate alongside a GCF -funded guarantee facility intended 
to secure  on -lending  by IFIs of CABEI’s co- financing funds and mobiliz e additional lending from 
IFI resources , as well as additional resources from public and private investors , by mitigating 
against credit risks associated with commercial EbA f inance.  
18. Technical as sistance w ould be provided for PFIs to access the EbA lending facility. This 
is meant to ensure that capacities, especially for adequate monitoring, reporting and verification 
systems, are in place to manage the EbA finance and to determine whether would -be borrowers 
are eligib le for such loans . 
19. The independent TAP notes that the loan and guarantee component of the project seek s 
to re -orient existing financial sectors to funding activities that would lead to climate benefits 
and ultimately prove to be financially sustainable, contributing to impact potential . This 
typically requires a mix of initial concessionality, knowledge sha ring and improved institutional 
capacity. This funding proposal  aims to do that at scale and across seven countries. However, 
the independent TAP note s that the investment objectives of the loan facility are broad: (i) 
implementing integrated catchment man agement and restoring catchment s; (ii) improving 
hydrological flow and filtration of rainwater into groundwater reserves through forest and 
ecosystem restoration; and (iii) reducing demand for scarce water resources by implementing 
water -efficient technolo gies at the farm and household level. The funding proposal does not 
include investment eligibility criteria to ensure climate impacts  are achieved.  According to the 
AE these criteria will be defined by the Regional Project Steering Committee, and will be used by 
the project executive units, the national coordination units and regional lending committees 
when reviewing eligibility . However, the independent TAP considers that  the absence of th e 
criteria makes it difficult to assess the overall impacts of EbA intervention s. Scattered 
interventions might not add to the overall objectives of the lending operation in the catchment 
areas, particularly  regarding  objectives 1 and 2. The independent TAP will require as a 
condition to disbursement that t he investment eligibility criteria be finalized.  
20. In the amended funding proposal s presented for B.29 and B.30 , the AE has provided 
improved data on the market size and potential demand for  the loan and guarantee products. In 
the original independent TAP assessment  at B.28 , the independent TAP questioned market


---
*Page 139*
---

 
 GCF/B.30/02/Add.0 6 
Page 139 
 
 
study proxy data assumptions regarding the numbers of smallholder farming units, rural 
community projects and commercial farms that were  expected to access loans to promote EbA 
in their regions. A completely new section on the demand analysis was  added to the B.29  
funding proposal  with details provided to facilitate understand ing of  the absorption capacity at 
the country level and establis hing a comparison with the numbers of expected loans and 
financial entities through this programme. The new information provides a more convincing 
demonstration of  the potential uptake of the proposed products.  
21. Information from the Microfinance Network of Central America and the Caribbean  
(REDCAMIF ) was used to analyse  the supply capacity for EbA finance and the feasibility of the 
proposed lending facility. According to latest statistics from the REDCAMIF MIF  Index for June 
2020, the microfinance institutio ns that belong to the network  manage a total inclusive 
agricultural portfolio of USD 324 million across all seven  countries for 221,477 clients. This 
represents more than three  times the total size of the targeted lending facility’s volume of USD 
102.8 million (GCF plus CABEI co -financing). The microfinance sector’s involvement in inclusive 
agricultural finance is a useful proxy for its ability to serve EbA finance, which will primarily be 
targeted at small -scale farmers.  
22. Moreover, the amended funding propo sal has introduce d a clearer process for phasing 
disbursements against milestones,  although the details we re yet to be finalized at the time of 
this assessment, and no draft term sheet had been shared with the independent TAP. The 
amended funding proposal  also reduces the overall size of the loan facility  in response to a 
suggestion by the independent TAP to consider scaling down th e loan facility both in volume 
and possibly in geographic and intervention focus. The adjustment in size help s slightly to  
mitigate the risk of deployment failure, which could be damag ing to the perception of 
bankability of EbA solutions across the region , including among government partners, IFIs and 
PFIs.  This concern is relevant considering  the experience of comparative efforts such as 
Microfinance for Ecosystem- Based Adaptation (MEbA)  and the Productive Investment Initiative 
for Adaptation to Climate Ch ange ( CAMBio II) , where loan disbursement rates have been very 
slow – partly because of  the impact of the COVID -19 pandemic, but also possibly because of 
capacity constraints in partner institutions. In addition to these reasons, the independent TAP 
has a real concern that the slow uptake may also relate to limited absorptive capacity by the market. The Secretariat informed the independent TAP that the specific performance thresholds 
for the loan progr amme described by the AE in the amended funding proposal  will be reflected 
as conditions for GCF disbursement to be set in the FAA, which will include a disbursement 
schedule with milestones to be met to ensure that GCF proceeds are disbursed in accordance  
with pre -agreed terms.  
23. It is the view  of the independent TAP that,  with the phasing safeguards  not just  included  
in principle , but as a covenant in the  FAA and the relevant governing agreements for the 
programme , the loan and guarantee component of the funding proposal  could be useful in 
testing and educating the market for EbA demand. This is in fact a core role for GCF finance. Of 
course, the effectiveness of the phasing approach will hinge on the ability and willingness of the 
GCF to monitor disbursement rates and adjust loan amounts as necessary to adjust to actual 
capacity constraints of partner institutions and demand. For this reason, the independent TAP is 
endorsing the pr oject subject to the inclusion of a covenant determining this phased approach . 
The impact of the loan and guarantee component could thus be positive, but, as with the grant 
component, must be underpinned by a clear adaptation rationale, so that the theory of change 
for how precisely these lo ans will reduce vulnerability to climate change is clear. In the case of 
these loans, the sheer diversity of interventions under consideration and the region -wide 
application makes this more challenging . The independent TAP note s that,  unlike the grant 
com ponents which are focused on the target catchments , the loan program me is intended to 
eventually allocate funds to the entirety of the Dry Corridor region across the seven countries .


---
*Page 140*
